Characterizations of reflexive Banach spaces
Tianyi Zhou

Abstract
. In this paper we survey known results of characterizations of reflexive Banach spaces, which are based on convergence of usual and generalized arithmetic mean (or Cesàro sum), weakly compact subsets, affine sets in a Banach space or its dual and an unbounded bi-orthogonal system generalized from the one in a finite-dimensional Banach space. We also include results that describe precisely when a subspace is linearly isomorphic to ℓ 1 \ell^{1} or c 0 c_{0} in a Banach space that has a Schauder basis, which can imply non-reflexivity of a Banach space in general and is proven to be equivalent to non-reflexivity when the given Schauder basis is unconditional. After reflexivity, we will also study other geometric properties that are strictly stronger, implications among them and their characterizations.



Introduction

The characterization of a reflexive Banach space is a classic topic that can date back to around a hundred years ago. The basic ideas of such characterization includes fining the precise descriptions of the closed unit ball or an affine set, and the behaviors of a Schauder basis (if exists) or the dual space. Relevant results that follow ideas above will be included in this paper. Much of the progress that describes a reflexive Banach space by weakly compact sets was completed and summarized by R. James in
[
Jam64a
]
and
[
Jam64b
]
. Results that describe reflexivity by the dual space are mainly from R. James in
[
Jam72a
]
and P. Vlastimil in
[
Pta59
]
. Since it is clear that a reflexive Banach space contains no non-reflexive subspaces (see
[
DS88
]
), one can check if a Banach space is reflexive or not by checking the existence of a subspace that linearly isomorphic to a non-reflexive Banach space, such as
\ell^{1}
or
c_{0}
, and can refer to
[
Jam50
]
,
[
Ros74
]
and
[
Sin62
]
. In the case when a Schauder basis exists, reflexivity can be proven when such a basis has certain properties that are related to the existence of
\ell^{1}
and
c_{0}
(see
[
Jam50
]
and
[
Ros74
]
).

Results on characterizing reflexivity can naturally be applied to the study of a super-reflexive Banach space, which is a Banach space on which no reflexive Banach spaces can be finitely (with respect to dimension) representable. As a result, the study of properties between reflexivity and super-reflexivity and properties that are weaker or stronger then emerged. The result of such study can be summarized by the following diagram from
[
TK98
]
:

Figure 1.

where
C_{NJ}(X)
is the
von Neumann-Jordan constant
of
(X,\|\cdot\|)
, which is first introduced in
[
JVN35
]
and plays an important role in characterizing super-reflexive Banach spaces;
\tilde{C}_{NJ}(X)
is the infimum among all
C_{NJ}(X)
when
X
is equipped with a norm that is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|
; The definition of a
uniformly non-square
space can be found in
[
Jam72d
]
and the one of a
uniformly convex
can be found in
[
Kak39
]
.

In this paper, we mean to give an overview of known results that arise from or are related to definitions in the diagram above. However, as the study of reflexivity is rather complete, there will definitely be topics we cannot cover due to space limitations. One can refer to
[
Rid77
]
for more implications between reflexivity and stronger properties, such as
rotundity
and
smooth norm
, and, for instance,
[
OS98
]
, for another precise geometric description of reflexivity. Precise proofs of main results will be included. However, we do not mean to copy and paste the original proofs as several of them, from the author’s perspective, lack details or explanations between statements, and those proofs will be recreated based on the original ideas.

This paper is organized as follows. The
Section 1
discusses how arithmetic means (of vectors) in a Banach spaces can be related to reflexivity and a few other stronger properties that will appear in later sections.
Section 2
deals with the equivalence between non-reflexivity and a pointwise vanishing sequence of continuous linear functionals.
Section 3
is devoted to the characterizations of reflexivity by affine sets in a Banach space or in its dual. In
Section 4
we find the relation among reflexivity, the existence of
\ell^{1}
or
c_{0}
, and a Schauder basis with certain properties. In
Section 5
we generalized the bi-orthogonal system that always exists in a finite-dimensional normed linear space, to an infinitely-dimensional Banach space and show how such a system describes reflexivity.
Section 6
deals with the characterizations of reflexivity by weakly compact sets, and gives an summary that includes previous characterizations. The last section gives an overview of properties that are stronger than reflexivity and their characterizations.

1.
Arithmetic means in Banach spaces

In this section, we start with the definition of uniform convexity, and will later see how it is stronger than all other geometric properties we will describe. We will discuss the relation between reflexivity, uniform convexity, the convergence of Cesàro sums (the usual arithmetic mean) of a bounded sequence and the convergence of a generalized arithmetic means of a bounded sequence, which is defined by an
\mathbb{N}
-by-
\mathbb{N}
matrix, each of whose rows up to
1
.

Definition 1.1
.

A normed linear space is
uniformly convex
if given any two vectors
x,y
in the closed unit ball of the space, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
there exists a
\delta\in(0,1)
such that:

\|x-y\|>\epsilon\hskip 8.5359pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt1<\|x+y\|<2-\delta

Definition 1.2
.

Given a normed linear space
X
and a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X
, a
summability method

T
is a real matrix
\big[c_{m,n}\big]_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\{c_{m,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is summable for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The
T
-
means
of the sequence is another sequence
\{t_{m}\}
where each
t_{m}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{n\leq N}c_{m,n}x_{n}
. We say
T
is
regular
if
x_{n}\rightarrow x
implies
t_{m}\rightarrow x
.

Theorem 1.3

(
[
Sil10
, Silverman-Toeplitz Theorem]
)
.

In the set-up of
Definition
1.2
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

T
is regular.

(2)

\sup_{m}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\big|\,c_{m,n}\,\big|<\infty
.

(3)

\lim_{m}c_{m,n}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(4)

\lim_{m}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{m,n}=1
.

Definition 1.4
.

In the set-up of
Definition
1.2
, for a regular
T=\big[c_{m,n}\big]_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we call
T
essentially positive if:

\lim_{m}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\big|\,c_{m,n}\,\big|=1

Definition 1.5
.

Given a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in a Banach space
X
, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define:

s_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}x_{i}

Then
X
is said to have
Banach-Saks property
if whenever
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is weakly convergent,
(s_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in norm to the same limit.

1.1.
Uniformly convexity
\,\implies\,
Banach-Saks property

Proposition 1.6

(
[
Kak39
]
)
.

A normed linear space
X
is
unifomrly convex
if and only if for any two vectors
x,y\in X
=, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
there exists
\delta\in(0,1)
:

(1)

\|x-y\|>\epsilon\max\big(\|x\|,\|y\|\big)\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{1}{2}\|x+y\|\leq(1-\delta)\max\big(\|x\|,\|y\|\big)

Proof.

By replacing
x,y
in (
1
) by
\dfrac{x}{\max(\|x\|,\|y\|)}
and
\dfrac{y}{\max(\|x\|,\|y\|)}
, the conclusion follows immediately by
Definition
1.1
.

∎

Theorem 1.7

(
[
Kak39
]
)
.

A uniformly convex Banach space has the
Banach-Saks Property
.

Proof.

Suppose
\|x_{n}\|\leq M\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}
and hence we can assume the sequence is in the closed unit ball. By definition, for any two elements
x,y
in the closed unit ball, if
\|x-y\|>\dfrac{1}{2}
then we can find
\delta_{M}
such that
\dfrac{1}{2}\|x+y\|\leq 1-\delta_{M}
. Then we need to find a subsequence
\{x_{k_{i}}\}\subseteq\{x_{i}\}
such that
\dfrac{1}{2}\|x_{k_{i}}+x_{k_{i+1}}\|\leq\theta\,\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N}
where
\theta=\max\left(\dfrac{3}{4},1-\delta_{M}\right)<1
.

Case a)

Fix
k_{1}=2
. If
\|x_{2}\|\leq\dfrac{1}{2}
then set
k_{2}=3
and then we have
\dfrac{1}{2}\|x_{k_{1}}+x_{k_{2}}\|\leq\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{1}{2}+1\right)\leq\theta

Case b)

Fix
k_{1}=2
. If
\|x_{2}\|>\dfrac{1}{2}
. Suppose for all other integers
m,\|x_{2}-x_{m}\|\leq\dfrac{1}{2}
. Pick
f_{n}\in X^{*}
such that
f_{n}(x_{2})>\|x_{2}\|-\dfrac{1}{n}
. WLOG we can assume
x_{n}
converges to zero weakly so that:

\sup_{i}\|x_{2}-x_{i}\|\geq\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(x_{2}-x_{i})=f_{n}(x_{2})>\|x_{2}\|-\frac{1}{n}\,\implies\,\|x_{2}\|\leq\sup_{i}\|x_{2}-x_{i}\|\leq\frac{1}{2}

which is absurd.

Hence when
\|x_{2}\|>\dfrac{1}{2}
we can find
k_{2}
such that
\|x_{k_{1}}-x_{k_{2}}\|>\dfrac{1}{2}
. Therefore,
\dfrac{1}{2}\|x_{k_{1}}+x_{k_{2}}\|\leq 1-\delta_{0}\leq\theta
.

Now after
k_{2}
is determined, set
k_{3}=k_{2}+1
and set
k_{4}
using the method above. Then set
k_{5}=k_{4}+1
and so on. After we have
\{x_{k_{i}}\}
define
y_{i}^{(1)}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(x_{k_{2i-1}}+x_{k_{2i}}\right)
. Then the sequence
\left\{y_{i}^{(1)}\right\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
also converges to zero and bounded by
\theta
in norm. In the new sequence
\left\{y_{i}^{(1)}\right\}
find the subsequence
\left\{y_{k_{i}}^{(1)}\right\}
using the same method and then define
y_{i}^{(2)}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(y_{k_{2i-1}}^{(1)}+y_{k_{2i}}^{(1)}\right)
and obtain the new sequence
\left\{y_{i}^{(2)}\right\}
. Notice this new sequence will be bounded by
\theta^{2}
. Therefore, by induction for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we will have a subsequence
\left\{y_{i}^{(k)}\right\}
of
\{x_{i}\}
such that
\left\|y_{i}^{(k)}\right\|\leq\theta^{k}
and
y_{i}^{(k)}
converges to zero weakly.

Fix an integer
i
and then consider
\left\{y_{i}^{(n)}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. For
n>0,y_{i}^{(n)}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(y_{k_{2i-1}}^{(n-1)}+y_{k_{2i}}^{(n-1)}\right)
. Also each
y_{k_{2i-1}}^{(n-1)}
and
y_{k_{2i}}^{(n-1)}
is the mean of another two elements from
\left\{y_{i}^{(n-2)}\right\}
. For a fixed
p\in\mathbb{N}
we can find a finite subset
F_{i}^{(p)}=\left\{l_{1,i}^{(p)},l_{2,i}^{(p)},\cdots,l_{2^{p},i}^{(p)}\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
(ordered strictly increasing) such that
y_{i}^{(p)}=\dfrac{1}{2^{p}}\left(x_{l_{1,i}^{(p)}}+x_{l_{2,i}^{(p)}}+\,\cdots\,+x_{l_{2^{p},i}^{(p)}}\right)
. Now define:

\displaystyle n_{1}=1,\hskip 8.5359ptn_{2}=l_{1,1}^{(1)},\hskip 8.5359ptn_{3}=l_{2,1}^{(1)},\hskip 8.5359ptn_{4}=l_{1,1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle n_{5}=l_{2,1}^{(2)},\hskip 8.5359ptn_{6}=l_{3,1}^{(2)},\hskip 8.5359ptn_{7}=l_{4,1}^{(2)},\hskip 8.5359ptn_{8}=l_{1,1}^{(3)}

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle n_{2^{p}-1}=l_{2^{p},1}^{(p-1)},\hskip 8.5359ptn_{2^{p}}=l_{1,1}^{(p)},\cdots

For the new subsequence
\{x_{n_{i}}\}
we want to show that:

\left\|\frac{1}{k}(x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{k}})\right\|\overset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\rightarrow}0

and it suffices to show that:

\left\|\frac{1}{2^{m}-1}\left(x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{2^{m}-1}}\right)\right\|\overset{m\longrightarrow\infty}{\rightarrow}0

Fix
q,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
q<m
. Define
k=2^{m}-1
.

\displaystyle\|x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{k}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{2^{q}-1}}\right\|

\displaystyle+\sum_{2\leq j\leq 2}\left\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{(n_{j2^{q}})-1}\right\|

\displaystyle+\sum_{3\leq j\leq 4}\left\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{(n_{j2^{q}})-1}\right\|

\displaystyle+\sum_{5\leq j\leq 8}\left\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{(n_{j2^{q}})-1}\right\|

\displaystyle+\cdots

\displaystyle+\sum_{2^{m-1}+1\leq j\leq 2^{m}}\left\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{k}}\right\|

Recall that for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|x_{i}\|\leq 1
. Then when
j=2
we have:

j=2\,\implies\,x_{n_{(j-1)2^{q}}}+x_{n_{(j-1)2^{q}+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{j2^{q}-1}}=x_{l_{1}^{(q)}}+x_{l_{2}^{(q)}}+\,\cdots\,+x_{l_{2^{q}-1}^{(q)}}=2^{q}y_{1}^{(q)}

and similarly when
j=3
or
4
:

\displaystyle j=3\,\implies\,x_{n_{(j-1)2^{q}}}+x_{n_{(j-1)2^{q}+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{j2^{q}-1}}=x_{n_{2^{q+1}}}+x_{n_{2^{q+1}+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{3\,\cdot\,2^{q}-1}}=

\displaystyle x_{l_{1,1}^{(q+1)}}+x_{l_{2,1}^{(q+1)}}\,\cdots\,x_{l_{2^{q},1}^{(q+1)}}

\displaystyle j=4\,\implies\,x_{n_{(j-1)2^{q}}}+x_{n_{(j-1)2^{q}+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{j2^{q}-1}}=x_{n_{3\,\cdot\,2^{q}}}+x_{n_{3\,\cdot\,2^{q}+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{2^{q+2}-1}}=

\displaystyle x_{l_{2^{q}+1,1}^{(q+1)}}+x_{l_{2^{q}+2,1}^{(q+1)}}\,\cdots\,x_{l_{2^{q+1},1}^{(q+1)}}

Therefore
y_{1}^{(q+1)}
can be rewritten as the following form:

y_{1}^{(q+1)}=\frac{1}{2^{q+1}}\left[x_{l_{1,1}^{(q+1)}}+x_{l_{2,1}^{(q+1)}}\,\cdots\,x_{l_{2^{q},1}^{(q+1)}}+x_{l_{2^{q}+1,1}^{(q+1)}}+x_{l_{2^{q}+2,1}^{(q+1)}}\,\cdots\,x_{l_{2^{q+1},1}^{(q+1)}}\right]

Meanwhile, we know that:

y_{1}^{(q+1)}=\frac{1}{2}\left(y_{k_{1}}^{(q)}+y_{k_{2}}^{(q)}\right)=\frac{1}{2^{q+1}}\left[\sum_{j\in F_{k_{1}}^{(q)}}x_{j}+\sum_{i\in F_{k_{2}}^{(q)}}x_{i}\right]

Since
F_{k_{1}}^{(q)}
is unique to
y_{k_{1}}^{(q)}
,
F_{k_{2}}^{(q)}
is unique to
y_{k_{2}}^{(q)}
,
F_{k_{1}}^{(q)}
is disjoint from
F_{k_{2}}^{(q)}
and the max number in
F_{k_{1}}^{(q)}
will be smaller than the min number in
F_{k_{2}}^{(q)}
, then we will have
F_{k_{1}}^{(q)}\cup F_{k_{2}}^{(q)}=\left\{l_{1,1}^{(q+1)},l_{2,1}^{(q+1)},\cdots\,l_{2^{q+1},1}^{(q+1)}\right\}
. Then:

\sum_{3\leq j\leq 4}\left\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{(n_{j2^{q}})-1}\right\|\leq 2^{q+1}\theta^{q+1}

When
5\leq j\leq 8
:

\displaystyle y_{1}^{(q+2)}=\frac{1}{2^{q+2}}\left[x_{l_{1,1}^{(q+2)}}+x_{l_{2,1}^{(q+2)}}+\,\cdots\,+x_{l_{2^{q+2},1}^{(q+2)}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\left[y_{k_{1}}^{(q+1)}+y_{k_{2}}^{(q+1)}\right]

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|x_{l_{1,1}^{(q+2)}}+x_{l_{2,1}^{(q+2)}}+\,\cdots\,+x_{l_{2^{q+2},1}^{(q+2)}}\right\|\leq 2^{q+2}\theta^{q+2}

By induction we have::

\displaystyle\|x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{k}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{2^{m}-1}}\|+

\displaystyle\sum_{2\leq j\leq 2}\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{(n_{j2^{q}})-1}\|+

\displaystyle\sum_{3\leq j\leq 4}\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{(n_{j2^{q}})-1}\|+

\displaystyle\sum_{5\leq j\leq 8}\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{(n_{j2^{q}})-1}\|+

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\sum_{2^{m-1}+1\leq j\leq 2^{m}}\|x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)}}+x_{n_{2^{q}(j-1)+1}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{k}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q\leq i\leq m-1}2^{i}\theta^{i}

\displaystyle\implies\,\frac{1}{k}\|x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{k}}\|=\frac{1}{2^{m}-1}\|x_{n_{1}}+x_{n_{2}}+\,\cdots\,x_{n_{2^{m}-1}}\|

\displaystyle<\frac{2^{q}}{2^{m}-1}+\sum_{q\leq i\leq m-1}\theta^{i}=\frac{2^{q}}{2^{m}-1}+\theta^{q}\frac{1-\theta^{m-q}}{1-\theta}

Now for any
\epsilon>0
, find
q_{0},m\in\mathbb{N}
with
(q_{0}<m)
such that
\dfrac{\theta^{q_{0}}(1-\theta^{m-q_{0}})}{1-\theta}<\epsilon
. Then find
m_{0}>m
such that
\dfrac{2^{q_{0}}}{2^{m_{0}}-1}<\epsilon
. Hence now we have:

\frac{1}{2^{m_{0}}-1}\left\|\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{m_{0}}-1}x_{n_{i}}\right\|<2\epsilon

and then we can conclude that:

\lim_{k}\left\|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}x_{n_{i}}\right\|=0

∎

1.2.
Banach-Saks property
\,\Longrightarrow\,
reflexivity

Definition 1.8
.

A Banach space is said to have the
property

\mathcal{P}(w\mathcal{P}\,resp.)
if for every bounded sequence
\{x_{n}\}
there is a regular summability method
T
such that there exists a subsequence of
\{x_{n}\}
whose
T
-means converge strongly (weakly
resp.
).

Theorem 1.9

(
[
NW63
]
)
.

In a Banach space, given a infinite real matrix
T=[c_{m,n}]_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that each line of sequence is summable, the following statements are equivalent:

a)

B
is reflexive

b)

B
has property
\mathcal{P}
and, for each bounded sequence
\{x_{n}\}
, the regular summability method
T
that is given by the property
\mathcal{P}
and associated with
\{x_{n}\}
is essentially positive.

c)

B
has property
w\mathcal{P}
and, for each bounded sequence
\{x_{n}\}
, the regular summability method
T
that is given by the property
\mathcal{P}
and associated with
\{x_{n}\}
is essentially positive.

Proof.

1)

a)\implies b)
: WLOG assume the bounded sequence
\{x_{n}\}
is inside the closed unit ball. By reflexivity there exists
x
with
\|x\|\leq 1
such that
x
is a clustered point of
\{x_{n}\}
and hence also a clustered point of
\operatorname{conv}\{x_{n}\}
. Therefore, there exists a sequence
\{s_{m}\}\subseteq\operatorname{conv}\{x_{n}\}
such that
\lim_{m}\|s_{m}-x\|=0
. For each
s_{m}
let
s_{m}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq a_{m}}c_{m,j}x_{j}
where each
c_{m,j}\in(0,1)
and
\sum_{1\leq j\leq a_{m}}c_{m,j}=1
. For each
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
, define:

d_{m,k}=\begin{cases}c_{m,j},\hskip 28.45274ptk\in\big\{m+j-1\,|\,j\in\{1,\cdots,a_{m}\}\big\}\\
0,\hskip 40.97203pt\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

Then define a matrix
T=[d_{m,k}]_{m,k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Since each column of
T
only has one non-zero value, the second condition in
Definition
1.2
is satisfied and obviously for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}d_{m,k}=1
, which implies that
T
is essentially positive.

2)

b)\implies c)
: Immediate.

3)

c)\implies a)
: Suppose
T
is the given regular and essentially positive matrix and the
T
-means
\{t_{m}\}
converges to
x
weakly. WLOG we assume
x\neq 0
. By
Hahn-Banach Theorem
because there exists
g_{x}\in X^{*}
such that
g_{x}(x)=\|x\|
we must have
\liminf\|x_{n}\|>0
. Otherwise, we can find
\delta>0
such that because
\inf_{n\geq k}\|x_{n}\|<\delta
, we have:

2\delta<\|x\|=g_{x}(x)\leq\inf_{n\geq k}g_{x}(x_{n})+\delta\leq 2\delta

which leas to a contradiction.

Because
\sup_{m}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|\,c_{m,n}\,|<\infty
, for each
\epsilon>0
we can find
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sup_{m}\sum_{n>N_{1}}|\,c_{m,n}\,|<\epsilon
. Meanwhile, because
\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},c_{m,n}\overset{m\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
, we can find
N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|\sum_{n\leq N_{1}}c_{m,n}x_{n}\|<\epsilon
for all
n\geq N_{2}
(for
\{x_{n}\}
being bounded). Now pick
f\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}
such that
\|x\|\leq f(x)+\epsilon
. Then find
L\in\mathbb{N}
bigger than
N_{2}
such that:

\displaystyle\|x\|\leq f(x)+\epsilon\leq f(t_{L})+2\epsilon=f\left[\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right]+2\epsilon

\displaystyle=f\left[\sum_{n\leq N_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right]+f\left[\sum_{n>N_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right]+2\epsilon\leq f\left[\sum_{n>N_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right]+3\epsilon

Next we claim that the following statement is false:

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}\,\exists\,n_{1}\geq n\hskip 5.69046pt\text{such that}\hskip 5.69046pt\exists\,k\geq n_{1}\,\forall\,j\in\{n_{1},n_{1}+1,n_{1}+2,\cdots,k\},\hskip 14.22636ptf\left(\sum_{n_{1}\leq i\leq k}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)>\|x_{j}\|

Assume by contradiction that it is true. Find
n_{1}\geq N_{1},k_{1}\geq n_{1}
such that:

f\left(\sum_{n_{1}\leq i\leq k_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)>\|x_{n_{1}}\|+\epsilon

Then find
n_{2}\geq k_{1}+1,k_{2}\geq n_{2}
such that:

f\left(\sum_{n_{2}\leq i\leq k_{2}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)>\|x_{n_{1}}\|+\epsilon

B
ecause
\liminf\|x_{n}\|>0
we can assume
\inf_{n\geq K}\|x_{n}\|=\delta>0
and by repeating the steps above for infinitely many times we will have:

f\left(\sum_{i\geq n_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)>\sum_{i\geq 1}\|x_{n_{i}}\|>\sum_{i\geq K}\inf_{n\geq K}\|x_{n}\|=\infty

which is absurd. Hence we have the following statement:

(2)

\exists\,n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\,\forall\,n\geq n_{1}\hskip 5.69046pt\text{such that}\hskip 5.69046pt\forall\,k\geq n\,\exists\,j\in\{n,n+1,n+2,\cdots,k\},\hskip 5.69046ptf\left(\sum_{n_{1}\leq i\leq k}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)\leq\|x_{j}\|

Now since
\sum_{n<N_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}
converges we can find
M_{1}
such that
\|\sum_{n>M_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\|<\epsilon
. WLOG we can assume
N_{1}\geq n_{1}
where
n_{1}
is given in (
2
) (since (
2
) is independent to the choice of
L
). Now find
j_{1}\in\{N_{1},N_{1}+1,\cdots,M_{1}\}
such that:

f\left(\sum_{N_{1}\leq i\leq M_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)\leq\|x_{j}\|

and hence:

(3)

f\left(\sum_{n>N_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)+3\epsilon\leq\|x_{j_{1}}\|+f\left(\sum_{n>M_{1}}c_{L,n}x_{n}\right)+3\epsilon\leq\|x_{j_{1}}\|+4\epsilon

Since
\epsilon>0
is arbitrarily small, for a smaller
\epsilon
we can find another
N_{1}
and the corresponding
j_{1}
such that (
3
) holds. Hence there is a subsequence
\{x_{j_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\|x\|\leq\limsup_{k}\|x_{j_{k}}\|\leq\limsup_{i}\|x_{i}\|
and hence we have
f(x)\leq\limsup_{n}f(x_{n})\,\forall\,f\in X^{*}

Again fix
\epsilon>0.f\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}
and find large
m_{1}
such that
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{m_{1},n}\in(1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)
and
f(t_{m_{1}})\in(f(x)-\epsilon,f(x)+\epsilon)
. Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and find
m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|\sum_{n\leq N}c_{m_{2},n}x_{n}\|<\epsilon
(because
\lim_{m}c_{m,n}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{x_{n}\}
bounded) and then we have:

f\left(\sum_{n>N}c_{m_{2},n}x_{n}\right)\in\big(f(x)-2\epsilon,f(x)+2\epsilon\big)

Therefore for
m>\max(m_{1},m_{2})
we have:

f(x)+2\epsilon>f\left(\sum_{n>N}c_{m,n}x_{n}\right)\geq\inf_{n>N}f(x_{n})\sum_{n>N}c_{m,n}>\inf_{n>N}f(x_{n})(1-\epsilon)

When
\epsilon
is getting smaller, we need a bigger
N
such that
\|\sum_{n\leq N}c_{m,n}x_{n}\|<\epsilon
. Hence we can conclude
f(x)\geq\liminf_{n}f(x_{n})
. Therefore, we have
\liminf_{n}f(x_{n})\leq f(x)\leq\limsup_{n}f(x_{n})
for all
f\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}
. We can now conclude the closed unit ball in
X
is weakly compact and hence
X
is reflexive.

∎

Corollary 1.10

(
[
NW63
]
)
.

A Banach space that possesses Banach-Saks Property is reflexive.

Proof.

Define
T=[t_{i,j}]_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
where
t_{i,j}=\dfrac{1}{i}
when
i\leq j
and zero otherwise. Then
T
is a regular summable and essentially positive matrix and each weakly convergent (therefore bounded) sequence will have its
T
-means converging in norm. By
Theorem
1.9
we have the space is reflexive.
∎

Remark 1.11
.

A counter-example to the converse of
Corollary
1.10
can be found in
[
Bae72
]
.

Definition 1.12
.

In a Banach space
X
with a Schauder basis
\{x_{n}\}
, we call
\{x_{n}\}

boundedly complete
if
\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}\|<M\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}
for some fixed
M\in\mathbb{N}
implies
\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}x_{i}
converges.

Theorem 1.13

(
[
NW63
]
)
.

A Schauder basis in a Banach space with property
w\mathcal{P}
will be boundedly complete.

Proof.

Let
\{x_{n}\}
be the Schauder basis of
X
and
T=[c_{m,n}]_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a regular summarble method. Suppose
\{v_{n}\}_{n}
is a uniformly bounded by
M\in\mathbb{N}
and that
v_{n}=\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then by
Theorem 1.9
, the
T
-means of a subsequence
\{v_{n}\}
, say
\{t_{m}\}
, converges to some
x
in norm. Assume:

x=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}b_{i}x_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt

and, by restricting to the proper subsequence given by
Theorem
1.9
, we can write each
t_{m}
in the following form:

\displaystyle t_{m}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{m,n}v_{n}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{m,n}\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}

\displaystyle\implies\,t_{m}=\lim_{n}\sum_{k\leq n}c_{m,k}\sum_{i\leq k}a_{i}x_{i}=\lim_{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\left[a_{i}\big(\sum_{i\leq k\leq n}c_{m,k}\big)\right]x_{i}

Meanwhile, since the sequence
\Big\{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{m,n}\Big\}_{m}
is bounded and converges to
1
, then for each
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\left[a_{i}\left(\sum_{i\leq k\leq n}c_{m,k}\right)\right]x_{i}-\sum_{i\leq n}\left[a_{i}\left(\sum_{k\geq i}c_{m,k}\right)\right]x_{i}\right\|=\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\left[a_{i}\left(\sum_{k>n}c_{m,k}\right)\right]x_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left|\,\sum_{k>n}c_{m,k}\,\right|\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq M\left|\,\sum_{k>n}c_{m,k}\,\right|\overset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

\displaystyle\implies\,t_{m}=\lim_{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\left[a_{i}\left(\sum_{i\leq k\leq n}c_{m,k}\right)\right]x_{i}=\lim_{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\left[a_{i}\left(\sum_{k\geq i}c_{m,k}\right)\right]x_{i}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\left[a_{i}\left(\sum_{k\geq i}c_{m,k}\right)\right]x_{i}\overset{m\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}x

\displaystyle\implies\,\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pta_{i}\lim_{m}\sum_{k\geq i}c_{m,k}=b_{i}

By
Definition
1.2
, we have that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lim_{m}c_{m,n}=0
. Therefore, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{m}\sum_{k<i}c_{m,k}=\sum_{k<i}\lim_{m}c_{m,k}=0

\displaystyle\implies\,a_{i}\lim_{m}\sum_{k\geq i}c_{m,k}=a_{i}\lim_{m}\sum_{k\geq i}c_{m,k}+\lim_{m}a_{i}\sum_{k<i}c_{m,k}=a_{i}\lim_{m}\sum_{k\geq 1}c_{m,k}=b_{i}

\displaystyle\implies\,a_{i}=b_{i}

Hence
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a_{i}x_{i}
converges.

∎

2.
Characterizations by dual space

In this section, we will discover the equivalence between non-reflexivity of a Banach space and behaviors of bounded linear functionals and all main results are from
[
Jam72a
]
. As an immediate consequence of
[
Gol38
, Goldstine’s Theorem]
(or one can refer to
[
DS88
, Theorem
V.4.7
]
), a Banach space is reflexive if and only if its closed unit ball is weakly compact, if and only if every bounded linear functional attains supremum on its closed unit ball. Besides, in a non-reflexive Banach space, we can see how much the weak topology differs from the norm topology in the dual space by showing there exists a weakly vanishing sequence of bounded linear functionals, whose convex hull’s closure is positively distant away from zero.

2.1.
Main Theorems

Theorem 2.1
.

Given
B
a separable Banach space, the following statements are equivalent:

a)

B
is not reflexive.

b)

If
\theta\in(0,1)
then there is a sequence
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq B^{*}_{\leq 1}
such that
\|f\|\geq\theta
for all
f\in\operatorname{conv}\{f_{n}\}
and
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to zero pointwise.

c)

If
\theta\in(0,1)
and
\{\lambda_{n}\}\subseteq(0,1)
such that
\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda_{n}=1
then there is
\alpha\in[\theta,1]
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq B^{*}_{\leq 1}
converging pointwise to zero where
\|\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda_{n}g_{n}\|=\alpha
and for each
n
:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|\leq\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right).

d)

There is a
f\in B^{*}
such that
f
cannot obtain its maximum on the closed unit ball of
B
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Theorem
2.1
will also be true when
B
is not separable.

Theorem 2.3
.

If
X
is a separable bounded weakly closed subset of a complete LCTVS, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is not weakly compact.

(2)

There is a
\theta>0
and
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq B^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
such that
\|f\|\geq\theta
for all
f\in\operatorname{conv}\{f_{n}\}
and
\{f_{n}\}
converge to zero pointwise on
X
.

(3)

There exists
\theta>0
such that, if
\{\lambda_{n}\}\subseteq(0,1)
is a sequence with
\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda_{n}=1
, then there is
\alpha\geq\theta
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq B^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
converging to zero pointwise on
X
such that:

\left\|\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda_{n}g_{n}\right\|=\alpha

and, for each
x\in X
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}(x)\right\|<\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda\right).

(4)

There exists
f\in X^{*}
such that
f
cannot attain
\|f\|
on the closed unit ball of
X
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Theorem
2.3
will also be true when
X
is not separable.

2.2.
Important Lemmas

Lemma 2.5
.

Given a Banach space
B,\theta\in(0,1)
and
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq B^{*}_{\leq 1}
with
\|f\|\geq\theta
for all
f\in\operatorname{conv}\{f_{n}\}
, if
\{\lambda_{n}\}\subseteq(0,1)
satisfy
\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda_{n}=1
then we can find
\alpha\in[\theta,1]
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq B^{*}
where, for each
k\in\mathbb{N},g_{k}\in V_{k}(\{f_{n}\})=\operatorname{conv}\{f_{i}\}_{i\geq k}
such that:

\left\|\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}g_{k}\right\|=\alpha

and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|<\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda\right)

Proof.

For each
\lambda_{k}
find
\epsilon_{k}\in(0,1)
such that:

\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\epsilon_{k}}{\lambda_{k+1}}<1-\theta

so that:

(4)

\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\lambda_{k}\epsilon_{k}}{\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq k}\lambda_{i}}<\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\lambda_{k}\epsilon_{k}}{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{k+1}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\epsilon_{k}}{\lambda_{k+1}}<1-\theta

Define:

\alpha_{n}=\inf\left\{\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g\right\|\,|\,g\in V_{n}(\{f_{i}\})\right\}

and then find
g_{n}\in V_{n}(\{f_{i}\})
such that:

\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}\right\|<\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})

Since
V_{n+1}(\{f_{i}\})\subseteq V_{n}(\{f_{i}\})
, for each
g\in V_{n+1}(\{f_{i}\})
, we have:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\lambda_{n}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}g_{n}+\dfrac{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}g\in V_{n}

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\{\lambda_{n}g_{n}+\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}g:g\in V_{n+1}(\{f_{i}\})\right\}\subseteq\left\{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}g:g\in V_{n}(\{f_{i}\})\right\}

Hence we have
\alpha_{n}
is non-decreasing. Since
\{f_{i}\}\subseteq B^{*}_{\leq 1}
we have
\alpha_{n}\leq 1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and hence
\alpha_{n}\uparrow\alpha=\sup_{n}\alpha_{n}
exists such that
\alpha=\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\|
(because
\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\overset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rightarrow}0
). Therefore for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by (
4
):

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\left(\frac{\lambda_{n}+\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\right)\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i})g_{n}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|+\frac{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\frac{\lambda_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|\right]

\displaystyle\implies\,\dfrac{1}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|<\dfrac{\lambda_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon)}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}+\dfrac{1}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|

By induction we will have:

(5)

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\frac{\lambda_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|\right]

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\frac{\lambda_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{\lambda_{n-1}\alpha_{n-1}(1+\epsilon_{n-1})}{\sum_{i>n-1}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n-1}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{i\geq n-1}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n-1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|\right]

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{2\leq k\leq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}(1+\epsilon_{k})}{\sum_{j\geq k}\lambda_{j}\sum_{j>k}\lambda_{j}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{j\geq 2}\lambda_{j}}\|\lambda_{1}g_{1}\|\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}(1+\epsilon_{k})}{\sum_{j\geq k}\lambda_{j}\sum_{j>k}\lambda_{j}}\right]

Since
\alpha=\sup_{n}\alpha_{n}
, by (
5
) we have:

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|<\alpha\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\dfrac{\lambda_{k}(1+\epsilon_{k})}{\sum_{j\geq k}\lambda_{j}\sum_{j>k}\lambda_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\dfrac{\lambda_{k}}{\sum_{j\geq k}\lambda_{j}\sum_{j>k}\lambda_{j}}+\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\dfrac{\lambda_{k}\epsilon_{k}}{\sum_{j\geq k}\lambda_{j}\sum_{j>k}\lambda_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle<\alpha\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\left(\frac{1}{\sum_{j>k}\lambda_{j}}-\frac{1}{\sum_{j\geq k}\lambda_{j}}\right)+(1-\theta)\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\dfrac{1}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}-1+1-\theta\right]=\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)

∎

Definition 2.6
.

Given a normed linear space
X
and
\{\phi_{n}\}_{n}\subseteq X^{*}_{\leq 1}
we define:

L(\{\phi_{n}\}_{n})=\{\omega\in X^{*}\,|\,\,\forall\,x\in X,\liminf_{n}\phi_{n}(x)\leq\omega(x)\leq\limsup_{n}\phi_{n}(x)\}

Lemma 2.7
.

Given a Banach space
B,\theta\in(0,1),\{f_{n}\}_{n}\subseteq B^{*}_{\leq 1}
such that:

\forall\,f\in\operatorname{conv}\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\,\forall\,\omega\in L\big(\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\big),\hskip 8.5359pt\|f-\omega\|\geq\theta

If
\{\lambda_{i}\}\subseteq(0,\infty)
satisfies
\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}=1
, then we can find
\alpha\in[\theta,2]
and
\{g_{i}\}\subseteq B^{*}_{\leq 1}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega\in L(\{\phi_{n}\})
:

\left\|\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}(g_{k}-\omega)\right\|=\alpha,\hskip 28.45274pt\left\|\sum_{k\leq n}\lambda_{k}(g_{k}-\omega)\right\|<\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda\right)

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
Lemma
2.5
, find
\{\epsilon_{k}\}_{k}\subseteq(0,1)
such that:

\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\lambda_{k}\epsilon_{k}}{\sum_{j>k}\lambda_{j}\sum_{j\geq k}\lambda_{j}}<1-\theta

For each
n
and each sequence of bounded linear functionals
\{\phi_{i}\}\subseteq X^{\ast}
, define
V_{n}(\{\phi_{i}\})=\operatorname{conv}\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\geq n}
and define
V(\{\phi_{i}\}_{i})
be the set of all sequences of linear functionals
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j}
such that for each
j,\psi_{j}\in V_{j}(\{\phi_{i}\}_{i})
. In this case for every
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j}\in V(\{\phi_{i}\}_{i})
we will have
V(\{\psi_{j}\}_{j})\subseteq V(\{\phi_{i}\})
. To complete the proof, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we will find a real number
\alpha_{n}
, a bounded linear functional
g_{n}\in X^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
and sequences of bounded linear functionals
\{\phi_{i}^{n}\},\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}\subseteq X^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
.

First let
\{\psi_{j}^{0}\}_{j}=\{f_{j}\}_{j}
, the sequence given by the lemma. Starting from
n=1
, define:

\alpha_{n}=\inf\left\{\sup_{\omega\in L(\{\phi_{i}\})}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g-\omega\right\|:g\in V_{n}\left(\{\psi_{j}^{n-1}\}_{j}\right),\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}\in V\left(\{\psi_{j}^{n-1}\}_{j}\right)\right\}

Then pick
g_{n}\in V_{n}(\{\psi_{j}^{n-1}\}_{j}),\{\phi_{i}^{n}\}_{i}\in V(\{\psi_{j}^{n-1}\}_{j})
such that:

(6)

\alpha_{n}\leq\sup_{\omega\in L(\{\phi_{i}^{n}\}_{i\geq 1})}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}-\omega\right\|<\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})

Then pick
\omega_{n}\in L(\{\phi_{i}^{n}\}_{i\geq 1})
such that:

\alpha_{n}(1-\epsilon_{n})<\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}-\omega_{n}\right\|<\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})

and
x_{n}\in B_{\leq 1}
such that:

\alpha_{n}(1-\epsilon_{n})<\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}(x_{n})+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}(x_{n})-\omega_{n}(x_{n})

Find
\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j}\subseteq\{\phi_{i}^{n}\}_{i}
such that
\lim_{j}\psi_{j}(x_{n})=\liminf_{i}\phi_{i}(x_{n})
. Since
\{\psi_{j}^{n}(x_{n})\}_{j}
is convergent, then for each
\omega\in L(\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j\geq 1}),\omega(x_{n})=\lim_{j}\psi_{j}(x_{n})=\liminf_{i}\phi_{i}(x_{n})\leq\omega_{n}(x_{n})
. Therefore for each
\omega\in L(\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j})
:

(7)

\alpha_{n}(1-\epsilon_{n})<\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}(x_{n})+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}(x_{n})-\omega(x_{n})

After finding each
g_{n}
, because
g_{n}\in V_{n}(\{\psi_{i}^{n-1}\})_{i}
, we will have
\{g_{i}\}_{i>n}\subseteq V(\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j})
and hence
L(\{g_{i}\}_{i\geq n})\subseteq L(\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j})\subseteq L(\{\phi_{i}^{n}\})
. Notice that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
L(\{g_{i}\}_{i\geq 1})=L(\{g_{i}\}_{i\geq n})
. Then by (
6
) and (
7
), for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega\in L(\{g_{i}\}_{i\geq n})
, we have:

(8)

\alpha_{n}(1-\epsilon_{n})<\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}-\omega\right\|\leq\sup_{\omega\in L(\{\phi_{i}^{n}\}_{i\geq 1})}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}-\omega\right\|<\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})

Since, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
V(\{\psi_{j}^{n-1}\}_{j})\subseteq V(\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j})
, similar to the proof of
Lemma
2.5
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\{\sup_{\omega\in L(\{\phi_{i}\})}\left\|\sum_{i<n+1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n+1}\lambda_{i}\right)g-\omega\right\|:g\in V_{n+1}\left(\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j}\right),\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}\in V\left(\{\psi_{j}^{n}\}_{j}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{\sup_{\omega\in L(\{\phi_{i}\})}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g-\omega\right\|:g\in V_{n}\left(\{\psi_{j}^{n-1}\}_{j}\right),\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}\in V\left(\{\psi_{j}^{n-1}\}_{j}\right)\right\}

Hence we have
\alpha_{n}
is non-decreasing. Since for each
\omega\in V(\{f_{n}\}_{n})
, we have
\omega\leq 1
, we have
\alpha_{n}\leq 2
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies that
\alpha=\lim_{n}\alpha_{n}=\sup_{n}\alpha_{n}
exists. Since
\alpha_{1}\geq\theta
, we then have
\theta\leq\alpha\leq 2
. For each
\omega\in L(\{g_{i}\}_{i})
, by (
8
) we have:

\displaystyle\alpha=\lim_{n}\alpha_{n}(1-\epsilon_{n})\leq\lim_{n}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right)g_{n}-\omega\right\|\leq\lim_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})=\alpha

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{i}g_{i}-\omega\right\|=\alpha

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega\in L(\{g_{i}\}_{i})
. Then by (
8
):

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|=\left\|\frac{\lambda_{n}+\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left[\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right]+\lambda_{n}(g_{n}-\omega)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)+\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)(g_{n}-\omega)\right\|+\frac{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}-\left(\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}\right)g_{n}-\omega\right\|+\frac{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\frac{\lambda_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|\right]

\displaystyle\implies\,\dfrac{1}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|<\dfrac{\lambda_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}+\dfrac{1}{\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|

Then by induction on
n
we have:

(9)

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\frac{\lambda_{n}\alpha_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})}{\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{\lambda_{n-1}\alpha_{n-1}(1+\epsilon_{n-1})}{\sum_{i>n-1}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq n-1}\lambda_{i}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{i\geq n-1}\lambda_{i}}\left\|\sum_{i<n-1}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|\right]

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{2\leq k\leq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}(1+\epsilon_{k})}{\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq k}\lambda_{k}}+\frac{1}{\sum_{i\geq 2}\lambda_{i}}\|\lambda_{1}(g_{1}-\omega)\|\right]

\displaystyle<\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}\alpha_{k}(1+\epsilon_{k})}{\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq k}\lambda_{i}}

Since
\alpha=\sup_{n}\alpha_{n}
, from (
9
) we have:

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|

\displaystyle<\alpha\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq k}\lambda_{i}}+\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\frac{\lambda_{k}\epsilon_{k}}{\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}\sum_{i\geq k}\lambda_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle<\alpha\left(\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)\left[\sum_{k\leq n}\left(\frac{1}{\sum_{i>k}\lambda_{i}}-\frac{1}{\sum_{i\geq k}\lambda_{i}}\right)+(1-\theta)\right]

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)

∎

2.3.
Proof of Main Theorems

Proof of Theorem 2.1:

•

1)\implies 2)
:
Suppose
Q
is the canonical mapping from
X
to
X^{\ast\ast}
and
B
is not reflexive. Now pick
F\in X^{\ast\ast}
such that
d[F,Q(B)]>\theta
where
\|F\|<1
and
\theta\in(0,1)
. Suppose
\{x_{n}\}
is dense in
B
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
pick
f_{n}\in B^{*}
such that:

a)

\|f_{n}\|<1

b)

F(f_{n})=0

c)

f_{n}(x_{i})=0\,\forall\,i\leq n

Indeed, according to
Theorem
3.7
, this will be true if there is
M>0
such that
\theta\leq M\|t_{0}F+\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\|
for any
\{t_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
. Here if we let
M=\dfrac{\theta}{d[F,Q(X)]}<1
, then given
F,Q(x_{1})\in X^{\ast\ast}
, we can find
f_{1}\in X^{\ast}_{<1}
such that
F(f_{1})=\theta
and
Q(x_{1})(f_{1})=f_{1}(x_{1})=0
. Next, with
F,Q(x_{1}),Q(x_{2})\in X^{\ast\ast}
, we then can find
f_{2}\in X^{\ast}_{<1}
such that
F(f_{2})=\theta
,
f_{2}(x_{1})=f_{2}(x_{2})=0
. By induction, we can find
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq X^{\ast}_{<1}
that satisfies conditions
a),b)
and
c)
. Notice that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\|f_{n}\|=\sup_{\phi\in X^{\ast\ast}}|\,\phi(f_{n})\,|

Hence
\|f_{n}\|\geq\theta
for each
n
and hence for each
f\in\operatorname{conv}\{f_{n}\}
,
\|f\|\geq\theta
. Meanwhile, we have:

\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\lim_{n}f_{n}(x_{i})=0\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\forall\,x\in X,\hskip 8.5359pt\lim_{n}f_{n}(x)=0

•

2)\implies 3)
:
Given
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq X^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
, the set
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq\operatorname{conv}\{f_{n}\}
and the
\alpha\in[\theta,1]
obtained by
Lemma
2.5
completes the proof

•

3)\implies 4)
:
We will show
\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}
does not attain its
\sup
in the closed unit ball of
X
. Fix
x\in B
and find
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
g_{i}(x)<\alpha\theta\,\forall\,i>n
. Then:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}(x)

\displaystyle<\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}(x)+\alpha\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle<\left\|\sum_{i\geq n}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\right\|+\alpha\theta\sum_{i<>n}\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle<\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)+\alpha\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}=\alpha

Because
\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\|=\alpha
, such function never obtains its norm in the closed unit ball.

•

4)\implies 1)
:
If
B
is reflexive, then foe each
f\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}
we can find
F\in X^{\ast\ast}_{\leq 1}
such that
F(f)=\|f\|
. Then we can find
x\in X_{\leq 1}
such that
Q(x)=F
and hence
f(x)=\|f\|

∎

Proof of Theorem 2.2:

•

1)\implies 2)
:
According to the
Eberlein–Šmulian’s Theorem
, if
B
is not reflexive, there exists a (linearly) independent sequence
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq B_{\leq 1}
such that
\{x_{i}\}
has no clustered points in weak topology. Therefore, let
X
denote the closure of the span of
\{x_{i}\}
and then
X
will not be reflexive. By
Theorem
2.1
, there is
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq X^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
such that
\{f_{n}\}
converges to zero pointwise on
X
and
\left\|f\Big|_{X}\right\|\geq\theta
for each
f\in\operatorname{conv}\{f_{n}\}
. Extending each
f_{n}
by setting
f_{n}\Big|_{B\backslash X}=0
completes the proof.

•

2)\implies 3)
:
Since
\{f_{n}\}
converges to zero pointwise,
L(\{f_{n}\})=\{0\}
and hence the proof follows by
Lemma
2.7
.

•

3)\implies 4)
:
By making
\lambda_{1}
comparatively large enough, we can find
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2})
such that for each
n,\lambda_{n+1}<\delta\lambda_{n}
. We then will show that
\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)
does not obtain its
\sup
in the closed unit ball for each
\omega\in L(\{g_{i}\})
. By the fact that
\{g_{n}\}
converges to zero pointwise, fix an arbitrary
x\in B_{\leq 1}
and then we can find large
m,n\in\mathbb{N}\,(m\leq n)
such that:

\displaystyle g_{n+1}(x)<\inf_{k\geq m}g_{k}(x)+\theta^{2}-2\delta\leq\liminf_{k}g_{k}(x)+\theta^{2}-2\delta\leq\omega(x)+\theta^{2}-2\delta

\displaystyle\implies\,g_{n+1}(x)-\omega(x)<\theta^{2}-2\delta

According to the proof in
Lemma
2.7
we have
\theta\leq\alpha
and hence
g_{n+1}(x)-\omega(x)\leq\alpha\theta-2\delta
, which implies:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)(x)

\displaystyle<\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)(x)+(\alpha\theta-2\delta)\lambda_{n+1}+\sum_{i>n+1}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)\right\|+(\alpha\theta-2\delta)\lambda_{n+1}+2\sum_{i>n+1}\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle<\alpha\left(1-\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}\right)+(\alpha\theta-2\theta)\lambda_{n+1}+2\sum_{i>n+1}\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle<\alpha-\alpha\theta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}+(\alpha\theta-2\delta)\lambda_{n+1}+2\delta\sum_{i>n}\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=\alpha-(\alpha\theta-2\delta)\sum_{i>n+1}\lambda_{i}<\alpha-(\theta^{2}-2\delta)<\alpha

Hence
\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}(g_{i}-\omega)(x)<\alpha\,\forall\,x\in B_{\leq 1}

∎

Remark 2.8

(
[
NB11
]
)
.

Given topological vector space
T
, according to
[
Pta59
, Theorem 5.11.4]
, there exists a family of Banach spaces
(\{B_{\alpha}\}_{,}\|\cdot\|_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Omega}
such that
T
is linearly isomorphic to a subspace (not necessarily closed in the product topology generated by each norm in
B_{\alpha}
) of
\prod_{\alpha\in\Omega}B_{\alpha}
. Set
B=\prod_{\alpha\in\Omega}B_{\alpha}
. By the same theorem, if
T
is complete, then its image in
B
will be closed and hence weakly closed. Set
P_{\alpha}
to be the canonical projection from
B
to
B_{\alpha}
. Given a separable subset
X\subseteq B
, by
Tychonorff’s Theorem
,
X
is weakly compact iff
X_{\alpha}=P_{\alpha}X
is weakly compact for each
\alpha\in\Omega
. Therefore, if
X
is not weakly compact, there exists
\beta\in\Omega
such that the weakly closure of
X_{\beta}
is not weakly compact in
B_{\beta}
. Notice that the weak closure of
X_{\beta}
is contained in
\overline{\operatorname{conv}(X_{\beta})}^{\|\cdot\|_{\beta}}
. If
X
is separable, so is
X_{\beta}
. Hence
\overline{\operatorname{conv}(X_{\beta})}^{\|\cdot\|_{\beta}}
is separable and so is the weak closure of
X_{\beta}
.

Proof of Theorem 2.3:

According to
Remark
2.8
it suffices to prove the case where
X
is a separable bounded weakly closed subset in a Banach space.

•

1)\implies 2)
:
Suppose
X
is not weakly compact and let
Y=\overline{\operatorname{Span}X}
. Let
Q
be the canonical mapping from
B
to
B^{\ast\ast}
and if
Y
is reflexive, then
X
will be weakly compact and hence we can find
F\in Y^{\ast\ast}_{\leq 1}
that is not in
Q(Y)
. Assume
d[F,Q(Y)]>\delta>0

Now find
\{x_{n}\}
a countable dense subset of
X_{\leq 1}
. According to
Theorem
3.7
, by setting
M=\dfrac{\delta}{d[F,Q(Y)]}<1
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we could find
f_{n}\in Y^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
such that:

(1)

\|f_{n}\|<1

(2)

F(f_{n})=\delta

(3)

f_{n}(x_{i})=0\,\forall\,i\leq n

because by any
\{t_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
:

\delta=\left|\,F(f_{n})+\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}(x_{i})\,\right|\leq M\left\|F+\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\right\|

Now given for each
f\in\operatorname{conv}\{f_{i}\}
, suppose
f=\sum_{i\leq m}\alpha_{i}f_{n_{i}}
where
n_{1}
is the smallest among
\{n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{m}\}
. Then:

\delta=\left|\,F(f)+f(x_{n_{1}})\,\right|\leq\|F\|\|f\|

Therefore letting
\theta=\dfrac{\delta}{\|F\|}
will prove
2)
.

•

2)\implies 3)
:
The result follows mainly by
Lemma
2.7
. Notice that
\alpha_{n}
(obtained in the proof of
Lemma
2.7
) is still non-decreasing. By scaling we could find
N>0
such that given
\|x\|\leq 1
we will have
\,|f_{n}(x)\,|\leq N\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}
. Because
X
is bounded, then each
\alpha_{n}\leq N\sup_{x\in X}\|x\|
and hence
\{\alpha_{n}\}
will still converges.

•

3)\implies 4)
:
Similar to the proof of
Theorem 2.2
, we will have
\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\|=\alpha
but
\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}
never obtained its norm in the closed unit ball of
X
.

•

4)\implies 1)
:
If
X
is weakly compact, then because each
f\in B^{*}
is continuous with respect to the weak topology, hence each
f
can obtain
\sup_{x\in X}f(x)
in
X

∎

Proof of Theorem 2.4:

•

1)\implies 2)
: First, according to
Remark
2.8
, it suffices to consider the case where
X
is a bounded weakly closed subset of a Banach space. If
X
is not weakly compact, then the result follows by the proof of
Theorem 2.2
.

•

2)\implies 3)\implies 4)\implies 1)
: The result follows by the proof of
Theorem 2.3

∎

3.
Characterizations by affine sets

This section will focus on characterizing reflexivity or non-reflexivity by studying weakly compact subsets in a Banach space or its dual. Different from the geometric method that is used in
Section 2
, in
Section 3.2
, the method to construct a weakly vanishing sequence of bounded linear functionals, whose closed convex hull is distant from zero by using
Helly’s condition
(see
Theorem
3.7
), a result that precisely describes when a finite set of hyperplanes in a Banach space has non-empty intersection. The method to prove weakly compactness in
Section 3.1
is still geometric and is mainly based on several separations theorems.

3.1.
Affine sets in a Banach space

Definition 3.1
.

Given a normed linear space
X
, call a subset
E

affine
iff
E-e
is a proper subspace for any
e\in E
. Given any subset
A\subseteq X
, we use
\operatorname{lin}(A)
to denote the smallest flat set that contains
A
.

Definition 3.2
.

Given a normed linear space
X
, for any subset
A\subseteq X
define
\|A\|=\inf_{a\in A}\|a\|
.

Theorem 3.3

(
[
Day73
, Theorem I.6.2 (Mazur’s Theorem)]
)
.

Given a locally convex topological vector space (in short LCTVS)
X
, let
K
be a convex set with non-empty interior and
E
a affine set
disjoint from
K
. Then there is a closed hyperplane
H
such that
H\cap K=\emptyset
but
E\subseteq H
. In other words, there is
f\in X^{*}
and a real number such that
f(x)=c\,\forall\,x\in E
and
f(y)>c
for any
y\in\operatorname{int}(K)
.

Proposition 3.4

(
[
Day73
, Chapter II, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

Given
E
, a closed proper affine set that does not contain zero, in a normed linear space
X
, we can always find a hyperplane
H
such that
\|H\|=\|E\|
.

Proof.

Since
\|E\|>0
, the open ball that is centred at the origin and has radius
\|E\|
is disjoint from
E
. Then, by
Theorem
3.3
, there is a hyperplane
H
that separates
E
and that open ball. Therefore
\|E\|\geq\|H\|
. However, because
H
is disjoint from that open ball with radius
\|E\|
we then have
\|H\|\geq\|E\|

∎

Proposition 3.5

(
[
Day73
, Chapter II, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

A real-valued function
f
defined on a subset
A\subseteq X
has an extension
F
on
X
with
\|F\|\leq M
iff for some
M>0
,
|\,\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f(x_{i})\,|\leq M\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}x_{i}\|
for any choice of real numbers
t_{i}
and any choice of
x_{i}\in A
.

Proof.

The
(\implies)
direction is obvious. On the other direction, we can define:

F:\operatorname{Span}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}x_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f(x_{i})

Since
F
is a bounded linear functional on
\operatorname{Span}(A)
,
F
can be continuously extended to be defined on
\overline{\operatorname{Span}(A)}
. Notice that the function
M\|\cdot\|
is a semi-norm defined on
X
and dominates
F
. Then by
Hahn-Banach Theorem
,
F
can be extended to
X
and satisfies
\|F\|\leq M
.

∎

Proposition 3.6

(
[
Day73
, Chapter II, Corollary 4.1]
)
.

Given a finite set
F_{x}=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}\subseteq X
and a finite set
F_{r}=\{r_{1},\cdots,r_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
there exists
f\in X^{*}
such that
f(x_{i})=r_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
iff for some
M>0
,
|\,\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}r_{i}\,|\leq M\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}x_{i}\|
for all choice of real numbers
t_{i}

Proof.

Given
f\in X^{\ast}
with
f(x_{i})=r_{i}
for each
i\leq n
, we will have
|\,f(x)\,|\leq\|f\|\|x\|
for each
x\in\operatorname{Span}(F_{x})
. On the other hand, if there exists
M>0
such that the given inequality holds for all choice of scalars
t_{i}
, define
f
on
F_{x}
by
f(x_{i})=r_{i}
for each
i\leq n
. Then the conclusion follows by
Proposition
3.5
.
∎

Theorem 3.7

(
[
Day73
, Chapter II, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

[Helly’s condition]
If
\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*},M>0,\{c_{1},\cdots,c_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, then for each
\epsilon>0
there is
x\in X
with
\|x\|<M+\epsilon
such that
f_{i}(x)=c_{i}
for all
i\leq n
iff:

\left|\,\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}c_{i}\,\right|\leq M\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\|

for all choice of real numbers
t_{i}
.

Equivalently, if we define
H_{i}=f_{i}^{-1}\{c_{i}\}
,
E=\bigcap_{i\leq n}H_{i}
and:

M_{0}=\sup\left\{\frac{|\,\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}c_{i}\,|}{\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\|}:\{t_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\hskip 5.69046pt\&\hskip 5.69046pt\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\right\|>0\right\}

Then we claim
\|E\|=M_{0}
.

Proof.

1)

(\Longrightarrow):
For each
\epsilon>0
and
x\in E
, we have that for any finite set of scalars
\{t_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
:

\left|\,\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}c_{i}\,\right|=\left|\,\big(\sum_{i\leq n}c_{i}f_{i}\big)(x)\,\right|\leq\|x\|\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\right\|\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359ptM_{0}\leq\|x\|

If
\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\right\|>0
, since
x
is arbitrarily piced from
E
, we then have
M_{0}\leq\|E\|
.

2)

(\Longleftarrow)
: WLOG suppose the set
\{f_{i}\}
is linear independent and hence for each
i\leq n
,
\bigcap_{j\leq n,j\neq i}\operatorname{Ker}f_{j}\backslash\operatorname{Ker}f_{i}
is non-empty. For each
i\leq n
, pick
y_{i}\in\bigcap_{j\leq n,j\neq i}\operatorname{Ker}f_{j}\backslash\operatorname{Ker}f_{i}
. If we set
y=\sum_{i\leq n}c_{i}y_{i}
, we then have
f_{i}(y)=c_{i}
for all
i\leq n
. Therefore
y\in E
. By
Proposition
3.4
, we can find a hyperplane
H
that contains
E
such that
\|H\|=\|E\|
. Suppose
H=f^{-1}\{c\}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
and
f\in X^{*}
. Then:

E-y=\bigcap_{i\leq n}\big(H_{i}-y\big)=\bigcap_{i\leq n}\operatorname{Ker}f_{i}\subseteq H-y=\operatorname{Ker}f

which implies that
f=\sum_{i\leq n}r_{i}f_{i}
for a finite set of scalars
\{r_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
and that, for each
x\in E
,
f(x)=f(y)
. Suppose
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq X_{=1}
is a sequence such that
f(x_{n})\rightarrow\|f\|
. Then
\dfrac{c}{f(x_{n})}x_{n}\in H
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and:

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\|H\|\leq\left\|\frac{c}{f(x_{n})}x_{n}\right\|\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\|H\|\leq\lim_{n}\left\|\frac{c}{f(x_{n})}x_{n}\right\|=\frac{|\,c\,|}{\|f\|}

Hence, we have
\|H\|=\dfrac{|\,c\,|}{\|f\|}
. Then:

\|E\|=\|H\|=\frac{|\,c\,|}{\|f\|}=\frac{|\,\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}c_{i}\,|}{\|\sum_{i\leq n}t_{i}f_{i}\|}\leq M_{0}

Hence
\|E\|\leq M_{0}
, which implies
\|E\|=M_{0}
.

∎

Corollary 3.8

(
[
Day73
, Chapter II, Corollary 4.2]
)
.

Given a normed linear space
X
and
\phi\in X^{\ast\ast}
, let
\{f_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq X^{*},H_{i}=\{x\in X\,|\,f_{i}(x)=\phi(f_{i})\},E=\bigcap_{i\leq n}H_{i}
, then we have
\|E\|\leq\|\phi\|

Proof.

Immediately follows by the second part of
Theorem
3.7
.
∎

Corollary 3.9

(
[
Day73
, Chapter II, Corollary 4.3]
)
.

Let
Q
be the canonical mapping from
X
to
X^{\ast\ast}
and pick
\phi\in X^{\ast\ast}\backslash Q(X)
. Fix
c\in\mathbb{C}
with and define
H_{0}=\{f\in X^{*}\,|\,\phi(f)=c\}
. For any
M>0
, if
M>\dfrac{|\,c\,|}{d[\phi,Q(X)]}
then the zero linear functional in
X^{*}
is in the weak-
\ast
closure of
H_{0}\cap X^{*}_{<M}

Proof.

It suffices to prove the case where
c\neq 0
. To show that
0
is in the weak-
\ast
closure of
H_{0}\cap X^{\ast}_{<M}
, it suffices to show that for any finite (linear independent) subset
\{x_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\subset X
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists
h\in H_{0}\cap X^{\ast}_{<M}
such that
|\,h(x_{i})\,|<\epsilon
for each
1\leq i\leq n
. Let
\{x_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
be a linear independent subset of
X
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
set
H_{i}=\operatorname{Ker}Q(x_{i})
and
E=\bigcap_{0\leq i\leq n}H_{i}
. Since the set of linear functionals
\big\{Q(x_{1}),\cdots,Q(x_{n}),\phi\big\}
is linear independent, given
\{t_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
and
f\in E
, we have:

\left|\,\left[t_{0}\phi+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}t_{i}Q(x_{i})\right](f)\,\right|=\left|\,t_{0}\phi(f)+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}t_{i}f(x_{i})\,\right|=|\,t_{0}\phi(f)\,|

Therefore by
Theorem
3.7
, for each
y\in E
:

(10)

\displaystyle\|E\|

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\frac{|\,t_{0}\phi(f)\,|}{\|t_{0}\phi+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}t_{i}Q(x_{i})\|}:\{t_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\hskip 8.5359pt\&\hskip 8.5359pt\left\|t_{0}\phi+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}t_{i}Q(x_{i})\right\|>0\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\frac{|\,\phi(f)\,|}{\left\|\phi+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}\dfrac{t_{i}}{t_{0}}Q(x_{i})\right\|}:t_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\{0\},\,\{t_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\hskip 8.5359pt\&\hskip 8.5359pt\left\|t_{0}\phi+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}t_{i}Q(x_{i})\right\|>0\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\frac{|\,\phi(f)\,|}{\|\phi+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}t_{i}Q(x_{i})\|}:\{t_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\right\}\leq\frac{|\,\phi(f)\,|}{d\big[\phi,Q(X)\big]}=\frac{|\,c\,|}{d\big[\phi,Q(X)\big]}<M

Hence, there exists
h\in E
with
\|h\|<M
such that
h(x_{i})=0
for each
i\leq n
.

∎

Theorem 3.10

(
[
Day73
, Chapter III, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

In a normed linear space
X
the following statements about a bounded subset
E\subseteq X
are equivalent:

1)

E
is weakly compact

2)

E
is weakly sequentially compact

3)

E
is weakly countably compact

4)

For each
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq E
there is
e\in E
such that
\liminf_{n}f(e_{n})\leq f(e)\leq\limsup_{n}f(e_{n})
for all
f\in X^{*}
.

5)

If
\{K_{n}\}
is a decreasing sequence of closed convex sets in
X
and
K_{n}\cap E\neq\emptyset
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}\cap E\neq\emptyset
.

Below are several set-up results that are borrowed from
[
Day73
]
and for proving
Theorem
3.10
.

Theorem 3.11

(
[
Day73
, Theorem I.6.4 (Eidelheit Separation Theorem)]
)
.

In a LCTVS
X
let
K_{1},K_{2}
be convedx sets such that
K_{1}
has an interior point and
K_{2}
contains no interior points of
K_{1}
. Then there is a closed hyperplane
H
separating
K_{1}
from
K_{2}
; on the other hands, there is
f\in X^{*}
such that
\sup_{k_{2}\in K_{2}}f(k_{2})\leq\inf_{k_{1}\in K_{1}}f(k_{1})

Proof.

Let
K=K_{1}-K_{2}
. Therefore
K_{1}
has interior points and
0
is disjoint from
\operatorname{int}(K)
. By
Theorem 3.3
, there is a non-zero
f\in X^{*}
such that
f|_{K}\geq 0\,\implies\,f(k_{1})>f(k_{2})
for any
k_{1}\in K_{1}
and any
k_{2}\in K_{2}
. Now set
c=\inf_{k_{1}\in K_{1}}f(k_{1})
and let
H=f^{-1}\{c\}
. Then we have for each
k_{1}\in\operatorname{int}(K_{1}),f(k_{1})>c,f(k_{2})\leq c
for any
k_{2}\in K_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 3.12

(
[
Day73
, Chapter I, Theorem 6.5]
)
.

If
K
is a closed convex set in a LCTVS
X
and if
x\notin K
then there exists
f\in X^{*}
such that
f(x)>\sup_{k\in K}f(k)
.

Proof.

Take a convex neighborhood
K_{1}
of
x
and apply
Theorem 3.11
to
K_{1}
and
K_{2}=K

∎

Corollary 3.13

(
[
Day73
, Chapter I, Corollar 6.2]
)
.

Given
E
a subset of a LCTVS
X
, we have

\overline{\operatorname{conv}(E)}=\bigcap_{f\in X^{*}}\left\{x\in X\,|\,f(x)\leq\sup_{y\in E}f(y)\right\}

Definition 3.14
.

Given a topological vector space
X
, say a subspace
\Gamma\leq X^{*}
is total if for all
x\in X
,
f(x)=0
for all
f\in\Gamma
implies
x=0
. We use
\tau(\Gamma)
to denote the weak topology generated by
\Gamma
.

Theorem 3.15

(
[
DS88
, Theorem V.3.9]
)
.

In a topological vector space
X
, let
\Gamma
be a total subspace of linear functionals in
X^{*}
. Then all linear functionals continuous with respect to
\tau(\Gamma)
belong to
\Gamma

Proof.

Clearly all functionals in
\Gamma
are continuous with respect to
\tau(\Gamma)
. Now let
g\in X^{*}
be a
\tau(\Gamma)
-continuous linear functional. Suppose for some finite set
\{f_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq\Gamma
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, we have:

N(0,f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{n},\epsilon)=\{x\in X\,|\,f_{i}(x)<\epsilon\,\forall\,i\leq n\}\subseteq g^{-1}\Big(\big\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:|\,\lambda\,|\leq 1\big\}\Big)

WLOG assume
\{f_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
is linear independent. Pick
x_{0}\in\bigcap_{i\leq n}\operatorname{Ker}f_{i}
(by
X
having infinite dimension). Therefore
|\,g(x_{0})\,|<1
and also
|\,g(nx_{0})\,|<1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies
g(x_{0})=0
. Hence we have
\bigcap_{i\leq n}\operatorname{Ker}f_{i}\subseteq\operatorname{Ker}g
and conclude
g
is a linear combination of
\{f_{i}\}_{i\leq n}

∎

Proof of
Theorem
3.10
:

The implications of
1)\,\implies\,2),2)\,\implies\,3),3)\,\implies\,4)
are immediate. Then we will work on
4)\,\implies\,5),5)\,\implies\,1)
. Assume
4)
holds. Then pick
x_{n}\in K_{n}\cap E
and therefore obtain
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
. By
Corollary
3.13
we have:

K_{n}=\bigcap_{f\in X^{*}}\left\{x\in X\,|\,f(x)\leq\sup_{y\in K_{n}}f(y)\right\}

By
Corollary
3.13
we can find
x\in E
such that
\liminf_{n}f(x_{n})\leq f(x)\leq\limsup_{n}f(x_{n})
, for large
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
x\in K_{n}
and hence
x\in\bigcap_{n\geq 1}K_{n}\cap E
.

Then we will prove
5)\,\implies\,1)
. Let
Q
be the canonical mapping from
X
to
X^{\ast\ast}
. Assume by contradiction that there exists
\phi
in the weak-
\ast
closure of
Q(E)
but not in
Q(E)
. Therefore for each
v\in E
we can find
f_{v}\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}
and
\epsilon_{v}>0
such that
|\,f_{v}(v)-\phi(f_{v})\,|>\epsilon_{v}
. Then we have:

Q(E)\subseteq\bigcup_{v\in E_{\infty}}\Big\{\psi\in X^{\ast\ast}:|\,\psi(f_{v})-\phi(f_{v})\,|>\epsilon_{v}\Big\}

which implies:

Q(E)\subseteq\overline{Q(E)}^{w\ast}\subseteq\bigcup_{v\in E_{\infty}}\left\{\psi\in X^{\ast\ast}:|\,\psi(f_{v})-\phi(f_{v})\,|\geq\epsilon_{v}\right\}\subseteq\bigcup_{v\in E_{\infty}}\left\{\psi\in X^{\ast\ast}:|\,\psi(f_{v})-\phi(f_{v})\,|>\frac{\epsilon_{v}}{2}\right\}

Since the weak-
\ast
closure of
E
is weak-
\ast
compact, without losing generality, assume:

Q(E_{\infty})\subseteq\bigcup_{k\leq n}\left\{\psi\in X^{\ast\ast}:|\,\psi(g_{k})-\phi(g_{k})\,|>\frac{\epsilon_{k}}{2}\right\}

where
\{g_{1},g_{2},\cdots,g_{N}\}\subseteq\{f_{v}\,|\,v\in E_{\infty}\}
. Let
\epsilon_{0}<\min_{k\leq N}\epsilon_{k}
so that we have for each
v\in E
, there is
g\in\{g_{i}\}_{i\leq N}
such that
|\,g(v)-\phi(g)\,|>\epsilon_{0}
. Let
l
be smallest integer such that
\dfrac{1}{l}<\epsilon_{0}
.

Let
L_{n}=\left\{f\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}\,|\,\phi(f)>\dfrac{n}{n+1}\|\phi\|\right\}
and
L_{n+1}\subseteq L_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Again first pick an arbitrary
f_{1}\in L_{1}
and then find
x_{1}\in E
such that
|\,\phi(f_{1})-f_{1}(x_{1})\,|<\dfrac{1}{2}
. Next pick an arbitrary
f_{2}\in L_{2}
. There exists
x_{2}\in E
such that
|\,\phi(f_{2})-f_{2}(x_{2})\,|<\dfrac{1}{3}
and
|\,\phi(f_{1})-f_{1}(x_{2})\,|<\dfrac{1}{3}
. Repeat steps above until
l
(
l
is given above with
\dfrac{1}{l}<\epsilon_{0}
). For each
k\leq l
, set
B_{k}=\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}\}
(where
f_{i}\in L_{i}
for each
i\leq k
) and
S_{k}=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\}
. Then we have, for each
k\leq l
:

\forall\,i\leq j\leq k,\hskip 8.5359pt\big|\,\phi(f_{i})-f_{i}(x_{j})\,\big|<\frac{1}{j}

Since
\phi
is picked from the weak-
\ast
closure of
Q(E)
, for each
1\leq i\leq N
, we can find
x_{l+i}\in E
such that:

\forall\,j\leq i,\hskip 8.5359pt|\,\phi(g_{j})-g_{j}(x_{l+i})\,\big|<\frac{1}{l+i}

and:

\forall\,k\leq N,\hskip 8.5359pt\big|\,\phi(f_{k})-f_{k}(x_{l+i})\,\big|<\frac{1}{l+i}

For each
i\leq N
, set
f_{l+i}=g_{i}
,
B_{l+i}=\{f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{l+i}\}
and
S_{l+i}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{l+i}\}
. For each
k>l+N
, given an arbitrary
f_{k}\in L_{k}
, again we can find
x_{k}\in E
such that:

\forall\,j\leq k,\hskip 8.5359pt\big|\,\phi(f_{j})-f_{j}(x_{k})\,\big|<\frac{1}{k}

Then for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
B_{k}=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\}
is defined and each
B_{k}
is contained in
E
. For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, define
K_{j}=\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{x_{j},x_{j+1},\cdots\}}
. By
5)
, suppose
e\in E\cap\bigcap_{j\geq 1}K_{j}
. Hence we have:

\forall\,f\in\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}B_{k}\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 5.69046pt|\,\phi(f)-f(e)\,|<\frac{1}{n}\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\forall\,f\in\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}B_{k},\hskip 8.5359pt\phi(f)=f(e)

However, since
e\in E
, there exists
g\in\{g_{i}\}_{i\leq N}
such that
|\,\phi(g)-g(e)\,|>0
and meanwhile we have
\{g_{i}\}_{i\leq N}s\subseteq\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}B_{k}
. Hence, we obtain a contradiction and we can now conclude that
Q(E)
is weak-
\ast
close. Hence, since
E
is bounded,
Q(E)
is weak-
\ast
compact, or
E
is weakly compact.

∎

3.2.
Affine sets in the dual space

Proposition 3.16

(
[
DS88
, Theorem V.6.2]
)
.

In a normed linear space, any countable family of norm-closed convex subset, which possesses finite intersection property, has a non-empty intersection if and only if any countable family of weakly closed subset, which possesses finite intersection property, has a non-empty intersection.

Proof.

(\Longleftarrow):
The result follows by the fact that, in a normed linear space, the weak closure of a convex set coincides with its normed closure.

(\Longrightarrow):
Suppose
\{F_{n}\}
is a countable family of weakly-closed subset, which possesses finite intersection property, but has an empty intersection. Therefore, we have:

\emptyset=\operatorname{conv}\left(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}\right)=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{conv}(F_{n})\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\overline{\operatorname{conv}\left(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F_{n}\right)}=\overline{\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{conv}(F_{n})}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\operatorname{conv}(F_{n})}=\emptyset

Hence
\{\overline{\operatorname{conv}(F_{n})}\}
is a decreasing sequence of normed-closed convex set, which possesses finite intersection property but has empty intersection.

∎

Theorem 3.17

(
[
Jam64
, Theorem 1]
,
[
Jam64a
, Theorem 7]
)
.

Given a Banach space
X
the following statements are equivalent:

1)

X
is not reflexive

2)

For each
\theta\in(0,1)
there is a sequence
\{x_{i}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}_{<1}
such that:

g_{n}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}\theta,\hskip 28.45274pti\geq n\\
0,\hskip 28.45274pti<n\end{cases}

3)

There exists
\theta\in(0,1)
there is a sequence
\{x_{i}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}_{<1}
such that:

g_{n}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}\theta,\hskip 28.45274pti\geq n\\
0,\hskip 28.45274pti<n\end{cases}

4)

For each
\theta\in(0,1)
there is a sequence
\{x_{i}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

d[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>n}]\geq\theta

5)

For some
\theta\in(0,1)
there is a sequence
\{x_{i}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

d[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>n}]\geq\theta

Proof.

•

1)\implies 2)
: Assume
X
is not reflexive. Then by
Riesz’s Lemma
, for each
\theta\in(0,1)
there exists
f\in X^{\ast\ast}
with
\|f\|=1
such that
d\big[f,Q(X)\big]>\theta
. Hence, there exists
\phi\in X^{\ast\ast\ast}_{\leq 1}
such that
Q(X)\subseteq\operatorname{Ker}\phi
and
\phi(f)>\theta
. First, find
g_{1}\in X^{\ast\ast}_{<1}
such that
f(g_{1})=\theta
. Then, for any
a_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have:

(11)

|\,a_{1}\theta\,|=|\,f(a_{1}g_{1})\,|\leq\|a_{1}g_{1}\|

Hence, by
Theorem
3.10
, there exists
x_{1}\in X_{\leq 1}
with
g_{1}(x_{1})=\theta
. Next, for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have:

(12)

|\,a_{2}\theta\,|=\frac{\theta}{\phi(f)}|\,\phi(a_{2}f)\,|=\frac{\theta}{\phi(f)}\Big|\,\phi\big[a_{2}f+a_{1}Q(x_{1})\big]\,\Big|\leq\frac{\theta}{\phi(f)}\|a_{2}f+a_{1}Q(x_{1})\|

Again by
Theorem
3.7
, there exists
g_{2}\in X^{\ast}
with
\|g_{2}\|<1
(since
\phi(f)>\theta
) such that
f(g_{2})=\theta
and
g_{2}(x_{1})=0
. With
g_{1},g_{2}\in X^{\ast}_{<1}
and
x_{1}\in X_{\leq 1}
, for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, replace
a_{1}g_{1}
in (
11
) by
a_{1}g_{1}+a_{2}g_{2}
and then we have:

|\,a_{1}\theta+a_{2}\theta\,|=|\,f(a_{1}g_{1}+a_{2}g_{2})\,|\leq\|a_{1}g_{1}+a_{2}g_{2}\|

By
Theorem
3.7
, we can find
x_{2}\in X_{\leq 1}
such that
g_{2}(x_{2})=g_{1}(x_{2})=\theta
. Next, with
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
, replace
a_{2}f+a_{1}Q(x_{1})
in (
12
) by
a_{3}f+a_{2}Q(x_{2})+a_{1}Q(x_{1})
. Then by
Theorem
3.7
, we can then find
g_{3}\in X^{\ast}_{<1}
such that
f(g_{3})=\theta
and
g_{3}(x_{1})=g_{3}(x_{2})=0
. By induction, we then can obtain the desired
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{\ast}_{<1}
and
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
.

•

2)\implies 3)
: Immediate.

•

3)\implies 4)
: Let
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{\ast}_{<1}
be given as
g_{n}(x_{i})=\theta
for all
i\geq n
and zero for all
i<n
. Now for any
x\in\operatorname{conv}\{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}\},z\in\operatorname{conv}\{x_{n+1},x_{n+2},\cdots\},\|z-x\|\geq|\,f_{n+1}(z-x)\,|=\theta
.

•

4)\implies 5)
: Immediate.

•

5)\implies 1)
: Let
C_{n}=\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{x_{j}\}_{j>n}}
. If
X
is reflexive, the normed closed unit ball of
X
is also weakly compact, which, by
Proposition
3.16
, implies that
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}C_{n}\neq\emptyset
. Let
y\in\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}C_{n}
. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
x_{n}\in C_{n}
with
\|x_{n}-y\|<\dfrac{1}{n}
. Therefore, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\|x_{n}-x_{n+1}\|\leq\|x_{n}-y\|+\|x_{n+1}-y\|<\frac{2}{n}\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359ptd\big[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n},C_{n}\big]<\frac{2}{n}\overset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

∎

4.
Characterizations by Schauder basis

This section includes results that describe reflexivity based on properties of a Schauder basis. It is clear that all subspaces of a reflexive Banach space are reflexive and, for instance,
\ell^{1}
,
c_{0}
are rather famous non-reflexive Banach spaces. One way to characterize a non-reflexive Banach space is to show that there exists a subspace that is linearly isomorphic to
\ell^{1}
or
c_{0}
. We will see how the existence of such a subspace is related to certain properties of a Schauder basis. Meanwhile when a subspace is linearly isomorphic to
\ell^{1}
or
c_{0}
, a non-separable subspace will exist in the double dual. As a side result, checking separability of the double dual (see
Corollary
4.24
) can also help with checking reflexivity. On the other hand, since a Banach space is reflexive if and only if its dual is reflexive (see
Theorem
4.23
), techniques introduced above can also be applied to determine when the dual space is reflexive. We will also see even in a Banach space with a Schauder basis, the set of linear functionals defined by giving the
n
-th coefficients is not always a Schauder basis of the dual space.

4.1.
Rosenthal’s Condition

Definition 4.1
.

In an infinite set
S
, given
X\subseteq S
and a sequence of subsets
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we say
\{A_{n}\}

converges on
X
if
\{\chi_{A_{n}}\}
converges pointwise on
X
. In particular if
X=\emptyset
, we say
\{A_{n}\}

converges vaguely on

X
. We call
\{A_{n}\}

Boolean independent
if for every pair of disjoint finite subsets
N_{1},N_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
\max N_{1}<\min N_{2}
:

\bigcap_{i\in N_{1}}\bigcap_{j\in N_{2}}A_{i}\backslash A_{j}\neq\emptyset

Definition 4.2
.

In an infinite set
S
, let
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of pair of subsets of
S
such that
A_{n}\cap B_{n}=\emptyset
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
X\subseteq S
, we say
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}

converges on

X
if
\{A_{n}\}
or
\{B_{n}\}
converges on
X
. In particular, if
X=\emptyset
, we say
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges vaguely on

X
. We call
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}

Boolean independent
if for every pair of disjoint finite subsets
N_{1},N_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
\max N_{1}<\min N_{2}
:

\bigcap_{i\in N_{1}}\bigcap_{j\in N_{2}}A_{i}\cap B_{j}\neq\emptyset

Given two infinite subsets
M_{1},M_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, we call
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{1}}
is a
subsequence of

\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{2}}
if
M_{1}\backslash M_{2}
is finite.

Lemma 4.3
.

Given an infinite set
S
and
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a sequence of pair of subsetes of
S
with
A_{n}\cap B_{n}=\emptyset
for each
n
, suppose that for each
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
\{X_{i}\}_{i\leq l}
a
l
-size family of disjoint subsets of
S
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has no convergent subsequences on each
X_{i}
. Then there exists an infinite subset
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
K\in M
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
has no convergent subsequences on
X_{i}\cap A_{K}
or
X_{i}\cap B_{K}
for each
i\leq l
.

Proof.

We will complete the proof by induction. When
l=1
, by assumption
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}
has no convergent subsequences on
S
. Next, assume by contradiction that for any infinite subset
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
has a convergent subsequence on
A_{K}
or
B_{K}
. Start from
M_{0}=\mathbb{N}
and
n_{0}=1
. Then we can find an infinite subset
N_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in N_{1}}
converges on
A_{1}
or
B_{1}
. Set
M_{1}=N_{1}\cap M_{0}=N_{1}
and hence
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{1}}
converges on
A_{1}
or
B_{1}
. Set
n_{1}=\min M_{1}
. By assumption, we then can find an infinite subset
N_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
N_{2}\backslash M_{1}
is finite and
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in N_{2}}
converges on
A_{n_{2}}
or
B_{n_{2}}
. Set
M_{2}=N_{2}\cap M_{1}
,
n_{2}=\min M_{2}
and hence
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{2}}
converges on
A_{n_{2}}
or
B_{n_{2}}
, as well as on
A_{n_{1}}
or
B_{n_{1}}
. By induction, we then can find an non-decreasing sequence of integers
\{n_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, an decreasing (in the sense of inclusion) sequence of infinite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, such that for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{n_{i}\}_{i\geq j}\subseteq M_{j}
and
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{j}}
converges on
A_{i}
or
B_{i}
for each
i\leq j
.

Next, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{i}}
converges on
A_{n_{i}}
, set
a_{i}=1
and define
M_{a}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,a_{i}=1\}
; if
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{i}}
converges on
B_{n_{i}}
, set
b_{i}=1
and define
M_{b}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,b_{i}=1\}
. Since for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{i}}
converges on
A_{n_{i}}
or
B_{i}
, we have at least one of
M_{a}
or
M_{b}
must infinite. Without losing generality, assume
M_{a}
be infinite. For each
i\in M_{a}
and
x\in A_{n_{i}}
:

•

If
\lim_{n\in M_{i}}\chi_{A_{n}}(x)=0
, then there exists
n(i,x)\in\mathbb{N}
with
n(i,x)\geq n_{i}
such that for each
n\in M_{i}
,
x\notin A_{n}
whenever
n\geq n(i,x)
.

•

If
\lim_{n\in M_{i}}\chi_{A_{n}}(x)=1
, then there exists
n(i,x)\in\mathbb{N}
with
n(i,x)\geq n_{i}
such that for each
n\in M_{i}
,
x\in A_{n}
whenever
n\geq n(i,x)
. In this case, since
A_{k}\cap B_{k}=\emptyset
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we will have that
x\notin B_{n}
for each
n\in N_{i}
whenever
n\geq n(i,x)

According to our assumption, for any infinite subset
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
,
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
is not convergent on
S
. In particular, for the subsequence
\{(A_{n_{i}},B_{n_{i}})\}_{i\in M_{a}}
, there exists
y\in S
such that all of the following sets are infinite.

\{i\in M_{a}\,|\,y\in A_{n_{i}}\},\hskip 5.69046ptM_{a}\backslash\{i\in M_{a}\,|\,y\in A_{n_{i}}\},\hskip 5.69046pt\{i\in M_{a}\,|\,y\in B_{n_{i}}\},\hskip 5.69046ptM_{a}\backslash\{i\in M_{a}\,|\,y\in B_{n_{i}}\}

However, if we fix
i\in M_{a}
such that
y\in A_{n_{i}}
, we then can find
n(i,y)\geq n_{i}
such that either:

(13)

\forall\,k\in\big\{n\in M_{i}\,|\,n\geq n(i,y)\big\},\hskip 8.5359pt\chi_{A_{k}}(y)=0

or:

(14)

\forall\,k\in\big\{n\in M_{i}\,|\,n\geq n(i,y)\big\},\hskip 8.5359pt\chi_{B_{k}}(y)=0

Recall that for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{n_{i}\}_{i\geq j}\subseteq M_{j}
. If (
13
) is true, since the set
\{i\in M_{a}\,|\,y\in A_{n_{i}}\}
is infinite, there exists
J\in\{i\in M_{a}\,|\,y\in A_{n_{i}}\}
such that
n_{J}\geq n(i,y)
and
y\in A_{n_{J}}
. Then, since
n_{J}\geq n(i,y)\geq n_{i}
,
n_{J}\in M_{i}
and hence
y\notin A_{n_{J}}
by (
13
), which is absurd. The case when (
14
) is true will lead to the same contradiction. Therefore, the base case is proved.

The base case when
l=1
is proved and now by induction assume the statement holds when
l=n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we will prove the case when
l=n+1
. Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\leq n+1}
be a
(n+1)
-size of disjoint subsets of
S
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has no convergent subsequences on each
X_{i}
. Then by assumption there exists an infinite
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
K\in M
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
has no convergent subsequences on
X_{i}\cap A_{K}
or
X_{i}\cap B_{K}
for each
i\leq n
. Assume by contradiction that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
has a convergent subsequence on
X_{n+1}\cap A_{K}
or
X_{n+1}\cap B_{K}
. Suppose
M^{\prime}
is the infinite set that make
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M^{\prime}}
convergent on
A_{K}\cap X_{n+1}
or
B_{K}\cap X_{n+1}
and we can assume
K\in M^{\prime}
. Let
K_{1}=K
and
M_{1}=\{n\in M^{\prime}\,|\,n>K_{1}\}
.
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{1}}
is a subsequence of
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and hence does not converge on each
X_{i}(i\leq n+1)
. By assumption there exists
M_{2}\subseteq M^{\prime}
and
K_{2}\in M_{2}
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{2}}
has no convergent subsequences on
X_{i}\cap A_{K_{2}}
or
X_{i}\cap B_{K_{2}}
for each
i\leq n
. If
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{2}}
has a convergent subsequence on
X_{n+1}\cap A_{K_{2}}
or
X_{n+1}\cap B_{K_{2}}
, suppose
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in(M_{2})^{\prime}}
is the desired subsequence and we can assume
K_{2}\in(M_{2})^{\prime}
.

Assume the process above will not stop. Then for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{i}}
is convergent on
X_{n+1}\cap A_{K_{i}}
or
X_{n+1}\cap B_{K_{i}}
. Then for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, set
a_{i}=1
if
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{i}}
converges on
X_{n+1}\cap A_{K_{i}}
; set
b_{i}=1
if
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{i}}
converges on
X_{n+1}\cap B_{K_{i}}
. Again define
N_{a}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,a_{i}=1\}
and
N_{b}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,b_{i}=1\}
. Without losing generality, assume
N_{a}
is infinite. Hence
\{(A_{K_{i}},B_{K_{i}})\}_{i\in N_{a}}
is a subsequence and has no convergent subsequences on
X_{n+1}
. Then there exists
y\in X_{n+1}
such that all of the following sets are infinite:

\{i\in N_{a}\,|\,y\in A_{K_{i}}\},\hskip 5.69046ptN_{a}\backslash\{i\in N_{a}\,|\,y\in A_{K_{i}}\},\hskip 5.69046pt\{i\in N_{a}\,|\,y\in B_{K_{i}}\},\hskip 5.69046ptN_{a}\backslash\{i\in N_{a}\,|\,y\in B_{K_{i}}\},

Fix
j\in M_{a}
such that
y\in A_{K_{j}}
. Since
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{j}}
is convergent on
X_{n+1}\cap A_{K_{j}}
or
X_{n+1}\cap B_{K_{j}}
, we then can find
k(j,y)\geq K_{i}
such that either:

(15)

\forall\,k\in\{m\in M_{j}\,|\,m\geq k(j,y)\},\hskip 8.5359pt\chi_{A_{k}}(y)=0

or

(16)

\forall\,k\in\{m\in M_{j}\,|\,m\geq k(j,y)\},\hskip 8.5359pt\chi_{B_{k}}(y)=0

Recall that
\{K_{i}\}_{i\geq j}\subseteq M_{j}
. If (
15
) if true, then there exists
J\in\{i\in N_{a}\,|\,y\in A_{K_{i}}\}
such that
K_{J}\geq k(j,y)
and
y\in A_{K_{J}}
. However, by (
15
) we also have
y\notin A_{K_{J}}
, which is absurd. The case when (
16
) is true can also lead to a contradiction similarly.

∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of pairs of sets where
A_{n}\cap B_{n}=\emptyset\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}
has no convergent subsequence on
S
. Then we can find an infinite set
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
so that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
is Boolean Independent.

Proof.

According to the proof of base case in
Lemma
4.3
, we can find
M_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
n_{1}\in M_{1}
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{1}}
has no convergent subsequence on
A_{n_{1}}
or
B_{n_{1}}
. With
S
replaced by
A_{n_{1}}
of
B_{n_{1}}
(depending on which set is the one where
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{1}}
has no convergent subsequence), apply the proof of the base case in
Lemma
4.3
again, we then can find an infinite subset
M_{2}\subseteq M_{1}
and
