n_{2}\in M_{2}
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{2}}
has no convergent subsequence on each of the following sets:

A_{n_{1}}\cap A_{n_{2}},\hskip 5.69046ptA_{n_{1}}\cap B_{n_{2}},\hskip 5.69046ptB_{n_{1}}\cap A_{n_{2}},\hskip 5.69046ptB_{n_{1}}\cap B_{n_{2}}

Repeat the method above and then obtain an infinite sequence of integers
\{n_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a decreasing sequence of infinite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
. Assume by contradiction that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
is not Boolean independent. Then there exists
F,G
two disjoint finite subsets of
M
with
\max F<\min G
such that:

\bigcap_{i\in F}\bigcap_{j\in G}A_{i}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset

Suppose
F\subseteq\{n_{1},\cdots,n_{F}\}
and
G\subseteq\{n_{F}+1,\cdots,n_{G}\}
for some
n_{F},n_{G}\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
F_{M}=\{n_{1},\cdots,n_{F}\}
and
G_{M}=\{n_{F}+1,\cdots,n_{G}\}
. Consider the set
M_{n_{G}}
. Since
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{n_{G}}}
has no convergent sequences on
\bigcap_{i\in F_{M}}\bigcap_{j\in G_{M}}A_{i}\cap B_{j}
, there exists
y\in\bigcap_{i\in F_{M}}\bigcap_{j\in G_{M}}A_{i}\cap B_{j}
such that all of the following sets are infinite:

\{n\in M_{n_{G}}\,|\,y\in A_{n}\},\hskip 5.69046ptM_{n_{G}}\backslash\{n\in M_{n_{G}}\,|\,y\in A_{n}\},\hskip 5.69046pt\{n\in M_{n_{G}}\,|\,y\in B_{n}\},\hskip 5.69046ptM_{n_{G}}\backslash\{n\in M_{n_{G}}\,|\,y\in B_{n}\}

Hence:

y\in\bigcap_{i\in F_{M}}\bigcap_{j\in G_{M}}A_{i}\cap B_{j}\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in F}\bigcap_{j\in G}A_{i}\cap B_{j}=\emptyset

which is absurd.

∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a uniformly bounded sequence of real-valued scalar function defined on
S
. Fix
\delta,r>0
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
A_{n}=\{x\in S\,|\,f_{n}(x)>\delta+r\},B_{n}=\{x\in S\,|\,f_{n}(x)<r\}
. If there exists an infinite set
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M}
is Boolean Independent, then
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in M}
is equivalent, in the
\sup
norm, to the standard basis of the real
\ell_{1}
space.

Proof.

Without losing generality, we can let
\ell_{1}
be indexed by
M
since
M
is infinite. Then it suffices to show that for any
v
in the real
\ell_{1}
(indexed by
M
):

\frac{\delta}{2}\|v\|_{1}\leq\sup_{x\in S}\left|\,\sum_{i\geq 1}v_{i}f_{i}(x)\,\right|\leq\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|\|v\|_{1}

The second inequality is immediate. Now fix
v
in the real
\ell_{1}
space with
\|v\|_{1}=1
. Define
V_{+}=\{i\in M\,|\,v_{i}>0\}
and
V_{-}=\{i\in M\,|\,v_{i}<0\}
. If we use
\mathcal{F}_{+}
to denote the set of all finite subsets of
V_{+}
,
\mathcal{F}_{-}
to denote the set of all finite subsets of
V_{-}
, we then have:

\lim_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{+},G\in\mathcal{F}_{-}}\sum_{i\in F\cup G}|\,v_{i}\,|=\lim_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{+},G\in\mathcal{F}_{-}}\sum_{i\in F}v_{i}-\sum_{j\in G}v_{i}=\sum_{i\in M}|\,v_{i}\,|

Fix
F\in\mathcal{F}_{+}
,
G\in\mathcal{F}_{-}
. By assumption suppose
x\in\bigcap_{i\in F}\bigcap_{j\in G}A_{i}\cap B_{j}
. Define
G_{0}=\{j\in G\,|\,f_{j}(x)>0\}
. Then we have:

(17)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in G}v_{i}f_{i}(x)\geq\sum_{i\in G_{0}}v_{i}f_{i}(x)=\sum_{i\in G_{0}}|\,v_{i}\,|(-f_{i}(x))\geq\sum_{i\in G_{0}}(-r)|\,v_{i}\,|\geq\sum_{i\in G}(-r)|\,v_{i}\,|

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in F}v_{i}f_{i}(x)\geq\sum_{i\in F}v_{i}(\delta+r)

\displaystyle\implies\,\sum_{i\in F\cup G}v_{i}f_{i}(x)\geq\sum_{i\in F}v_{i}(\delta+r)+\sum_{i\in G}(-r)|\,v_{i}\,|

Again by assumption, suppose
y\in\bigcap_{j\in G}\bigcap_{i\in F}A_{j}\cap B_{i}
. Then we have:

(18)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in G}v_{i}f_{i}(y)=\sum_{i\in G}|\,v_{i}\,|(-f_{i}(y))\leq\sum_{i\in G}|\,v_{i}\,|(-(\delta+r))

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in F}v_{i}f_{i}(y)\leq r\sum_{i\in F}v_{i}

\displaystyle\implies\,-\sum_{i\in F\cup G}v_{i}f_{i}(y)\geq\sum_{i\in G}|\,v_{i}\,|(\delta+r)+\sum_{i\in F}v_{i}(-r)

Combining (
17
) and (
18
) gives us:

\sum_{i\in F\cup G}v_{i}f_{i}(x)+\left(-\sum_{i\in F\cup G}v_{i}f_{i}(y)\right)\geq\delta\sum_{i\in F\cup G}|\,v_{i}\,|

If one of two sums on the left hand side is non-positive, then the other must be greater than or equal to
\delta\sum_{i\in F\cup G}|\,v_{i}\,|
. If both sums on the left hand side are positive, then at least one must be greater than or equal to
\dfrac{\delta}{2}\sum_{i\in F\cup G}|\,v_{i}\,|
. In general, we have:

\sup_{x\in S}\left|\,\sum_{i\in M}v_{i}f_{i}\,\right|\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{i\in F\cup G}|\,v_{i}\,|

Since
F,G
are arbitrarily picked from
\mathcal{F}_{+}
and
\mathcal{F}_{-}
, we then can conclude:

\sup_{x\in S}\left|\,\sum_{i\in M}v_{i}f_{i}\,\right|\geq\lim_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{+},G\in\mathcal{F}_{-}}\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{i\in F\cup G}|\,v_{i}\,|=\frac{\delta}{2}

∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Given a sequence of uniformly bounded real-valued functions defined on
S
, say
\{f_{n}\}
, for each
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, define:

\delta(M)=\sup_{x\in S}[\limsup_{M}f_{m}(x)-\liminf_{M}f_{m}(x)]

Then there exists an infinite subset
Q\subseteq\mathbb{N}
so that
\delta(L)=\delta(Q)
for all infinite subsets
L\subseteq Q
.

Proof.

Given any two subsets
L,M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, if
L\subseteq M
, we will have
\delta(L)\leq\delta(M)
. When
\delta(\mathbb{N})=0,\mathbb{N}
will be the desired set in the conclusion so we from now on assume
\delta(\mathbb{N})>0
. Assume by contradiction any infinite subset
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
contain an infinite subset
L
so that
\delta(L)<\delta(M)
. Then by transfinite induction, we can choose
\{N_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha<\Omega\}
a transfinite family of subsets of
\mathbb{N}
. Such family is indexed by the set of ordinals less than
\Omega
, the first uncountable ordinal, and has the property that for any
\alpha<\beta<\Omega
,
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\alpha}
and
\delta(N_{\beta})<\delta(N_{\alpha})
. Hence we obtain
\{\delta(N_{\alpha})\,|\,\alpha<\Omega\}
a strictly decreasing transfinite family of positive real numbers, which will not exist since such family must be at most countable.

∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\{f_{i}\}
be a uniformly bounded sequence of real-valued functions defined on
S
. According to
Lemma
4.6
, let
Q\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be the set so that
\delta(Q)=\delta(N)
for every infinite subset
N\subseteq Q
and set
\lambda=\dfrac{\delta(Q)}{2}
. Show that there exists an infinite subset
M\subseteq Q
and a rational number
r
so that for every infinite subset
L\subseteq M
there is
s\in S
so that
\limsup_{i\in L}f_{i}(s)>\lambda+r
and
\liminf_{i\in L}f_{i}(s)<r

Proof.

Let
\{r_{1},r_{2},\cdots\}
be the enumeration of
\mathbb{Q}
and assume the conclusion is false. Then we start from
M=Q
and
r_{1}
. Then we can find
L_{1}\subseteq M
such that for each
s\in S
, the following is true for
r=r_{1}
:

(19)

\limsup_{i\in L_{1}}f_{i}(s)\leq\lambda+r\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{or}\hskip 28.45274pt\liminf_{i\in L_{1}}f_{i}(s)\geq r

By induction, we then have
\{L_{k}\}
a decreasing sequence of infinite subsets of
Q
such that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for each
s\in S
, the following is true for all
r\in\{r_{i}\}_{i\leq k}
:

\limsup_{i\in L_{k}}f_{i}(s)\leq\lambda+r\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{or}\hskip 28.45274pt\liminf_{i\in L_{k}}f_{i}(s)\geq r

Fix
s\in S
. For each
k\in L_{k}
, pick
n_{k}\in L_{k}
such that
f_{n_{k}}(s)\leq\lambda+r_{k}
and
f_{n_{k}}(s)\geq r_{k}
. Define
L=\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Since
L_{0}\subseteq L_{k}
for each
k
, we then have:

\limsup_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s)\leq\limsup_{i\in L_{k}}f_{i}(s)\leq\lambda+r_{k}\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{or}\hskip 28.45274pt\liminf_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s)\geq\liminf_{i\in L_{k}}f_{i}(s)\geq r_{k}

for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
s
is arbitrarily picked, we then have for each
s\in S
and each
r\in\mathbb{Q}
:

(20)

\limsup_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s)\leq\lambda+r\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{or}\hskip 28.45274pt\liminf_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s)\geq r

Since
\delta(L_{0})=\delta(Q)=2\lambda
, if we let
\epsilon=\dfrac{\lambda}{2}
, we then can find
s_{0}\in S
such that
\limsup_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s_{0})-\liminf_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s_{0})>2\lambda-\epsilon
. Set
a=\limsup_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s_{0})
and
b=\liminf_{i\in L_{0}}f_{i}(s_{0})
. Hence
a>2\lambda-\epsilon+b>b
. Now find
r_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
r-b\in\left(0,\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\right)
. Thus:

b<r_{0}<r_{0}+\lambda=(r_{0}-b)+(\lambda+b)<\frac{\lambda}{2}+\lambda+b=2\lambda-\epsilon+b<a

which implies that
a>\lambda+r_{0}
and
b<r_{0}
, but this contradicts our assumption.

∎

Definition 4.8
.

A bounded sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in a Banach space
(B,\|\cdot\|)
is
equivalent to the standard basis of

\ell^{1}
if there exists
\delta>0
such that for any finite choice of scalars
(c_{i})_{i\leq n}
(depending on if
B
is an
\mathbb{R}
- or
\mathbb{C}
-vector space):

\delta\sum_{i\leq n}|\,c_{i}\,|\leq\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}c_{i}x_{i}\right\|

In this case, if we let
(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the standard basis of
\ell^{1}
, the mapping
x_{n}\mapsto e_{n}
can be linearly and continuously extended to be a linear isomorphism between the closure of span of
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\ell^{1}
.

Theorem 4.9

(Rosenthal’s Condition)
.

Let
S
be an infinite set and
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a uniformly bounded sequence of real-valued functions defined on
S
. Then there exists
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in M}
a subsequence of
\{f_{n}\}
that satisfies precisely one of the following alternatives:

1)

\{f_{n}\}_{n\in M}
converges pointwise

2)

\{f_{n}\}_{n\in M}
is equivalent to the standard basis of a real
\ell_{1}
space

Proof.

Suppose
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in M}
is a subsequence that does not converge pointwise. Hence
\delta(M)>0
where
\delta
is defined in
Lemma
4.6
. Then by
Lemma
4.7
, there exists
M_{0}\subseteq M
,
r\in\mathbb{Q}
and
x\in S
such that
\limsup_{i\in M_{0}}f_{i}(x)>\delta(M)+r
and
\liminf_{i\in M_{0}}f_{i}(s)<\delta(M)
. For each
i\in M_{0}
, define
A_{i}=\{s\in S\,|\,f_{i}(s)>\delta(M)+r\}
and
B_{i}=\{s\in S\,|\,f_{i}(s)<r\}
. Because of
x
, the following two sets are infinite:

\{i\in M_{0}\,|\,f_{i}(x)>\delta(M)+r\},\hskip 28.45274pt\{i\in M_{0}\,|\,f_{i}(x)<r\}

which implies all of the following sets are infinite:

\{i\in M_{0}\,|\,f_{i}(x)>\delta(M)+r\},\hskip 5.69046ptM_{0}\backslash\{i\in M_{0}\,|\,f_{i}(x)>\delta(M)+r\},\hskip 5.69046pt\{i\in M_{0}\,|\,f_{i}(x)<r\},\hskip 5.69046ptM_{0}\backslash\{i\in M_{0}\,|\,f_{i}(x)<r\}

Therefore
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{0}}
does not converge on
S
. By
Lemma
4.4
, there exists
M_{1}\subseteq M_{0}
such that
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n\in M_{1}}
is Boolean independent. Hence by
Proposition
4.5
,
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in M_{1}}
is equivalent to the standard basis of a real
\ell_{1}
space.

∎

Theorem 4.10

(Rosenthal-Dor
\ell_{1}
Theorem)
.

In a real Banach space
B
any bounded sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
will have a subsequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in M}
(for some infinite subset
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
) that satisfy one of the following two mutually exclusive conditions:

1)

\{x_{n}\}_{n\in M}
is weakly Cauchy.

2)

\{x_{n}\}_{n\in M}
is equivalent to the standard basis of a real
\ell_{1}
space.

Proof.

Fix a bounded sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq B
and let
Q
be the canonical mapping from
B
to
B^{\ast\ast}
. Since
B
is a real Banach space,
B^{*}
is the space of all real linear bounded functional and same for
B^{\ast\ast}
. Then for any infinite
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
,
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in M}
being weakly Cauchy is equivalent that
\{Q(x_{n})\}_{n\in M}
is pointwise Cauchy, or pointwise convergent. Hence the conclusion follows immediately by
Theorem
4.9
.

∎

Theorem 4.11
.

Given a Banach space
B
, if
B
is weakly complete, then
B
is either reflexive or contains a subspace that is linearly isomorphic to
\ell^{1}
. If weak convergence in
B
is equivalent to norm convergence (the
Schur property
), then every infinite-dimensional subspace of
B
contains a subspace that is isomorphic to
\ell^{1}
.

Proof.

If
B
is reflexive, then all subspaces of
B
are also reflexive. Hence if
B
contains a subspace isomorphic to the real
\ell^{1}
space, then
B
cannot be reflexive. Otherwise, given that
B
is weakly complete, by
Theorem 4.10
, we have that every bounded sequence has a weakly convergence subsequence. Then by
Theorem
3.10
, the closed unit ball of
B
is weakly compact. Together with
[
Gol38
, Goldstine’s Theorem]
,
B
is reflexive.

If
B
has the Schur property, then by
Theorem
4.10
, every bounded sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
either contains a convergent subsequence or a subsequence that is equivalent to the standard basis of
\ell^{1}
. If
V\leq B
is an infinite-dimensional subspace, then the closed unit ball of
V
cannot be compact and hence must contains a
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that has a subsequence that is equivalent to the standard basis of
\ell^{1}
.

∎

4.2.
Existence of isomorph of
\ell_{1}
or
c_{0}

Proposition 4.12
.

Given a Banach space
B
and a subspace
V\leq B
that is isomorphic to
\ell_{1}
, there will be an
f\in B^{*}
such that
f
does not attain its
\sup
on the unit sphere of
B

Proof.

Suppose
\{x_{n}\}
is a countable basis of
V
that is isomorphic to the standard basis in
\ell_{1}
and define
f_{n}\in V^{*}
such that:

f_{n}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}1,\hskip 31.2982pti\geq n\\
-1,\hskip 22.76228pti<n\end{cases}

WLOG assume each
f_{n}
is already continuously extended to the entire
V
. Now define
f
on
V
by
f(x)=\lim_{n}f_{n}(x)
so that for each
x=\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{n}x_{n},f(x)=-\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{n}
. Extend
f
to the entire
B
by letting
f
equal to zero outside of
V
. Define:

g(x)=-f(x)+\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{2^{i}}f_{i}(x)

Then for each
x\in V_{\leq 1},|\,g(x)\,|\leq 2\|x\|
and we also have:

g(x_{n})=-f(e_{n})+\sum_{i\leq n}\frac{1}{2^{i}}-\sum_{i>n}\frac{1}{2^{i}}=1-\frac{1}{2^{n}}-\frac{1}{2^{n}}+1=2-\frac{1}{2^{n-1}}

Hence
\|g\|=2
. Assume
\|v\|=1
and
g(v)=2
. Because each
|\,f_{k}(v)\,|\leq 1
we must have
f_{k}(v)=1
in order to let
g(v)=2
and hence
f(v)=1\,\implies\,g(v)=0
. Contradiction

∎

Theorem 4.13
.

Suppose a Banach space
B
contains a isomorph of
c_{0}
(i.e., a closed subspace of
B
is linearly isomorphic to
c_{0}
). Then
B^{*}
contains a isomoph of
\ell_{1}

Proof.

Let
M
is a closed subspace isomorphic to
c_{0}
and
\Phi
be the linear isomorphism. By
Inverse Mapping Theorem
both
\Phi
and
\Phi^{-1}
are bounded. Let
\{x_{n}\}
be the pre-image of the standard basis of
c_{0}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the following linear functional on
M
:

f_{n}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}1,\hskip 28.45274pti=n\\
0,\hskip 28.45274pti\neq n\end{cases}

Next, define:

V=\left\{\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}f_{i}:\sum_{i\geq 1}|\,\alpha_{i}\,|<\infty\right\}

Let
\{e_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the standard basis of
c_{0}
. Then we have for each
N\in\mathbb{N}

\left\|\sum_{n\leq N}x_{n}\right\|=\left\|\Phi^{-1}\left(\sum_{n\leq N}e_{i}\right)\right\|\leq\left\|\Phi^{-1}\right\|\left\|\sum_{n\leq N}e_{n}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|\Phi^{-1}\right\|

Hence for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, fix
\{\alpha_{i}\}
a absolutely summable sequence and let
\lambda_{i}
be the sign of
\alpha_{i}
. Then:

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}f_{i}\right\|\geq\left|\,\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}f_{i}\left(\frac{1}{\|\Phi^{-1}\|}\sum_{n\leq N}\lambda_{n}x_{n}\right)\,\right|=\frac{1}{\|\Phi^{-1}\|}\sum_{i\leq N}\big|\,\alpha_{i}\,\big|

\displaystyle\implies\,\frac{1}{\|\Phi^{-1}\|}\sum_{i\geq 1}\big|\,\alpha_{i}\,\big|\leq\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}f_{i}\right\|\leq\sum_{i\geq 1}\big|\,\alpha_{i}\,\big|

Then we can conclude that
\overline{V}
is an isomorph of
\ell_{1}
.

∎

4.3.
Boundedly complete basis and shrinking basis

Theorem 4.14

(Banach’s Condition)
.

In a Banach space
B
, given a linearly independent sequence
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Schauder basis of a (closed) subspace iff there is
K\geq 1
such that:

K\left\|\sum_{i\leq n+p}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|\geq\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|

for every
n,p\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{a_{i}\}_{i\leq n+p}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

(\Longrightarrow):
Given
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a Schauder basis of a closed subspace
V
, define:

P_{n}:V\rightarrow V,\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}z_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}z_{i}

Since
\{z_{i}\}
is a Schauder basis, each
P_{n}
is continuous. For each
x\in V
and each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\|P_{n}x\|=\left\|\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}z_{i}\right)-\sum_{i>n}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|\leq\|x\|+\left\|\sum_{i>n}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|\overset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\|x\|

Hence, for each
x\in V
,
\sup_{n}\|P_{n}x\|<\infty
. By
Uniform Boundedness Theorem
, we have
K=\sup_{n}\|P_{n}\|<\infty
. Given
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m<n
, we then have:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq m}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|=\left\|P_{m}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}z_{i}\right)\right\|\leq K\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|

Notice that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\|P_{n}\left(\frac{z_{1}}{\|z_{1}\|}\right)\right\|=\left\|\frac{z_{1}}{\|z_{1}\|}\right\|\leq\sup_{n}\|P_{n}\|=K

Hence we have
K\geq 1
.

(\Longleftarrow):
Consider
S=\operatorname{Span}(\{z_{i}\})
. Clearly each element in
S
is a finite linear combination of elements from
(z_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
but without confusion we write each element in
S
as an infinite sum
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a_{i}z_{i}
where only finitely many
(a_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are non-zero. Next, define:

P_{n}:S\rightarrow S,\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}z_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}z_{i}

According to the assumption, given
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m>n
, we have:

\left\|P_{n}\left(\sum_{i\leq m}a_{i}z_{i}\right)\right\|\leq K\left\|\sum_{i\leq m}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|

Hence, we have that for each
s\in S
,
\|P_{n}(s)\|\leq K\|s\|
and hence each
P_{n}
can continuously extended to
\overline{S}
. Suppose
x\in\overline{S}
and
\{s_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq S
converges to
x
. We then have:

\|x-P_{n}(x)\|\leq\|x-s_{k}\|+\|s_{k}-P_{n}(s_{k})\|+\|P_{n}(s_{k})-P_{n}(x)\|

Since
P_{n}
is continuous on
\overline{S}
and, for each
s\in S
,
P_{n}(s)
is eventually equal to
s
, we then can conclude that
P_{n}(x)\rightarrow x
and hence
\{z_{i}\}
is a Schauder basis of
\overline{S}
.

∎

Corollary 4.15
.

In the set-up of
Theorem
4.14
,
(z_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an unconditional Schauder basis of a subspace if and only if there exists
K\geq 1
such that:

K\left\|\sum_{i\in A}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|\geq\left\|\sum_{i\in B}a_{i}z_{i}\right\|

for all finite subsets
B\subseteq A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
(a_{i})_{i\in A}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Put
S=\operatorname{Span}\big(\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big)
. For each finite subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, define:

P_{A}:S\rightarrow S,\hskip 12.51927pt\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}z_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i\in A}a_{i}z_{i}

Similar to the reasoning in the proof of
Theorem
4.14
,
(z_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an unconditional Schauder basis of
\overline{S}
if and only if the set of operators
\big(P_{A}:A\subseteq\mathbb{N},|\,A\,|<\infty\big)
is uniformly bounded.

∎

Given a Schauder basis
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a
(f_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq B^{*}
such that
f_{m}(x_{n})=\delta_{m,n}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
[
Jam50
, Theorem 3]
provides necessary and sufficient condition for when
(f_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a basis of
B^{*}
. We will introduce this result later as it contains terminologies we only need in later subsections.

Definition 4.16

(
[
Sin62
]
)
.

Given
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a Schauder basis of a Banach space
B
,
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is said to be
boundedly complete
if for any sequence of complex numbers
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
:

\sup_{n}\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|<\infty\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}x_{i}\textbf{ converges}

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
V_{n}=\operatorname{Span}\{x_{i}\}_{i\geq n}
. For each
f\in X^{*}
, let:

\|f\|_{n}=\sup_{n\in V_{n}}\frac{|\,f(x)\,|}{\|x\|}

Then
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is said to be
shrinking
if for each
f\in B^{*}
,
\lim_{n}\|f\|_{n}=0
.

Remark 4.17
.

Let
X,Y
be two Banach spaces and
\Phi:X\rightarrow Y
is a linear isomorphism. According to the
Inverse Mapping Theorem
and
Theorem
4.14
, when
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X
is a Schauder basis of
X
,
\big\{\Phi(x_{i})\big\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Schauder basis of
Y
. Therefore the boundedly-completeness of a Schauder basis can be preserved by a linear isomorphism. Notice that for each
x\in X
and
f\in X^{*}
, we have:

\big|\,f\big[\Phi(x)\big]\,\big|\leq\|\Phi\|\big|\,f(x)\,\big|

Hence
\big\{\Phi(x_{i})\big\}
is shrinking whenever
\{x_{i}\}
is shrinking.

Theorem 4.18

(
[
Jam50
, Theorem 1]
)
.

A Banach space
B
with a Schauder basis
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is reflexive iff
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is both bounded complete and shrinking.

Proof.

Suppose
B
is reflexive. Define
u_{n}=\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}
and suppose
\sup_{n}\|u_{n}\|\leq 1
. By reflexivity, without losing generality, assume
u_{n}
(or a subsequence of
\{u_{n}\}
) converges to
u\in B_{\leq 1}
weakly. Define
f_{n}
to be the
n
-th coordinate coefficient of each element in
B
. Since
\{x_{i}\}
is a Schauder basis, each
f_{n}
is continuous. Suppose
u=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}x_{i}
. Then:

\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pta_{i}=\lim_{n}f_{i}(u_{n})=f_{i}(u)=\alpha_{i}\hskip 28.45274pt\implies\hskip 28.45274ptu=\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}x_{i}

Hence when
B
is reflexive,
\{x_{i}\}
is boundedly complete. Now suppose the basis is not shrinking when
B
is reflexive. Then we can find
\epsilon>0
,
g\in B^{*}
, an increasing sequence of integers
\{p_{n}\}
and a sequence of
\{v_{n}\}\subseteq B_{\leq 1}
such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

v_{n}=\sum_{i\geq p_{n}}\alpha(n,i)x_{i}

and
g(v_{n})>\epsilon
. By reflexivity
\{v_{n}\}
(or a subsequence of
\{v_{n}\}
) has a weak limit
v\in B_{\leq 1}
. Notice that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lim_{n}f_{i}(v_{n})=0
. If
v\neq 0
, then
\lim_{n}f_{i}(v_{n})=f_{i}(v)=0
, which is absurd. If
v=0
, then
\epsilon<\lim_{n}g(v_{n})=g(v)=0
, which is also absurd. Hence when
B
is reflexive the basis is shrinking.

Conversely, suppose the
\{x_{i}\}
is both boundedly complete and shrinking. By
Theorem 4.12
, there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1]
such that for each
x=\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}x_{i}
and
n,p\in\mathbb{N}
:

(21)

\displaystyle\|x\|\geq\epsilon\left\|\sum_{i<n}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|\hskip 8.5359pt\textbf{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\|x\|\geq\epsilon\left\|\sum_{i\leq n+p}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle\implies\,2\|x\|\geq\epsilon\left\|\sum_{i\leq n+p}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|+\left\|\sum_{i<n}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|\geq\epsilon\left\|\sum_{i\leq n+p}a_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{i<n}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|\sum_{n\leq i\leq n+p}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\|x\|

Now fix a sequence
\{y_{n}\}\subseteq B_{\leq 1}
. Assume
y_{n}=\sum_{i\geq 1}a(n,i)x_{i}
. By (
21
), we have for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
:

\sup_{n}\|a(n,i)x_{i}\|\leq\sup_{n}\frac{2}{\epsilon}\|y_{n}\|=\frac{2}{\epsilon}\hskip 28.45274pt\implies\hskip 28.45274pt\sup_{n}|\,a(n,i)\,|\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon\|x_{i}\|}

Hence for each
i
, let
\alpha_{i}
be a clustered point of
\{a(n,i)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. By diagonal method, we can assume for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lim_{n}a(n,i)=\alpha_{i}
. Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. By (
21
), we have:

\lim_{n}\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}a(n,i)x_{i}\right\|\leq\frac{\|y_{n}\|}{\epsilon}\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\hskip 28.45274pt\implies\hskip 28.45274pt\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}

Since the basis is bounded complete,
y=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}x_{i}
exists and it remains to show that
y_{n}
converges to
y
weakly. Fix
f\in B^{*}_{\leq 1}
,
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|f\|_{M}<\dfrac{\epsilon\gamma}{8}
. Find
M_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
so that for all
n\geq M_{1}
:

\left\|\sum_{i<M}(\alpha_{i}-a(n,i)x_{i}\right\|<\frac{\gamma}{2\|f\|}

By (
21
), we have:

\lim_{p}\left\|\sum_{M\leq i\leq M+p}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|=\left\|\sum_{i\geq M}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\|y\|=\frac{2}{\epsilon}

and

\left\|\sum_{i\geq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}

Therefore, for any
n\geq\max(M_{1},M)
:

|\,f(y-y_{n})\,|=\left|\,f\left(\sum_{i<M}[\alpha_{i}-a(n,i)]x_{i}\right)\,\right|+\left|\,f\left[\sum_{i\geq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}-\sum_{i\geq M}a(n,i)x_{i}\right]\,\right|\leq\frac{\gamma}{2\|f\|}\|f\|+\|f\|_{M}(\frac{2}{\epsilon}+\frac{2}{\epsilon})=\gamma

Hence
\lim_{n}f(y_{n})=f(y)
.

∎

4.4.
Boundedly complete basis and isomorph of
c_{0}

Definition 4.19
.

In a Banach space
B
, a Schauder basis
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is
unconditional
if for any
x\in B
, the series form of
x
:

x=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{i}x_{i}

converges unconditionally.

Proposition 4.20

(
[
Jam50
, Lemma 1]
)
.

In
B
a Banach space with a unconditional Schauder basis
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is not boundedly complete iff
B
contains an isomorph of
c_{0}
.

Proof.

For each finite subset
F\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, define:

P_{F}:B\rightarrow B,\hskip 8.5359ptx=\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}x_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i\in F}a_{i}x_{i}

Because
\{x_{i}\}
is a unconditional Schauder basis, similar to (
21
),there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1]
such that for each finite subset
F\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\big\|P_{F}x\big\|\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\|x\|

For each
x\in X
, define:

\|x\|_{1}=\sup_{F\subseteq\mathbb{N},|\,F\,|<\infty}\big\|P_{F}x\big\|

Obviously
\|\cdot\|_{1}
is a well-defined norm in
B
and we also have:

\|x\|\leq\sup_{n}\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq\|x\|_{1}\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\|x\|

which implies
\|\cdot\|
is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{1}
.

(\Longrightarrow):
First suppose
\{x_{i}\}
is not boundedly complete and
\left\{\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded sequence that does not converge. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
define
s_{n}=\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}x_{i}
and
M=\sup_{n}\|s_{n}\|
. Since
\{s_{n}\}
does not converge with respect to
\|\cdot\|
, there exists
\delta>0
such that:

\limsup_{n,m}\|s_{n}-s_{m}\|>\delta

Then there exists a strictly increasing sequence
\{n_{i}\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
\|s_{n_{i+2}}-s_{n_{i+1}}\|>\delta
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
i
, define
z_{i}=s_{n_{i+2}}-s_{n_{i+1}}
and:

V=\overline{\operatorname{Span}(\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}})}^{\|\cdot\|_{1}}

Notice that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any
G\subseteq\{1,2,\cdots,N\}
:

\left\|\sum_{i\in G}z_{i}\right\|_{1}\leq\|s_{n_{N+2}}\|_{1}\leq\frac{2M}{\epsilon}

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. First fix
k\in\{1,2,\cdots,N\}
, a finite set
F\subseteq\{1,2,\cdots,N\}\backslash\{k\}
,
\theta_{k}\in[0,1]
and
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\in F}
a set of complex numbers indexed by
F
. Then we have:

\left\|\theta_{k}z_{k}+\sum_{i\in F}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|\leq(1-\theta_{k})\left\|\sum_{i\in F}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\theta_{k}\left\|z_{k}+\sum_{i\in F}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}

Therefore the following function defined on
[0,1]
:

g_{k}:[0,1]\rightarrow(0,\infty),\hskip 14.22636ptr\mapsto\left\|rz_{k}+\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}

is a convex function (concave up). Because
g(0)\leq g(1)
, we have
g(\theta)\leq g(1)
for each
\theta\in[0,1]
. Similarly, with
z_{k}
replaced by
-z_{k}
, the following function:

g_{-k}:[0,1]\rightarrow(0,\infty),\hskip 14.22636ptr\mapsto\left\|r(-z_{k})+\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}

is also convex. Hence, for each
r\in[-1,0]
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\|rz_{k}+\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}=\left\|(-r)(-z_{k})+\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\left(\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right)-z_{k}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|\left(\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right)\right\|_{1}+\|z_{k}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\frac{2M}{\epsilon}\leq\left\|z_{k}+\sum_{i\leq n,i\neq k}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\frac{2M}{\epsilon}

Next fix
i,j\in\{1,2,\cdots,N\}
and
\theta_{i},\theta_{j}\in(0,1)
. Fix
G
a finite subset in
\{1,2,\cdots,N\}\backslash\{i,j\}
and
\{\beta_{k}\}_{k\in F}
a finite set of complex numbers indexed by
F
. Then:

\displaystyle\left\|\theta_{i}z_{i}+\theta_{j}z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\theta_{i})(1-\theta_{j})\left\|\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}+(1-\theta_{i})\theta_{j}\left\|z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle+\theta_{i}(1-\theta_{j})\left\|z_{i}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}+\theta_{i}\theta_{j}\left\|z_{i}+z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}

Therefore the following function defined on
[0,1]\times[0,1]
:

h_{i,j}:[0,1]\times[0,1]\rightarrow(0,\infty),\hskip 14.22636pt(r_{1},r_{2})\mapsto\left\|r_{1}z_{i}+r_{2}z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}

is bi-convex. Since all of
h_{i,j}(0,0),h_{i,j}(0,1)
and
h_{i,j}(1,0)
are less than or equal to
h(1,1)
, we then have for each
r_{1},r_{2}\in[0,1]
,
h_{i,j}(r_{1},r_{2})\leq h(1,1)
. Similar to the previous case, given
r_{1},r_{2}\in[-1,1]
, if
r_{1}\leq 0,r_{2}\geq 0
, then:

(22)

\displaystyle\left\|r_{1}z_{i}+r_{2}z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|\left(z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right)-z_{i}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}+\|z_{i}\|_{1}\leq\left\|z_{i}+z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}+\frac{2M}{\epsilon}

If both
r_{1},r_{2}
are non-positive, then:

(23)

\displaystyle\left\|r_{1}z_{i}+r_{2}z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|\left(\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right)-z_{i}-z_{j}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}+\|z_{i}+z_{j}\|_{1}\leq\left\|z_{i}+z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}+\frac{2M}{\epsilon}

Combining (
22
) and (
23
) gives us:

\forall\,(r_{1},r_{2})\in[-1,1]\times[-1,1],\hskip 8.5359pt\left\|r_{1}z_{i}+r_{2}z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|z_{i}+z_{j}+\sum_{k\in F}\beta_{k}z_{k}\right\|_{1}+\frac{2M}{\epsilon}

By induction, we then can conclude:

(24)

\forall\,(\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\cdots,\theta_{N})\in[-1,1]^{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\theta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\frac{2M}{\epsilon}\leq\frac{4M}{\epsilon}

Since in
(V,\|\cdot\|_{1})
, the sequence
\{z_{i}\}
satisfies the condition in
Theorem
4.14
(with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{1}
), then
\{z_{i}\}
is a Schauder basis of
V
. Pick
v\in V
and assume
v=\sum_{i\geq 1}\beta_{i}z_{i}
. By our assumption, we have
\|z_{i}\|\in(\delta,2M)
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\|\beta_{i}z_{i}\|\leq\|v\|_{1}
. Hence
\beta=\sup_{n}|\,\beta_{n}\,|<\infty
. Define:

\displaystyle S_{1,+}=\{i\in\mathbb{N},i\leq N\,|\,\operatorname{Re}\beta_{i}\geq 0\},\hskip 28.45274ptS_{1,-}=\{i\in\mathbb{N},i\leq N\,|\,\operatorname{Re}\beta_{i}<0\}

\displaystyle S_{2,+}=\{i\in\mathbb{N},i\leq N\,|\,\operatorname{Im}\beta_{i}\geq 0\},\hskip 28.45274ptS_{2,-}=\{i\in\mathbb{N},i\leq N\,|\,\operatorname{Im}\beta_{i}<0\}

and hence by (
24
):

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\frac{\beta_{i}}{\beta}z_{i}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\beta_{i}}{\beta}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\frac{\operatorname{Im}\beta_{i}}{\beta}z_{i}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{i\in S_{1,+}}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\beta_{i}}{\beta}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\sum_{i\in S_{1,-}}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\beta_{i}}{\beta}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\sum_{i\in S_{2,+}}\frac{\operatorname{Im}\beta_{i}}{\beta}z_{i}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\sum_{i\in S_{2,-}}\frac{\operatorname{Im}\beta_{i}}{\beta}z_{i}\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{8M}{\epsilon}

Since
N
is arbitrarily fixed, now we can conclude that:

\|v\|_{1}=\lim_{N}\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}\leq\beta\frac{8M}{\epsilon}

Since for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|v\|_{1}\geq\|\beta_{i}z_{i}\|>\delta|\,\beta_{i}\,|
, now we can conclude:

(25)

\delta\sup_{n}|\,\beta_{i}\,|<\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{8M}{\epsilon}\sup_{n}|\,\beta_{i}\,|

Now define:

F:V\rightarrow\ell_{\infty},v=\sum_{i\geq 1}\beta_{i}z_{i}\rightarrow(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots)

By (
25
)
F(V)
is closed in
\ell_{\infty}
. Meanwhile for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\sup_{n\geq N}|\,\beta_{i}\,|<\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|\sum_{i\geq N}\beta_{i}z_{i}\right\|_{1}\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Hence
F(V)\subseteq c_{0}
. On the other hand, given
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in c_{0}
, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a finite subset
G\subseteq\{N,N+1,\cdots\}
, again by (
25
):

\Big\|\sum_{i\in G}\alpha_{i}z_{i}\Big\|_{1}\leq\frac{8M}{\epsilon}\sup_{n\geq N}|\,\alpha_{i}\,|\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\left\{\sum_{i\leq N}\alpha_{i}z_{i}\right\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\textbf{ converges in }\|\cdot\|_{1}

Hence
F(V)=c_{0}
. Since
(V,\|\cdot\|_{1})
is linearly isomorphic to
(V,\|\cdot\|)
, we can now conclude
B
contains an isomorph of
c_{0}
.

(\Longleftarrow):
Conversely, suppose
S
is a closed subspace of
B
, which is linearly isomorphic to
c_{0}
through
\Phi:S\rightarrow c_{0}
. Then by
Inverse Mapping Theorem
both
\Phi
and
\Phi^{-1}
are bounded. Let
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq S
be the pre-image of
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
the standard basis of
c_{0}
. Then for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}s_{i}\right\|=\left\|\Phi^{-1}\left(\sum_{i\leq N}e_{i}\right)\right\|\leq\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}e_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\|\Phi^{-1}\|=\|\Phi^{-1}\|

Meanwhile, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
F
a finite subset of
\{N,N+1,\cdots\}
, we have:

1=\left\|\sum_{i\in F}e_{i}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|\Phi\left(\sum_{i\in F}s_{i}\right)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|\sum_{i\in F}s_{i}\right\|\|\Phi\|\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\left\|\sum_{i\in F}s_{i}\right\|\geq\frac{1}{\|\Phi\|}.

which implies that the sequence
\{\sum_{i\leq N}s_{i}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
does not converge in
\|\cdot\|_{1}
, nor in
\|\cdot\|
. Thus
\{s_{i}\}
is not boundedly complete, which implies that
\{z_{i}\}
is not either.

∎

4.5.
Shrinking basis and isomorph of
\ell_{1}

Proposition 4.21

(
[
Jam50
, Lemma 2]
)
.

Let
B
be a Banach space with a unconditional Schauder basis
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is not shrinking iff
B
contains an isomorphs of
\ell_{1}
.

Proof.

(\Longrightarrow):
Assume by contradiction that there is
f\in B^{*}
such that
\lim_{n}\|f\|_{n}>0
. WLOG assume
\lim_{n}\|f\|_{n}=1
. For each
n
define
V_{n}=\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq n}
. Hence there exists a strictly increasing sequence of integers
\{n_{i}\}
,
\{y_{i}\}\subseteq B
where for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
y_{i}\in V_{i}
and
\|y_{i}\|=1
, such that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
f(y_{i})>\dfrac{1}{2}
. Next define
Y_{0}=\operatorname{Span}(\{y_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}})
and in
Y
define:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\alpha_{i}y_{i}\right\|_{1}=\sum_{i\leq N}|\,\alpha_{i}\,|

Clearly
\|\cdot\|_{1}
defines a norm in
Y_{0}
and for each
y\in Y_{0}
,
\|y\|\leq\|y\|_{1}
. Let
Y
be the completion of
Y_{0}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and we have
Y\subseteq\overline{Y_{0}}^{\|\cdot\|}
. According to
Theorem
4.14
, we have
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Schauder basis of
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{1})
. Next, since in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
1
-norm and
2
-norm are equivalent, there exists
0<r\leq R
such that for any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(26)

r\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}}\leq|\,\alpha\,|+|\,\beta\,|\leq R\sqrt{\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}}

Since
\{y_{i}\}
is a Schauder basis of
Y
, each
y\in Y
can be uniquely written as
y=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}y_{i}
. Fix
y=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}y_{i}
in
Y
and define:

\displaystyle S_{1,+}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,\operatorname{Re}\alpha_{i}\geq 0\},\hskip 28.45274ptS_{1,-}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,\operatorname{Re}\alpha_{i}<0\}

\displaystyle S_{2,+}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}\geq 0\},\hskip 28.45274ptS_{2,-}=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\,|\,\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}<0\}

Next, define:

y_{1,+}=\sum_{i\in S_{1,+}}\operatorname{Re}(\alpha_{i})y_{i}\hskip 28.45274pty_{2,+}=\sum_{i\in S_{2,+}}\operatorname{Im}(\alpha_{i})y_{i}\hskip 28.45274pty_{1,-}=\sum_{i\in S_{1,-}}\operatorname{Re}(\alpha_{i})y_{i}\hskip 28.45274pty_{2,-}=\sum_{i\in S_{2,-}}\operatorname{Im}(\alpha_{i})y_{i}

Since all these four points converges with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{1}
, they also converge with respect to
\|\cdot\|
. Since each
y_{i}\in V_{i}
and
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a unconditional basis, according to
Theorem
4.14
, there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1]
such that:

(27)

\max\big(\|y_{1,+}\|,\|y_{1,-}\|,\|y_{2,+}\|,\|y_{2,-}\|\big)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\|y\|

By assumption, we have
f(y_{i})>\dfrac{1}{2}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore:

(28)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in S_{1,+}}\operatorname{Re}(\alpha_{i})<f(y_{1,+})\leq\|f\|\|y_{1,+}\|\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in S_{1,-}}-\operatorname{Re}(\alpha_{i})<-f(y_{1,-})=|\,f(y_{1,-})\,|\leq\|f\|\|y_{1,-}\|

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in S_{2,+}}\operatorname{Im}(\alpha_{i})<f(y_{2,+})\leq\|f\|\|y_{2,+}\|\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in S_{2,-}}-\operatorname{Im}(\alpha_{i})<-f(y_{2,-})=|\,f(y_{2,-})\,|\leq\|f\|\|y_{2,-}\|

Hence combining (
26
), (
27
) and (
28
) gives us:

\displaystyle\|y\|_{1}=\sum_{i\geq 1}|\,\alpha_{i}\,|=\sum_{i\geq 1}\sqrt{\big(\operatorname{Re}\alpha_{i}\big)^{2}+\big(\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}\big)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\geq 1}\big(|\,\operatorname{Re}\alpha_{i}\,|+|\,\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}\,|\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\geq 1}|\,\operatorname{Re}\alpha_{i}\,|+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\geq 1}|\,\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}\,|=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\in S_{1,+}}\operatorname{Re}\alpha_{i}-\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\in S_{1,-}}\operatorname{Re}\alpha_{i}+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\in S_{2,+}}\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}-\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i\in S_{2,-}}\operatorname{Im}\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\|f\|}{r}\big(\|y_{1,+}\|+\|y_{1,-}\|+\|y_{2,+}\|+\|y_{2,-}\|\big)\leq\frac{8\|f\|}{r\epsilon}\|y\|

Since
y
is arbitrarily picked from
Y
, we then can conclude for each
y\in Y
:

\|y\|\leq\|y\|_{1}\leq\frac{8\|f\|}{r\epsilon}\|y\|.

Hence
\|\cdot\|
is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{1}
, or
Y
is linearly isomorphic to
\overline{Y_{0}}^{\|\cdot\|}
. Since
Y
is obviously linear isomorphic to
\ell_{1}
,
\overline{Y_{0}}^{\|\cdot\|}
is linearly isomorphic to
\ell_{1}
.

(\Longleftarrow):
Conversely, suppose
S
, a closed subspace of
B
, is linearly isomorphic to
B
and
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is the pre-image of the standard basis of
\ell_{1}
. By
Remark
4.17

\{s_{i}\}
is a Schauder basis of
S
. Since there exists
F\in B^{*}
such that
F(s_{i})=1
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and zero else, we could conclude that
\{s_{i}\}
is not shrinking so that
\{z_{i}\}
is not either.

∎

Theorem 4.22

(
[
Jam50
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Given a Banach space
B
with a unconditional Schauder basis
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
B
is non-reflexive iff
B
contains an isomorph of
c_{0}
or
\ell_{1}
.

Proof.

Immediate by
Theorem
4.18
,
Proposition
4.20
and
Proposition
4.21
.
∎

4.6.
Bases in the dual space

Proposition 4.23
.

A Banach space
B
is reflexive iff
B^{*}
is reflexive

Proof.

When
B
is reflexive, then obviously
B^{*}=(B^{\ast\ast})^{*}=(B^{*})^{\ast\ast}
. Now when
B^{*}
is reflexive, we have
B^{\ast\ast}
is reflexive. Hence the canonical embedding of
X
into
X^{\ast\ast}
as a closed subspace of
X^{\ast\ast}
is also reflexive, which implies that
X
is reflexive.
∎

Corollary 4.24

(
[
Jam50
]
)
.

Let
B
be a Banach space with a unconditional basis. If
B^{\ast\ast}
is separable, then
B
is reflexive.

Proof.

By
Theorem 4.
4.22
, it suffices to show that
B
contains no isomorphs of
c_{0}
or
\ell_{1}
. If
B
contains an isomorph of
c_{0}
, by
Theorem
4.13
,
B^{*}
contains an isomorph of
\ell_{1}
and suppose
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is the pre-image of the standard basis of
\ell_{1}
. Then for each subset
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, there exists
f_{M}\in B^{\ast\ast}
such that
f(e_{i})=1
for each
i\in M
and zero elsewhere. Then
\{f_{M}\}_{M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})}
is a non-separable subset, which contradicts our assumption. When
B
contains an isomorph of
\ell_{1}
, similarly we can define
\{f_{M}\}_{M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})}\subseteq B^{*}
as above. Then for each
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, there exists
F_{M}\in B^{\ast\ast}
such that for each
N\subseteq\mathbb{N}
:

F_{M}(f_{N})=\begin{cases}1,\hskip 28.45274ptN\subseteq M\\
0,\hskip 28.45274ptN\backslash M\neq\emptyset\end{cases}

and
F_{M}
is zero outside of
\operatorname{Span}(\{f_{M}\}_{M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})})
. Thus
\{F_{M}\}_{M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})}
is another non-separable set in
B^{\ast\ast}
, which is absurd.

∎

Theorem 4.25

(
[
Jam50
, Theorem 3]
)
.

Let
B
be a Banach space with a Schauder basis
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
f_{n}
be the
n
-th coordinate coefficient linear functional. Then:

a)

If
\{x_{i}\}
is unconditional and shrinking, then
\{f_{n}\}
is a unconditional Schauder basis for
B^{*}
.

b)

\{f_{n}\}
is a Schauder basis of
B^{*}
iff
\{x_{i}\}
is shrinking.

c)

\{f_{n}\}
is boundedly complete if it is a Schauder basis of
B^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1]
be the constant provided by
Theorem
4.14
such that for any
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
with
N<M
:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|\geq\epsilon\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|.

a)

Fix
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
f\in B^{*}
and find
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|f\|_{N}<\dfrac{\gamma}{1+K}
. Now let
\{x_{p_{i}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a permutation of
\{x_{i}\}
. Then there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p_{n}\geq N
iff
n>M
. Since
\{x_{i}\}
is assumed unconditional, fix
x\in X
with
\|x\|=1
and we have:

x=\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}x_{i}=\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{p_{i}}x_{i}

Hence, for each
n\geq M
:

\displaystyle\left|\,f(x)-\sum_{i\leq n}a_{p_{i}}f_{p_{i}}(x)\,\right|=\left|\,f\left(\sum_{i>n}a_{p_{i}}x_{i}\right)\,\right|\leq\|f\|_{N}\left\|\sum_{i>n}a_{p_{i}}x_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{N}\left(\|x\|+\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{p_{i}}x_{i}\right\|\right)\leq\|f\|_{N}(\|x\|+K\|x\|)<\gamma

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|f-\sum_{i\leq n}a_{p_{i}}f_{p_{i}}\right\|<\gamma

since
x
is arbitrarily fixed.

b)

(\Longrightarrow):
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
V_{n}=\operatorname{Span}\{x_{i}\}_{i\geq n}
. Fix
f\in B^{*}
and suppose
f=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}f_{i}
. Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
v_{k}\in V_{N}
with
\|v_{k}\|=1
such that:

\|f\|_{N}-\frac{1}{k}\leq f(v_{k})=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}f_{i}(v_{k})=\sum_{i\geq N}\alpha_{i}f_{i}(v_{k})\leq\left\|\sum_{i\geq N}\alpha_{i}f_{i}\right\|\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Hence
\{x_{i}\}
is shrinking.

(\Longleftarrow):
The proof to this direction is a special case of the proof to
a)
where the permutation is the identity.

c)

Fix a sequence of complex numbers
\{a_{i}\}
such that
R=\sup_{n}\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}f_{i}\|<\infty
. Then we want to show
\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}f_{i}
converges. Now fix
x=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}x_{i}
,
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
with
N<M
. Then:

(29)

\displaystyle\left|\,\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}a_{i}f_{i}(x)\,\right|=\left|\,\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}a_{i}f_{i}\left(\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right)\,\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}a_{i}f_{i}\right\|\,\left\|\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|<2R\left\|\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|

Since
\{x_{i}\}
is a Schauder basis, according to the proof of
Proposition
4.20
, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{1}
(defined in the proof of
Proposition
4.20
) is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|
. Hence we have:

\left|\,\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}a_{i}f_{i}(x)\,\right|<2R\left\|\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq 2R\left\|\sum_{i\geq N}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|_{1}\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Hence
\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}f_{i}(x)
converges for any
x\in B
. Since
\{x_{i}\}
is unconditional. by (
21
) (
29
) and the equivalence between
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|
, we have for each
M\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left|\,\sum_{i\leq M}a_{i}f_{i}(x)\,\right|<2R\left\|\sum_{i\leq M}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\right\|\leq 2R\|x\|_{1}\leq\frac{4R}{\epsilon}\|x\|\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\left|\,\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}f_{i}(x)\,\right|\leq\frac{4R}{\epsilon}\|x\|

Since
x
is arbitrarily fixed, we then can conclude
\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}f_{i}\in B^{*}
and by definition of each
f_{i}
,
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
uniquely corresponds to
\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}f_{i}
.

∎

Theorem 4.26

(
[
Sin62
, Proposition 5]
)
.

In the set-up of
Theorem
4.25
,
(f_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is shrinking if and only if
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is boundedly complete;
(f_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is boundedly complete if and only if
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is shrinking.

Proof.

The second part follows immediately by
Theorem
4.25
. For the first part, we first let
Q
denote the canonical mapping from
B
to
B^{\ast\ast}
. Then again apply
Theorem
4.25
to the bi-orthogonal system
(f_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\big(Q(x_{n})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

∎

5.
Characterizations by bi-orthogonal System

In this section, we will study results mainly from
[
Pta59
]
, which are about a linearly independent sequence
(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a sequence of linear functionals
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{n}}
where
f_{n}
is defined to output the
n
-th coefficient of each element in
\operatorname{Span}(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. The idea of proofs in this section seem to be similar to the one in
Theorem
3.17
or results in
Section 2
, but is not based on a certain affine set but the boundedness of
(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Throughout this section, given a Banach space
X
, we will use
Q
to denote the canonical embedding from
X
to
X^{\ast\ast}
, and
Q^{\prime}
to denote the canonical embedding from
X^{*}
to
X^{\ast\ast\ast}
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
E
be a Banach space. A
bi-orthogonal System
is a sequence
\{e_{n}\}\subseteq B
and
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq B^{*}
so that for each
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
:

f_{i}(e_{j})=\begin{cases}1,\hskip 28.45274pti=j\\
0,\hskip 28.45274pti\neq j\end{cases}

A bi-orthogonal System is bounded if both sequences are bounded in their own norms. In this section, whenever we are given
S=\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
a bi-orthogonal system, we define:

A(S)=\overline{\operatorname{Span}\{e_{i}\}}^{\|\cdot\|},\hskip 28.45274ptB_{1}(S)=\overline{\operatorname{Span}\{f_{i}\}}^{\|\cdot\|},\hskip 28.45274ptB(S)=\overline{\operatorname{Span}\{f_{i}\}}^{w\ast}

Proposition 5.2
.

Given
X
a non-reflexive Banach space,
Q
the canonical mapping from
X
to
X^{\ast\ast},r\in X^{\ast\ast}\backslash Q(X)
with
\|r\|\leq 1
and
\sigma>0
, show that for every finite sequence
\{f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}
and any
\sigma\in(0,1)
, there exists
b\in X
with
\|b\|\leq 1+\sigma
such that
f_{i}(b)=r(f_{i})
for each
i\leq n
.

Proof.

Assume that
\{f_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq X^{*}
is a finite set such that for any
b\in X_{\leq 1}
, there exists
i\leq n
with
f_{i}(b)\neq r(f_{i})
. Then in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
define:

W=\big\{(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x),\cdots,f_{n}(x)\,|\,x\in X_{\leq 1}\big\}

Obviously
W
contains an open neighborhood of zero. Now let
z=(r(f_{1}),r(f_{2}),\cdots,r(f_{n}))
. By assumption and the fact that
\overline{W}
is compact,
z\notin\overline{W}
. Since
\overline{W}
compact we can find a bounded linear functional that strictly separates
\overline{W}
and
z_{0}
. Namely we can find
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that:

\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}r(f_{i})>1\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{
and }\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\,(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\overline{W},\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\leq 1

Therefore
\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}f_{i}\right\|\leq 1
and hence:

1<r\left(\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}f_{i}\right)\leq\|r\|\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}f_{i}\right\|\leq 1

which is absurd. Hence for any
\{f_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq X^{*}
and
\sigma\in(0,1)
:

(r(f_{1}),\cdots,r(f_{n}))\in\overline{\{(f_{1}(x),\cdots,f_{n}(x))\,|\,x\in X_{\leq 1}\big\}}\subseteq(1+\sigma)\big\{(f_{1}(x),\cdots,f_{n}(x))\,|\,x\in X_{\leq 1}\big\}

or

(r(f_{1}),\cdots,r(f_{n}))\in\big\{(f_{1}(x),\cdots,f_{n}(x))\,|\,x\in X_{\leq 1+\sigma}\big\}

∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Any non-reflexive Banach space
X
will have a bounded bi-orthogonal system
(e_{i},f_{j})_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\sup_{n}\|\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\|<\infty
or
\sup_{n}\|\sum_{i\leq n}f_{i}\|<\infty

Proof.

Let
r\in X^{\ast\ast}\backslash Q(X)
and suppose
H
is an arbitrary finite dimensional subspace of
X
. Define:

\rho=\sup\big\{|\,r(y)\,|:y\in X^{*}_{=1}\cap H^{\perp}\big\}.

Suppose
H=\operatorname{Span}\{h_{1},h_{2},\cdots,h_{n}\}
and
H^{*}=\operatorname{Span}\{f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\}
where
f_{i}(h_{j})=1
iff
i=j
. If
\rho=0
, then
r\in(H^{\perp})^{\perp}
. Observe that
Q(H)^{\perp}=Q^{\prime}(H^{\perp})
and
(H^{\perp})^{\perp}\subseteq Q^{\prime}(H^{\perp})_{\perp}
. Then
r\in\big[Q(H)^{\perp}\big]_{\perp}=Q(H)\subseteq Q(X)
, which is absurd. Hence
\rho>0
. Below we will start constructing the desired bi-orthogonal system.

Suppose
\|r\|=1
. Fix
\sigma\in(0,1)
.
y_{1}\in X^{*}_{=1}
so that
\beta_{1}=r(y_{1})>\frac{1}{2}
. By
Proposition
5.2
we can find
b_{1}\in X_{\leq 1+\sigma}
so that
y_{1}(b_{1})=\beta_{1}
. Define
E_{1}=\operatorname{Span}\{b_{1}\}
and:

\rho_{1}=\sup\big\{r(y)\,|\,y\in X^{*}_{=1}\cap E_{1}^{\perp}\big\}

By the previous remark, we have
\rho_{1}>0
. Next find
y_{2}\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}\cap E_{1}^{\perp}
so that
\beta_{2}=r(y_{2})>\frac{1}{2}\rho_{1}
. Again by
Proposition
5.2
, find
b_{2}\in X_{\leq 1+\sigma}
so that
y_{1}(b_{2})=\beta_{1},y_{2}(b_{2})=\beta_{2}
. Define
E_{2}=\operatorname{Span}\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
and:

\rho_{2}=\sup\big\{r(y)\,|\,y\in X^{*}_{=1}\cap E_{2}^{\perp}\big\}

and similarly
\rho_{2}>0
. By induction for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we will have
\{b_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\subseteq X_{\leq 1+\sigma}
,
E_{n}=\operatorname{Span}\{b_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
,
y_{i+1}\in X^{\ast}_{\leq 1}\cap E_{i}^{\perp}
,
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
and:

\rho_{n}=\sup\big\{r(y)\,|\,y\in X^{\ast}_{=1}\cap E_{n}^{\perp}\big\}

such that
\beta_{1}=r(y_{1})>\dfrac{1}{2}
,
\beta_{n+1}=r(y_{n+1})\in\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\rho_{n},\rho_{n}\right]
and for each
i,j\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
:

y_{i}(b_{j})=\begin{cases}r(y_{i}),\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq i\leq j\leq n\\
0,\hskip 30.15985pt1\leq j<i\leq n\end{cases}

Since
H_{n}^{\perp}
is decreasing as
n
increases, we have
\{\rho_{n}\}
is non-increasing sequence in
(0,1]
and hence convergent. Assume that
\inf_{n}\rho_{n}=\lim_{n}\rho_{n}=0
. For each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and then suppose
\rho_{n}<\epsilon
for all
n\geq N
. Fix
f\in X^{\ast}_{\leq 1}
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\tau_{n}(f)=\max_{i\leq n}|\,f(b_{i})\,|
and:

z_{n}(f)=\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}f(b_{1})y_{1}+\sum_{1<i\leq n}\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}\big[f(b_{i})-f(b_{i-1})\big]y_{i}

Since for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

0<\frac{1}{2}\rho_{n+1}<\beta_{n}\leq\rho_{n}

we then have
\|z_{n}(f)\|\leq\dfrac{4\tau_{n}(f)}{\rho_{n}}

and
r\big[z_{n}(f)\big]=f(b_{n})
. Hence:

\left|\,\rho_{n}r\big[f-z_{n}(f)\big]\,\right|\leq\rho_{n}\|f-z_{n}(f)\|\leq\left[1+\dfrac{4n\tau_{n}(f)}{\rho_{n}}\right]\rho_{n}=\rho_{n}+4n\tau_{n}(f)

Hence, for each
n\geq N
, whenever
\tau_{n}(f)<\dfrac{\epsilon}{4n}
, we then have:

\big|\,\rho_{n}r(f)\,\big|\leq\Big|\,\rho_{n}r\big[z_{n}(f)\big]\,\Big|+\rho_{n}+4n\tau_{n}(f)<\rho_{n}\epsilon+\rho_{n}+\epsilon<3\epsilon

Hence:

(30)

\Big\{f\in X^{*}:\max_{i\leq N}|\,f(b_{i})\,|<\epsilon\Big\}\subseteq\Big\{f\in X^{*}:|\,r(f)\,|<\epsilon\Big\}

Since we assume that
\lim_{n}\rho_{n}=0
, for each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, we can then find
N\in\mathbb{N}
so that (
30
) is true, which implies that
r
is continuous with respect to the weak topology (on
X^{*}
) generated by
Q(X)
. Since
Q(X)
separates points on
X^{*}
, by
Theorem
3.15
we have
r\in Q(X)
and obtain a contradiction. Hence there exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that
\inf_{n}\rho_{n}>\beta
and
\beta_{i}>\beta
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Next, define
e_{1}=b_{1}
and for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
with
i>1
:

e_{i}=b_{i}-b_{i-1}.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, define
f_{i}=\dfrac{1}{\beta}y_{i}
. Hence for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|e_{i}\|\leq 2(1+\sigma)
,
\|f_{i}\|\leq\frac{1}{\beta}
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\right\|=\|b_{n}\|\leq 1+\sigma

Then
\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded bi-orthogonal system where
\sup_{n}\big\|\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\big\|<\infty
. On the other hand, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, define:

h_{i}=\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}y_{i}-\frac{1}{\beta_{i+1}}y_{i+1}.

Hence for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|h_{i}\|\leq\dfrac{2}{\beta}
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}h_{i}\right\|=\left\|\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}y_{1}-\frac{1}{\beta_{n+1}}y_{n+1}\right\|\leq\frac{2}{\beta}.

In this case we have
\{(b_{i},h_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
a bounded bi-orthogonal system where
\sup_{n}\big\|\sum_{i\leq n}h_{i}\big\|<\infty
.

∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Given a doubly indexed bounded sequence
\{a_{m,n}\}_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, define
c_{1,n}=a_{1,n}
and for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m>1
, define
c_{m,n}=a_{m,n}-a_{m-1,n}
. If, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{a_{m,n}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is monotonely increasing and, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{c_{m,n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is also monotonely increasing, then:

\lim_{m}\lim_{n}a_{m,n}=\lim_{n}\lim_{m}a_{m,n}

Proof.

By assumption and
Monotone Convergence Theorem
:

\lim_{n}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}c_{m,n}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\lim_{n}c_{m,n}

Hence:

\lim_{n}\lim_{m}a_{m,n}=\lim_{n}\lim_{m}\sum_{i\leq m}c_{m,n}=\lim_{m}\sum_{i\leq m}\lim_{n}c_{m,n}=\lim_{m}\lim_{n}\sum_{i\leq m}c_{m,n}=\lim_{m}\lim_{n}a_{m,n}

∎

Theorem 5.5
.

A Banach space
E
is reflexive iff for every bounded bi-orthogonal system
\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\big\{\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\big\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is not uniformly bounded and
\big\{\sum_{j\leq n}f_{j}\big\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is not uniformly bounded.

Proof.

One direction is proved by
Proposition
5.3
. When
E
is reflexive, assume
\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bi-orthogonal system where
\sup_{n}\|\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\|<\infty
. Therefore
\big\{\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\big\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(or restricted to a subsequence) will converge weakly to a point, say
e
. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f_{n}(e)=\lim_{k}f_{n}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}e_{i}\big)=1
. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lim_{n}f_{n}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}e_{i}\big)=0
. However, the doubly indexed sequence
\Big\{f_{n}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}e_{i}\big)\Big\}_{n,k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the condition in
Proposition
5.4
and hence:

1=\lim_{n}\lim_{k}f_{n}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}e_{i}\big)=\lim_{k}\lim_{n}f_{n}\big(\sum_{i\leq k}e_{i}\big)=0

which is absurd. On the other hand, when
E
is reflexive,
E^{*}
is also reflexive by
Proposition
4.23
. Therefore if
\sup_{n}\big\|\sum_{i\leq n}f_{i}\big\|<\infty
, the sequence
\{\sum_{i\leq n}f_{i}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(or a subsequence) converges weakly to a point, say
f
, which will lead to a contradiction similar to above.

∎

Theorem 5.6
.

Given a Banach space
E
, the following are equivalent:

1)

E
is non-reflexive.

2)

There exists a bounded bi-orthogonal system
\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\omega>0
so that for every strictly decreasing sequence
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,\infty)
with
\lim_{i}\alpha_{i}=0
, we have
\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}
converges and
\Big\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\Big\|\leq\omega\alpha_{1}
.

3)

There exists a bounded bi-orthogonal system
S=\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\gamma>0
such that for every summable sequence
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,\infty)
:

\inf\left\{\left\|x-\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\right\|:x\in B(S)_{\perp}\right\}\geq\gamma\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}

Proof.

•

1)\Longrightarrow 2):
Suppose
E
is non-reflexive. Then according to the proof of
Proposition
5.3
, for an arbitrary
\sigma\in(0,1)
, we will have
\{b_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}\subseteq X_{\leq 1+\sigma}
,
\{y_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
,
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}\subseteq(0,\infty)
such that
\inf_{n}\beta_{n}=\beta>0
and:

y_{i}(b_{j})=\begin{cases}\beta_{i},\hskip 22.76228pt1\leq i\leq j\leq n\\
0,\hskip 26.46085pt1\leq j<i\leq n\end{cases}

Define
e_{1}=b_{1}
and for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
with
i>1
, define
e_{i}=b_{i}-b_{i-1}
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, define
f_{i}=\dfrac{1}{\beta_{i}}y_{i}
. Then
\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded bi-orthogonal system. Given a strictly decreasing sequence
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,1)
with
\lim_{i}\alpha_{i}=0
, for each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m>n
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{n<i\leq m}\alpha_{i}e_{i}=\alpha_{n+1}(b_{n+1}-b_{n})+\cdots+\alpha_{m}(b_{m}-b_{m-1})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{m}b_{m}+(\alpha_{m-1}-\alpha_{m})b_{m-1}+\cdots+(\alpha_{n+1}-\alpha_{n+2})b_{n+1}-\alpha_{n+1}b_{n}

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|\sum_{n<i\leq m}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\right\|=\left\|\alpha_{m}b_{m}-\alpha_{n+1}b_{n+1}+\sum_{n<i<m}\big(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\big)b_{i}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\sigma)\left(\alpha_{m}+\alpha_{n+1}+\sum_{n<i<m}\big(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{i+1}\big)\right)=2(1+\sigma)\alpha_{n+1}\overset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Hence, we have:

\lim_{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\alpha_{i}e_{i}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\left\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\right\|\leq(1+\sigma)\alpha_{1}

•

1)\Longrightarrow 3):
Let
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a summable sequence and for an arbitrary
\sigma\in(0,1)
, let
\{b_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
\{y_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be given in the proof of
Proposition
5.3
such that
\inf_{n}\beta_{n}=\beta>0
and:

y_{i}(b_{j})=\begin{cases}\beta_{i},\hskip 22.76228pt1\leq i\leq j\leq n\\
0,\hskip 26.46085pt1\leq j<i\leq n\end{cases}

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
define:

h_{i}=\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}f_{i}-\frac{1}{\beta_{i+1}}f_{i+1}.

Again by the proof of
Proposition
5.3
,
\mathcal{S}=\{(b_{i},h_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded bi-orthogonal system and
\sup_{n}\Big\|\sum_{i\leq n}h_{i}\Big\|\leq\dfrac{2}{\beta}
. Then for any
z\in B(\mathcal{S})_{\perp}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

\left\|z+\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}b_{i}\right\|\geq\left|\,\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{j\leq n}h_{j}\left(z+\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}b_{i}\right)\,\right|=\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}.

Hence:

\displaystyle\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}\leq\inf\left\{\left\|x-\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\right\|:x\in B(S)_{\perp}\right\}

\displaystyle\implies\,\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}\leq\inf\left\{\left\|x-\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\right\|:x\in B(S)_{\perp}\right\}

•

2)\Longrightarrow 1):
Suppose
S=\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded bi-orthogonal system and
\omega>0
such that for any sequence
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,\infty)
with
\lim_{i}\alpha_{i}=0
, we have
\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}
converges and
\Big\|\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}e_{i}\Big\|\leq\omega\alpha_{1}
. Hence:

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\right\|\leq\omega\hskip 28.45274pt\implies\hskip 28.45274pt\sup_{n}\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}e_{i}\right\|\leq\omega

which implies that
E
is non-reflexive by
Theorem
5.5
.

•

3)\Longleftarrow 1):
Let
S=\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
be the bounded bi-orthogonal system and
\gamma>0
be given such that for any summable sequence of positive real numbers
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
:

\inf\left\{\left\|x-\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\right\|:x\in B(S)_{\perp}\right\}\geq\gamma\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}

Define
M=\sup_{j}\|f_{j}\|
. Without losing generality (or replace
\gamma
by a smaller positive real number), suppose
\gamma\in(0,\dfrac{1}{M})
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define:

V_{n}=\overline{\operatorname{Span}\big(B(S)_{\perp},\{e_{i}\}_{i>n}\big)}^{\|\cdot\|}

and

V_{0}=\overline{\operatorname{Span}\big(B(S)_{\perp},\{e_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}\big)}^{\|\cdot\|}

In the subspace
V_{0}
, for each different pair
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\|e_{i}-e_{j}\|=\sup\Big\{\big|\,f(e_{i}-e_{j})\,\big|:f\in V_{0}^{*},\|f\|\leq 1\Big\}\geq\frac{1}{M}\big|\,f_{i}(e_{i}-e_{j})\,\big|>\gamma

which implies that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\leq n
:

\inf_{v\in V_{n}}\|e_{i}-v\|>\gamma

By
Hahn-Banach Theorem
, when
n=1
, there exists
g_{1}\in V_{0}^{*}
such that
\|g_{1}\|=1
,
g_{1}\Big|_{V_{1}}=0
and
g_{1}(e_{1})>\gamma
. By induction assume that when
n=k
, there exists
g_{k}\in V_{0}^{*}
such that
g_{k}\Big|_{V_{k}}=0
and
g_{k}(e_{i})=0
for each
i\leq k
. Hence for any
i\leq k+1
, all of the following hold:

\left|\,\left(f_{k+1}\Big|_{V_{0}}g_{k}\right)(e_{i})\,\right|>\gamma,\hskip 14.22636pt\left\|f_{k+1}\Big|_{V_{0}}+g_{k}\right\|=1,\hskip 14.22636pt\left(g_{k}+f_{k+1}\Big|_{V_{0}^{*}}\right)\Big|_{V_{k+1}}=0

Hence, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
g_{k}\in V_{0}^{*}
such that
g_{k}(e_{i})>\gamma
for each
i\leq k
,
\|g_{k}\|=1
and
g_{k}\Big|_{V_{k}}=0
. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\overline{g_{k}}
be the norm-preserving extension of
g_{k}
to the entire
E
. Hence
\{\overline{g_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\big[B(S)_{\perp}\big]^{\perp}=B(S)
since
B(S)
is weak-
\ast
closed. Since
\{\overline{g_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded and, there exists
g\in B(S)
such that
\{\overline{g_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(or a proper subsequence) that converges to
g
in weak-
\ast
topology. Then we have
g(e_{k})>\gamma
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume by contradiction that
g\in B_{1}(S)
and
\{F_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\operatorname{Span}\{f_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
g
in norm. By diagonal method to find a proper subsequence, we can assume for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Big\{\big|\,F_{m}(e_{n})\,\big|\Big\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is increasing. Since, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence
\Big\{\big|\,F_{m+1}(e_{n})\,\big|\,-\big|\,F_{m}(e_{n})\,\big|\Big\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually equal to zero, the sequence
\Big\{\big|\,F_{m}(e_{n})\,\big|\Big\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the condition in
Proposition
5.4
and hence we have:

\lim_{n}\lim_{m}\big|\,F_{m}(e_{n})\,\big|=\lim_{m}\lim_{n}\big|\,F_{m}(e_{n})\,\big|=0

Since
\{F_{m}\}
also converges to
g
pointwise and
g(e_{k})>\gamma
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we then have:

0=\lim_{n}\lim_{m}\big|\,F_{m}(e_{n})\,\big|=\lim_{n}|\,g(e_{n})\,|>\gamma

which is absurd. Hence
g\notin B_{1}(S)
. If
E
is reflexive, so is
E^{*}
by
Proposition
4.23
and hence so is
B_{1}(S)
. In this case,
B(S)
is equal to the weak closure of
\operatorname{Span}\{f_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, which is again equal to
B_{1}(S)
. However, we then have
g\in B(S)\backslash B_{1}(S)
and obtain a contradiction. We can now conclude
E
is not reflexive.

∎

6.
Characterizations by weakly compact subsets

The main theorems in this section are from
[
Jam64a
]
and
[
Jam64b
]
. In the source, the main focus is on a complete
locally convex topological vector space
(in short LCTVS) and
E\subseteq X
a bounded weakly closed subset, and a list of conditions on
E
, which are equivalent to that
X
is reflexive, will be given. By
Remark
2.8
, it suffices to only consider the case where
X
is a Banach space. As it is mentioned in
Section 3
,
X
being reflexive is equivalent to the closed unit ball of
X
being weakly compact, which is an immediate consequence of
[
Gol38
, Goldstine’s Theorem]
. Also, given
S
a weakly closed convex subset of
X
, the first two theorems from
[
Jam64b
]
state precisely when
S
is weakly compact, which generalize the
Proposition
4.12
.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
Jam64b
, Theorem 5]
)
.

A weakly closed subset
S\subseteq X
in a Banach space is weakly compact if and only if every element in
X^{*}
attains its norm on
S
.

Several precise descriptions are given in
[
Jam64b
]
, and then later summarized by main theorems in
[
Jam64a
]
. Here we start from the first main theorem in
[
Jam64a
]

Definition 6.2
.

Given a vector space
V
, a sequence of points
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in\operatorname{conv}\{v_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
u_{1},u_{2}
are said to be
non-overlapping
if thee exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
u_{1}\in\operatorname{conv}\{v_{i}\}_{i\leq N}
and
u_{2}\in\operatorname{conv}\{v_{i}\}_{i>N}
. For each
v\in\operatorname{conv}\{v_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, define:

l(v)=\min\big\{n\in\mathbb{N}:v\notin\operatorname{conv}\{v_{i}\}_{i>n}\big\}

Theorem 6.3

(
[
Jam64b
, Theorem 1]
)
.

A bounded closed convex subset
C
of a Banach space is not weakly compact iff there exists
\mu,\sigma>0
such that for any
\delta,\Delta>0
with
\delta<\mu<\Delta
, there exists a sequence of elements
\{z_{i}\}\subseteq C
such that both of the following hold:

1)

\forall\,\xi\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
,
\|\xi\|\in(\delta,\Delta)

2)

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}\big]\geq\sigma

Proof.

•

\Longleftarrow):
Let
\{z_{i}\}\subseteq C,\delta,\Delta,\sigma,\mu
be chosen so that conditions
1),2)
are satisfied. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
K_{n}=\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq n}
. Assume by contradiction
C
is weakly compact. Hence by weakly compactness and
Proposition
3.16
,
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{K_{n}}\neq\emptyset
. Pick
w\in\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{K_{n}}
. Then we can find two non-overlapping points
\xi,\gamma\in K_{1}
such that
\|w-\xi\|<\dfrac{\sigma}{2}
and
\|w-\gamma\|<\dfrac{\sigma}{2}
. Hence
\|\xi-\gamma\|<\sigma
, which contradicts
2)
.

•

\Longrightarrow):
Assume
C
is not weakly compact. By
Proposition
3.16
, we can find a strictly decreasing sequence of closed convex subsets of
C
, say
\{T_{n}\}
, such that
\bigcap_{n\geq 1}T_{n}=\emptyset
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t_{n}\in T_{n}\backslash T_{n+1}
. Then we want to assume by contradiction that: for any
\mu,\sigma>0
there exists
\delta,\Delta>0
with
\delta<\mu<\Delta
, so that for any sequence
\{z_{i}\}\subseteq C
, there exists
\xi\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
so that
\|\xi\|\geq\Delta
or
\|\xi\|\leq\delta
, or there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
so that

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>k}\big]<\sigma

Since for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
T_{n+1}\subseteq T_{n}
, we then have:

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{c\in T_{N}}\|c\|=\lim_{N}\inf_{c\in T_{N}}\|c\|

If the limit above is equal to
0
, we then have
\inf_{c\in T_{N}}\|c\|=0
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies that
0\in T_{N}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
since each
T_{N}
is closed. However, this contradicts our assumption that
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}T_{n}=\emptyset
. Hence we can conclude there exists
\mu>0
such that starting from some
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\inf_{c\in T_{n}}\|c\|>\mu
for all
n\geq N_{1}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
define
V_{\geq n}=\operatorname{conv}\{t_{i}\}_{i\geq n}
. Next we claim:

(31)

\exists\,t_{1}^{1}\in V_{\geq 1}\textbf{ such that }\forall\,n\geq l(t_{1}^{1}),\hskip 8.5359ptd\big[t_{1}^{1},V_{\geq n}\big]\leq\frac{1}{2}

Assume that the claim above is false. Then first let
z_{1}=t_{1}
and then we can find
n_{1}\geq l(t_{1})
such that
d\big[t_{1},V_{\geq n_{1}}\big]>\dfrac{1}{2}
. Next let
z_{2}=t_{n_{1}}
and then find
n_{2}\geq\max\big(l(z_{1}),l(z_{2})\big)
such that both
d\big[z_{1},V_{\geq n_{2}}\big]
and
d\big[z_{2},V_{\geq n_{2}}\big]
are greater than or equal to
\dfrac{1}{2}
. For each
z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
, again find
n(z)
such that
d\big[z,V_{\geq n(z)}\big]>\dfrac{1}{2}
. Since, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the following function is continuous in
X
:

d_{N}:C\rightarrow(0,\infty),\hskip 8.5359ptx\mapsto d\big[x,V_{\geq N}\big]

Hence:

\operatorname{conv}\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{1},z_{2}\}}d_{n(z)}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2},\infty\right)

Since
\operatorname{conv}\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
is compact, then there exists
N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d\big[z,V_{\geq N_{2}}\big]>\frac{1}{2}
for each
z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
. By induction, we can find
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{t_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
so that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k>1
:

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq k},V_{\geq N_{k}}\big]>\frac{1}{2}

Recall that
\inf_{c\in T_{n}}\|c\|>\mu
for all
n\geq N_{1}
. Without losing generality, assume
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{t_{i}\}_{i\geq N_{1}}
(or remove finitely many points) and
\{N_{i}\}
is strictly increasing. For each
\delta,\Delta
with
\mu\in(\delta,\Delta)
and each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, pick
z_{n_{j}}\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq N_{j}}
such that
\|z_{n_{j}}\|\in(\delta,\Delta)
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
K\in\mathbb{N}
with:

\displaystyle\{z_{n_{j}}\}_{j\leq k}\subseteq\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq K},\hskip 28.45274pt\{z_{n_{j}}\}_{j>k}\subseteq V_{\geq N_{K}}

\displaystyle\implies\,d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n_{j}}\}_{j\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n_{j}}\}_{j>k}\big]\geq d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq K},V_{\geq N_{k}}\big]>\frac{1}{2}

which contradicts our assumption. Hence let
t_{1}^{1}\in V_{\geq 1}
such that for all
n\geq l(t_{1}^{1})
,
d\big[t_{1}^{1},V_{\geq n}\big]<\dfrac{1}{2}
. Let
n_{1}=\max(N_{1},l(t_{1}^{1})
,
\mu_{1}=\inf_{c\in T_{n_{1}}}\|c\|
,
\sigma_{1}=\dfrac{1}{2}
. Let
\{t_{n}^{1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V_{\geq n_{1}}
such that
\|t_{1}^{1}-t_{n}^{1}\|<\sigma_{1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\delta_{1},\Delta_{1}
be given by our assumption. Without losing generality (or restrict to a subsequence) suppose
\|t_{n}^{1}\|\in(\delta_{1},\Delta_{1})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq T_{n_{1}}
, for each
\xi\in\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{1}\}
,
\|\xi\|\in[\mu_{1},\Delta_{1})
. Hence by our assumption there exists
m_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

(32)

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{1}\}_{n\leq m_{1}},\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{1}\}_{n>m_{1}}\big]<\sigma_{1}

For our next claim:

(33)

\exists\,t_{1}^{2}\in\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{1}\}_{n>m_{1}}\textbf{ such that }\forall\,n>\max(l(t_{1}^{2}),m_{1}),\hskip 8.5359ptd\big[t_{1}^{2},V_{\geq n}\big]\leq\frac{1}{2^{2}}

similar to the how we prove (
31
), the claim above is true. Let
n_{2}=\max(m_{1},l(t_{1}^{2}))
,
\sigma_{2}=\dfrac{1}{2^{2}}
and
\mu_{2}=\inf_{c\in T_{n_{2}}}\|c\|
. Then there exists
\{t_{n}^{2}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{t_{n}^{1}\}_{n>m_{1}}
such that, with
\delta_{2},\Delta_{2}
given by our assumption and
\mu_{2}\in(\delta_{2},\Delta_{2})
,
\|t_{n}^{2}\|\in(\delta_{2},\Delta_{2})
and
\|t_{n}^{2}-t_{1}^{2}\|<\sigma_{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Also since
\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{2}\}\subseteq T_{n_{2}}
, by our assumption there exists
m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{2}\}_{n\leq m_{2}},\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{2}\}_{n>m_{2}}\big]<\sigma_{2}

Since
\{t_{n}^{2}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{t_{n}^{1}\}_{n>m_{1}}
, we have
\|t_{m_{1}}^{1}-t_{m_{2}}^{2}\|<\sigma_{1}
By induction, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\{t_{n}^{k}\}
,
m_{k}
and
\sigma_{k}=\dfrac{1}{2^{k}}
such that:

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{k}\}_{n\leq m_{k}},\operatorname{conv}\{t_{n}^{k}\}_{n>m_{k}}\big]<\sigma_{k}

and for each
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\leq j
, we have
\|t_{m_{i}}^{i}-t_{m_{j}}^{j}\|<\sigma_{i}
. Hence
\{t_{m_{i}}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
T_{n_{1}}
and suppose
t_{m_{i}}^{i}\rightarrow t
. Since for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
M_{K}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\{t_{m_{i}}^{i}\}_{i\geq k}\subseteq T_{M_{K}}
, by the assumption that
\{T_{n}\}
is strictly decreasing:

t\in\bigcap_{K\in\mathbb{N}}T_{M_{K}}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}T_{n}=\emptyset

which is absurd.

∎

Theorem 6.4

(
[
Jam64a
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and
E
a bounded weakly closed subset. Then the following statements are equivalent:

1)

E
is weakly compact

2)

E
is weakly countably compact

3)

For each sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
, we can find
x\in E
such that
\liminf f(x_{n})\leq f(x)\leq\limsup f(x_{n})\,\forall\,f\in X^{*}

4)

If
\{K_{n}\}
is a decreasing sequence of closed convex sets and
E\cap K_{n}\neq\emptyset\,\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
E\cap\bigcap_{n}K_{n}\neq\emptyset

5)

For each sequence of
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
and bounded sequence of
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}
we have:

\lim_{n}\lim_{k}f_{n}(x_{k})=\lim_{k}\lim_{n}f_{n}(x_{k})

whenever these limits exists

6)

For each sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
and bounded sequence of
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}
we have:

\inf\{f_{n}(x_{k})\,|\,n<k\}\leq\sup\{f_{n}(x_{k})\,|\,n>k\}

7)

For each sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
,
0\in\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n}-\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>n}]}

8)

For each sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
,
0\in\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq 1}[\operatorname{Span}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n}-\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>n}]}

9)

There will
NOT
exist
\theta>0
, a sequence
\{z_{n}\}\subseteq E
and a bounded sequence
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}
such that

g_{n}(x_{k})\begin{cases}>\theta,\hskip 28.45274ptn\leq k\\
=0,\hskip 28.45274ptn>k\end{cases}

10)

E
is weakly sequentially compact.

Proof.

•

1)\implies 2)\implies 3):
Immediate.

•

3)\implies 4):
Follows immediately by
Corollary
3.13
.

•

1)\implies 5):
While the equivalence between
1)
and
2)
is proven in
Theorem
3.10
, the conclusion follows immediately the existence of the weak clustered points of
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

•

5)\implies 6):
Given
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
and a bounded sequence of continuous linear functionals
\{f_{n}\}
, we have the set
\{f_{n}(x_{k})\}
is bounded in
\mathbb{C}
. Therefore, we can find subsequences of
\{x_{k_{j}}\}\subseteq\{x_{k}\}
and
\{f_{n_{i}}\}\subseteq\{f_{n}\}
such that both
\lim_{i}\lim_{j}f_{n_{i}}x_{k_{j}},\lim_{j}\lim_{i}f_{n_{i}}x_{k_{j}}
exists. If
\inf\{f_{n}(x_{k})\,|\,n<k\}>\sup\{f_{n}(x_{k})\,|\,n>k\}
, then:

\displaystyle\forall\,n,k\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\liminf_{j}f_{n}(x_{k_{j}})=\lim_{j}f_{n}(x_{k_{j}})>\limsup_{i}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k})=\lim_{i}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k})

\displaystyle\implies\,\forall\,i,j\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\lim_{j}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k_{j}})>\lim_{i}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k_{j}})

\displaystyle\implies\,\limsup_{i}\big(\lim_{j}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k_{j}})\big)>\liminf_{j}\big(\lim_{i}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k_{j}})\big)

\displaystyle\implies\,\lim_{i}\lim_{j}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k_{j}})>\lim_{j}\lim_{i}f_{n_{i}}(x_{k_{j}})

which contradicts our assumption.

•

6)\implies 9):
If
9)
is false, for the sequence
\{z_{n}\}
and
\{g_{n}\}
given by the assumption we have:

0=\sup\big\{g_{n}(z_{k})\,|\,n>k\big\}<\theta\leq\inf\big\{g_{n}(z_{k})\,|\,n<k\big\}

•

4)\implies 7):
Given a sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
, by assumption let
x\in\bigcap_{n}\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\geq n}}
. Fix an open ball centred at
0
, say
W
and then find a open convex neighborhood
U
so that
U-U\subseteq W
. Since
x\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}}
, there
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
v_{N}\in\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq N}
such that
x-v_{N}\in U
. Since
x\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>N}}
, let
s_{N}\in\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>N}
such that
x-s_{N}\ inU
. Hence:

v_{N}-s_{N}\in W\cap\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq N}-\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>N}\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359ptW\cap\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\big[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n}-\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>n}\big]\neq\emptyset

Since
W
is an arbitrary open ball centred at
0
, the conclusion follows immediately.

•

7)\implies 8):
Immediate.

•

8)\implies 9):
Suppose
9)
is false, Let
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}
,
\{z_{n}\}\subseteq E
and
\theta
be given such that:

g_{n}(z_{k})\begin{cases}>\theta,\hskip 28.45274ptn\leq k\\
=0,\hskip 28.45274ptn>k\end{cases}

Without losing generality, suppose
\|g_{n}\|\leq 1
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{a_{i}\}_{i\leq N}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
and a finite set of convex coefficients
\{\alpha_{j}\}
, we have:

\left\|\sum_{i\leq N}a_{i}z_{i}-\sum_{j>N}\alpha_{j}z_{j}\right\|\geq\left|\,g_{N+1}\left(\sum_{i\leq N}a_{i}z_{i}-\sum_{j>N}\alpha_{j}z_{j}\right)\,\right|=\left|\,\sum_{j>N}\alpha_{j}g_{N+1}(z_{j})\,\right|>\theta

•

1)\Longleftrightarrow 7):
Follows immediately by
Theorem
6.3
.

•

9)\Longrightarrow 1):
Since
1)
is equivalent to
7)
according to
Theorem
6.3
, it suffices to prove that
9)
implies
7)
. Suppose
7)
is false and let
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq E
be a sequence such that for some
\delta\in(0,1)
:

(34)

\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}\big]=\delta

Without losing generality (or remove
z_{1}
from
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
), we can assume:

\inf\big\{\|z\|:z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\big\}=\delta

Hence, by
Corollary
3.12
there exists
f\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}
such that:

\lambda=\inf\big\{f(z):z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}\big\}>f(0)=0

Observe that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
is compact and has non-empty interior. Then by (
34
) and
Theorem
3.11
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
g_{n}\in X^{*}_{\leq 1}
,
c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that:

\sup\big\{g_{n}(c):c\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\big\}<c_{n}<\inf\big\{g_{n}(c):c\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}\big\}

In the case where
\{c_{n}\}
is not bounded, we can then find
\{\lambda_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,1)
such that
\{\lambda_{n}c_{n}\}
is bounded and
\inf_{n}|\,\lambda_{n}c_{n}\,|=0
. Then replace each
g_{n}
by
\lambda_{n}g_{n}
and
c_{n}
by
\lambda_{n}c_{n}
. Therefore without losing generality, we can assume the set
\{c_{n}\}
is bounded and
\inf_{n}|\,c_{n}\,|=0
. Since
\inf_{n}c_{n}=0
, we can further assume
\lim_{n}c_{n}=0
(or replace
\{c_{n}\}
by a proper subsequence) and then assume that
\sup_{n}|\,c_{n}\,|<\lambda
(or remove the first
N
elements). Next define:

h_{n}:X\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\hskip 8.5359ptz\mapsto\begin{cases}0,\hskip 28.45274ptz\in\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}\\
g_{n}+f,\hskip 5.40608ptz\notin\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}\end{cases}

By our assumption, fix
\theta\in\big(0,\lambda-\sup_{n}|\,c_{n}\,|\big)
. Then for each
n,k\in\mathbb{N}

h_{n}(z_{k})\begin{cases}=0,\hskip 28.45274ptk\leq n\\
>\theta,\hskip 28.45274ptk>n\\
\end{cases}

•

9)\Longrightarrow 10):
Suppose
E
is not sequentially compact. Then
E
is not weakly compact. By
Theorem
6.3
, there exists
\mu,\sigma>0
such that for any
\delta,\Delta>0
with
\mu\in(\delta,\Delta)
, there exists a sequence
\{z_{i}\}\subseteq E
such that:

\forall\,\xi\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\},\hskip 8.5359pt\|\xi\|\in(\delta,\Delta)

and:

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359ptd\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}\big]\geq\sigma

Assume by contradiction that
3)
is true. Then there exists
z\in E
such that for each
g\in X^{*}
:

\liminf_{i}g(z_{i})\leq g(z)\leq\limsup_{i}g(z_{i})

By
Corollary
3.13
, we have
z\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}}
. Then there exists
z^{\prime}\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq N}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\|z-z^{\prime}\|<\dfrac{\sigma}{2}
, which implies for any
x\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>N}
:

\|z-x\|\geq\big|\,\|z-z^{\prime}\|-\|z^{\prime}-x\|\,\big|>\frac{\sigma}{2}

Hence
z\notin\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>N}}
. By
Corollary
3.12
, there exists
f\in X^{*}
such that:

(35)

f(z)>\sup\big\{f(v)\,|\,v\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>N}\big\}

Meanwhile, by assumption we have:

f(z)\leq\limsup_{i}f(z_{i})\leq\lim_{n}\sup\big\{f(v)\,|\,v\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq n}\big\}\leq\sup\big\{f(v)\,|\,v\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>N}\big\}

which contradicts (
35
).

•

10)\Longrightarrow 9):
Suppose
9)
is false and let
\{z_{i}\}\subseteq E
,
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}
and
\theta>0
be given such that:

g_{n}(z_{i})=\begin{cases}\theta,\hskip 28.45274ptn\leq i\\
0,\hskip 28.45274ptn>i\\
\end{cases}

Assume by contradiction that
24)
is correct. Then suppose
\{z_{n_{i}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{z_{i}\}
is a subsequence that weakly converges to
z\in E
. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{g_{n}(z_{n_{i}})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually equal to
\theta
and for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{g_{n}(z_{n_{i}})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually equal to
0
. Then by
Proposition
5.4
:
