\theta=\lim_{n}g_{n}(z)=\lim_{n}\lim_{i}g_{n}(z_{n_{i}})=\lim_{i}\lim_{n}g_{n}(z_{n_{i}})=0

which is absurd.

∎

Theorem 6.5

(
[
Jam64a
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and
E
a weakly closed bounded subset. Then the following statement are equivalent and each is equivalent to any one of statement listed under
Theorem
6.4
.

11)

For each complex-valued function
f
that is weakly continuous on
E
,
\sup_{e\in E}|\,f(e)\,|<\infty
.

12)

For each complex-valued function
f
that is weakly continuous on
E
, there exists
e_{0}\in E
such that
|\,f(e_{0})\,|=\sup_{e\in E}|\,f(e)\,|
.

13)

For each
f\in X^{*}
, there exists
e_{0}\in E
such that
|\,f(e_{0})\,|=\sup_{e\in E}|\,f(e)\,|
.

14)

\overline{\operatorname{circ}E}
is weakly compact.

15)

\overline{\operatorname{conv}E}
is weakly compact.

16)

For an arbitrary sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
, if
g
is a weakly continuous linear functional such that
\lim_{n}g(x_{n})
exists, then there exists
e\in E
with
g(e)=\lim_{n}g(x_{n})
.

17)

For an arbitrary sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq E
, if there exists
g\in X^{*}
such that
\lim_{n}g(x_{n})
exists, then there exists
e\in E
with
g(e)=\lim_{n}g(x_{n})
.

Proof.

From now on statements
1)\sim 9)
will be corresponding statements in
Theorem
6.4
.

•

1)\Longrightarrow 11):
Suppose
\pi
is a weakly continuous complex-valued function and unbounded on
E
. Then if we let
\mathbb{D}
be the unit disk on
\mathbb{C}
, then the weakly open cover,
\big\{\pi^{-1}(n\mathbb{D})\big\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, does not have any finite sub-covers. Hence
E
is not weakly compact.

•

11)\Longrightarrow 12):
Suppose
\pi
is a weakly continuous complex-valued function such that for all
e_{0}\in E
,
|\,\pi(e_{0})\,|<\sup_{e\in E}|\,\pi(e)\,|
. Then define:

\pi^{*}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\hskip 8.5359ptx\mapsto\frac{1}{\Big|\,\big[\sup_{e\in E}|\,\pi(e)\,|\big]-\pi(x)\,\Big|}

Clearly
\pi^{*}
is a weakly continuous but unbounded on
E
.

•

12)\Longrightarrow 13):
Immediate.

•

13)\Longrightarrow 14):
By
Theorem
2.4
, since
\overline{\operatorname{circ}E}
is weakly closed and bounded, if
\overline{\operatorname{circ}E}
is not weakly compact, there exists
g\in X^{*}
such that for all
e_{0}\in E
,
|\,g(e_{0})\,|<\sup_{e\in E}|\,g(e)\,|
.

•

14)\implies 15)\implies 1):
Immediate.

•

1)\implies 16):
Let
L=\lim_{n}g(x_{n})
. Suppose
L\notin g(E)
, then the following weakly open cover of
E
:

\left\{\Big\{x\in E:\big|\,L-g(x)\,\big|>\frac{1}{n}\Big\}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

does not have a finite sub-cover and hence
E
cannot be weakly compact.

•

16)\implies 17)\implies 13):
Immediate.

∎

To prove the next list of conditions is equivalent to previous one (from
Theorem
6.4
and
Theorem

), we need the following result:

Theorem 6.6

(
[
Tyc35
, Schauder-Tychonoff Theorem]
)
.

In a LCTVS
V
, if
K\subseteq X
is a convex subset and
K_{0}\subseteq K
is compact, then every continuous mapping from
K
to
K_{0}
has a fixed point in
K_{0}
.

Theorem 6.7

(
[
Jam64a
, Theorem 3]
)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and

a convex bounded closed subset of

. Then the following statements are equivalent to each of statements

in

and

18)

If
\big\{(x_{n},f_{i})\big\}_{n,i\in\mathbb{N}}

\{f_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

\sum_{i\leq N}x_{i}\notin E

20)

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\bigcap_{i\leq N}E\cap H_{i}\neq\emptyset\hskip 28.45274pt\implies\hskip 28.45274pt\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}E\cap H_{i}\neq\emptyset

21)

\operatorname{flat}(A)

(check

3.1

0\in\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{Span}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n}-\operatorname{flat}\{x_{i}\}_{i>n}}

E
into

23)

NOT
exist
\theta>0
, a sequence

20)\Longrightarrow 23):

21)\Longrightarrow 23):
Assume

is false and let
\{z_{n}\}\subseteq E

\{g_{i}\}\subseteq X^{*}

for some
\epsilon>0
. Fix an arbitrary

,
z\in\operatorname{Span}\{z_{n}\}_{n\leq N}

22)\Longrightarrow 23:
Suppose
23)
is false and let
\{z_{n}\}\subseteq E
,
\{g_{i}\}\subseteq X^{*}
and
\theta>0
be given by the assumption. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\alpha_{i}=\dfrac{1}{\theta}\big[g_{i}-g_{i+1}\big]
. Since, for each
z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
,
g_{1}(z)=\theta
and for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
g_{i}(z)-g_{i+1}(z)\geq 0
, we have for each
x\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}}
,
g_{1}(x)=\theta
and
\alpha_{i}(x)\geq 0
for each
i
. Also, for each
z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
,
g_{1}(z)-g_{k}(z)
is eventually equal to
\theta
. Therefore:

\displaystyle\forall\,z\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1},\hskip 8.5359pt\lim_{k}\sum_{i<k}\alpha_{i}(z)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(z)=\lim_{k}\frac{1}{\theta}\big[g_{1}(z)-g_{k}(z)\big]=1

\displaystyle\implies\,\forall\,x\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}},\hskip 8.5359pt\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(x)=1

Fix
x\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}}
and suppose
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
converges to
x
. Then consider
\{g_{i}(x_{n})\}_{i,n\in\mathbb{N}}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\big\{g_{i}(z_{n})\big\}
is eventually equal to zero. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{g_{i+1}(z_{n})-g_{i}(z_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually constant. Hence by
Proposition
5.4
, we have:

(37)

\forall\,x\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}},\hskip 8.5359pt\lim_{i}g_{i}(x)=\lim_{i}\lim_{n}g_{i}(x_{n})=\lim_{n}\lim_{i}g_{i}(x_{n})=0

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
define:

w_{N}=\frac{g_{N}(x)}{\theta}z_{N}+\sum_{i<N}\alpha_{i}(x)z_{i}

Then:

\displaystyle\left\|w_{N}-\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(x)z_{i}\right\|\leq\left\|\sum_{i\geq N}\alpha_{i}(x)z_{i}\right\|+\left|\,\frac{g_{N}(x)}{\theta}\,\right|\|z_{N}\|\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

\displaystyle\implies\,\lim_{N}w_{N}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(x)z_{i}

For each
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N>k
:

\displaystyle g_{k}(w_{N}-x)=\frac{g_{N}(x)}{\theta}g_{k}(z_{N})+\sum_{i<N}\alpha_{i}(x)g_{k}(z_{i})-g_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leq i<N}\alpha_{i}(x)\theta+g_{N}(x)-g_{k}(x)=g_{k}(x)-g_{N}(x)+g_{N}(x)-g_{k}(x)=0

\displaystyle\implies\,\lim_{N}g_{k}(w_{N}-x)=g_{k}\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(x)z_{i}\right)-g_{k}(x)=0

Since
\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
separates points in
\overline{\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}}
, we then can conclude
x=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(x)z_{i}
and, for
x
is arbitrarily fixed, we have for each
v\in\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}}
,
v=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(v)z_{i}
. Next define:

\pi:\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}}\rightarrow\overline{\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}},\hskip 8.5359ptv=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(v)z_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}(v)z_{i+1}

Clearly
\pi
is continuous and, since each
\alpha_{i}
is linear,
\pi
is also linear. If
u
is a fixed point of
\pi
, we then have:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}(u)=0\\
\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359pt\alpha_{i}(u)=\alpha_{i+1}(u)\\
\end{cases}\,\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\begin{cases}g_{1}(u)=g_{2}(u)=\theta\\
\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359ptg_{i}(u)-g_{i+1}(u)=g_{i+1}(u)-g_{i+2}(u)\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\implies\,\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 8.5359ptg_{i}(u)=\theta

which contradicts (
37
). Hence
\pi
has no fixed points.

23)\Longrightarrow 9):
Suppose
9)
is false and let
\{z_{n}\}\subseteq E
,
\{g_{i}\}\subseteq X^{*}
and
\theta>0
be given by the assumption such that for each
i,n\in\mathbb{N}
:

g_{i}(z_{n})\begin{cases}>\theta,\hskip 28.45274pti\geq n\\
=0,\hskip 28.45274pti>n\end{cases}

Define
\theta_{1}=\liminf_{n}g_{1}(z_{n})
.

–

If
\{g_{1}(z_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has a subsequence that is eventually equal to
\theta_{1}
, let
\{z_{n}^{1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be that subsequence and define
\sigma_{1}=\theta_{1}
.

–

Otherwise, let
\{z_{k_{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a proper subsequence such that
\lim_{n}g_{1}(z_{k_{n}})=\theta_{1}
. Since
g_{1}(z_{k_{n}})>\theta
for each
n
, without losing generality (or restrict to a subsequence), assume
\{g_{1}(z_{k_{n}})\}
is strictly decreasing. Fix
\sigma_{1}\in\big(g_{1}(z_{k_{2}}),g_{1}(z_{k_{1}})\big)
. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}(n>1)
, there exists
\alpha_{n}^{1}\in(0,1)
such that:

\alpha_{n}^{1}g_{1}(z_{k_{n}})+(1-\alpha_{n}^{1})g_{1}(z_{k_{1}})=\sigma_{1}

Then define
z_{1}^{1}=z_{k_{1}}
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>1
, define:

z_{n}^{1}=\alpha_{n}^{1}z_{k_{n}}+(1-\alpha_{n}^{1})z_{k_{1}}

In either case, we have a subsequence
\{z_{n}^{1}\}\subseteq\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\sigma_{1}\geq\theta
such that
g_{1}(z_{n}^{1})=\sigma_{1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Next find
K_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
that is large enough so that
g_{K_{2}}(z_{1}^{1})=0
. Define
\theta_{2}=\liminf_{n}g_{K_{2}}(z_{n}^{1})
. Similarly there exists
\sigma_{2}\geq\theta_{2}
,
\{z_{n}^{2}\}\subseteq\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}^{1}\}
such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
g_{K_{2}}(z_{n}^{2})=\sigma_{2}
and
g_{1}(z_{n}^{2})=\sigma_{1}
.By induction, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}(j>1)
, there is
\{z_{n}^{j}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\operatorname{conv}\{z_{n}^{j-1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
K_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
:

g_{K_{j}}(z_{n}^{i})=\begin{cases}\sigma_{j},\hskip 24.18501ptj\leq i\\
0,\hskip 28.45274ptj>i\end{cases}

For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, define
h_{j}=\dfrac{\theta}{\sigma_{j}}g_{K_{j}}
. Since
\{g_{i}\}
is bounded and
\sigma_{j}\geq\theta
for each
j
,
\{h_{j}\}
is also a bounded sequence. Also
\{z_{n}^{n}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is contained in
E
for
E
is convex. Then for each
j,n\in\mathbb{N}
:

h_{j}(z_{n}^{n})=\begin{cases}\theta,\hskip 28.45274ptj\leq n\\
0,\hskip 28.45274ptj>n\end{cases}

∎

Theorem 6.8

(
[
Jam64a
, Theorem 5]
)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and
E
a convex closed bounded subset. Then the following theorems are equivalent to each of
1)\sim 23)
from
Theorem
6.4
,
6.5
and
Theorem
6.7
.

24)

If two convex closed subsets
A_{1},A_{2}
of
E
are disjoint, then there exists
f\in X^{*}
such that:

\sup_{a_{1}\in A_{1}}f(a_{1})<\inf_{a_{2}\in A_{2}}f(a_{2})

25)

If two convex closed subsets
A_{1},A_{2}
of
E
are disjoint, then we can find
r>0
and
f\in X^{*}
such that:

\sup_{a_{1}\in A_{1}}f(a_{1})<r<\inf_{a_{2}\in A_{2}}f(a_{2})

Proof.

a)

1)\Longrightarrow 24):
Assume
E
is weakly compact. Then both
A_{1},A_{2}
are also weakly compact and hence weakly sequential compact by the equivalence of
1)
and
10)
. Assume by contradiction that
0\in\overline{A_{1}-A_{2}}
. Since
A_{1}-A_{2}
is convex,
0
is also in the weak closure of
A_{1}-A_{2}
. Then there exists two sequences,
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq A_{1}
and
\{y_{n}\}\subseteq A_{2}
such that
x_{n}-y_{n}
converges to
0
weakly. By weakly sequential compactness, let
a_{1}
be a weak cluster point of
\{x_{n}\}
and
a_{2}
a weak cluster point of
\{y_{n}\}
. Then we have
a_{1}=a_{2}
, which contradicts that
A_{1}\cap A_{2}=\emptyset
.

b)

24)\Longrightarrow 25):
Immediate.

c)

25)\Longrightarrow 23):
Suppose
23)
is false. Then similar to the proof to
24)\Longrightarrow 23)
, we will first define the following two disjoint closed convex subsets of
E
where
\{z_{i}\}
is given by the assumption:

\displaystyle B_{1}=\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{i}\Big[\frac{i}{i+2}z_{2i+1}+\frac{2}{i+2}z_{2i}\Big]:\{\gamma_{i}\}\subseteq[0,1]\textbf{ and }\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{i}\in\Big[\frac{1}{2},1\Big]\right\}

\displaystyle B_{2}=\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{i}\Big[\frac{i+1}{i+2}z_{2i+1}+\frac{1}{i+2}z_{2i}\Big]:\{\gamma_{i}\}\subseteq[0,1]\textbf{ and }\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{i}=\Big[\frac{1}{2},1\Big]\right\}

Then assume by contradiction that there exists
f\in X^{*}
and
r>0
such that:

\sup_{b_{1}\in B_{1}}f(b_{1})\leq r\leq\inf_{b_{2}\in B_{2}}f(b_{2})

Therefore, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{2}f\left[\dfrac{N+1}{N+2}z_{2N+1}+\dfrac{1}{N+2}z_{2N}\right]\geq r\\
\\
f\left[\dfrac{N}{N+}z_{2N+1}+\dfrac{2}{N+2}z_{2N}\right]\leq r\end{cases}\hskip 5.69046pt\implies\hskip 5.69046pt\begin{cases}(N+2)f(z_{2N+1})+f(z_{2N})\geq 2(N+2)r\\
Nf(z_{2N+1})+2f(z_{2N})\leq(N+2)r\end{cases}

\displaystyle\implies\,2f(z_{2N+1})-f(z_{2N})\geq(N+2)r\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359ptr\leq\frac{3}{N+2}\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\|z_{i}\|\|f\|\overset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Since
N
is arbitrarily picked, we then conclude that
r\leq 0
, which contradicts our assumption.

∎

Theorem 6.9

In a Banach space

\inf_{n}d\big[\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{flat}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}\big]>\sigma

Proof.

26)\Longrightarrow 29):

3.17
, for each
\sigma\in(0,1)

\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}_{\leq 1}
such that for each
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
:

g_{n}(z_{i})\begin{cases}>\sigma,\hskip 28.45274ptn\leq i\\
=0,\hskip 28.45274ptn>i\end{cases}

Therefore, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, given
x\in\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
and
y\in\operatorname{flat}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}
, we have:

\|x-y\|\geq\big|\,g_{n+1}(x-y)\,\big|>\theta

The conclusion follows since
x
and
y
are arbitrarily picked.

•

30)\Longrightarrow 26):
By assumption, for an arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
and
y\in\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}
, we have:

\|x-y\|\geq\frac{\sigma}{4}\|x\|>\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}.

which implies for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{conv}\{z_{i}\}_{i>n}\big]>\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}.

By
Theorem
3.17
,
X
is not reflexive.

•

26)\Longrightarrow 30):
Suppose
X
is not reflexive. Let
Q
be the canonical mapping from
X
to
X^{\ast\ast}
. Then fix an arbitrary
\Delta\in(0,1)
. There exists
F\in X^{\ast\ast}
such that
\|F\|<1
and
d\big[F,Q(X)\big]>\Delta
. Then
\|F\|\in(\Delta,1)
. If:

(38)

\inf\Big\{\big|\,F(f)\,\big|:f\in X^{*},\|f\|=1\Big\}\geq\Delta

then pick an arbitrary
f\in X^{*}
with
\|f\|=1
and define
\Phi=\dfrac{\Delta}{F(f)}f
. If the left hand side in
(\ref{e38})
is strictly less than
\Delta
, since
\|F\|>\Delta
, there must exist
f\in X^{*}
with
\|f\|=1
such that
F(f)=\Delta
. In general, there exists
\Phi\in X^{*}
with
\|\Phi\|\leq 1
such that
F(\Phi)=\Delta
. Next we will show there exists a sequence
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
such that
\Phi(z_{i})=\Delta
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence
\{h_{n}\}\subseteq X^{*}
such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|h_{n}\|<\dfrac{2}{\Delta}
,
F(h_{n})=0
, and for each
i,n\in\mathbb{N}
:

h_{n}(z_{i})=\begin{cases}\Delta,\hskip 25.03856ptn\geq i\\
0,\hskip 28.45274ptn<i\end{cases}

Since
\|F\|<1
and
F(\Phi)=\Delta
, if
\|\Phi\|\leq\Delta
, we then have
F\Big(\dfrac{\Phi}{\Delta}\Big)=1
, which implies that
\|F\|\geq 1
and contradicts our assumption. Therefore
\|\Phi\|>\Delta
and there exists
z_{1}\in X_{\leq 1}
such that
\Phi(z_{1})=\Delta
. Then for each
a_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
:

(39)

\big|\,a_{1}\Phi(z_{1})\,\big|=\big|\,a_{1}\Delta\,\big|\leq\big|\,F(\Phi)+a_{1}\Phi(z_{1})\,\big|+\big|\,F(\Phi)\,\big|\leq\big\|\Phi\big\|\big\|F+a_{1}Q(z_{1})\big\|+\big\|F\big\|.

Since
d\big[F,Q(X)\big]>\Delta
and
\|F\|\in(\Delta,1)
, we then have
\Delta\|F\|<d\big[F,Q(X)\big]\leq\|F+a_{1}Q(z_{1})\|
and together with (
39
) we have that for each
a_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
:

\big|\,a_{1}\Delta\,\big|\leq\big\|F+a_{1}Q(z_{1})\big\|+\|F\|<\big\|F+a_{1}Q(z_{1})\big\|+\frac{1}{\Delta}\big\|F+a_{1}Q(z_{1})\big\|=\left(1+\frac{1}{\Delta}\right)\big\|F+a_{1}Q(z_{1})\big\|

Therefore, for every
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
with
a_{2}\neq 0
, we have:

\Big|\,\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}\Delta\,\Big|\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{\Delta}\right)\Big\|F+\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}Q(z_{1})\Big\|\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\big|\,a_{1}\Delta+a_{2}\,0\,\big|\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{\Delta}\right)\Big\|a_{2}F+a_{1}Q(z_{1})\Big\|

Then by
Theorem
3.10
, there exists
h_{1}\in X^{*}
with
\|h_{1}\|\leq\dfrac{2}{\Delta}
such that
F(h_{1})=0
and
h_{1}(z_{1})=\Delta
. Hence for every
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
:

(40)

\big|\,a_{1}\Delta+a_{2}\,0\,\big|=\big|\,a_{1}F(\Phi)+a_{2}F(h_{1})\,\big|\leq\|F\|\big\|a_{1}\Phi+a_{2}h_{1}\big\|

Again by
Theorem
3.10
, there exists
z_{2}\in X
with
\|z_{2}\|<1
such that
\Phi(z_{2})=\Delta
and
h_{1}(z_{2})=0
. Next with
z_{1},z_{2}\in X_{\leq 1}
and
h_{1}\in X^{*}
, we will find
h_{2}
by the following inequality: for every
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle\big|\,a_{1}\Delta+a_{2}\Delta\,\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\,a_{1}\Phi(z_{1})+a_{2}\Phi(z_{2})\,\big|\leq\big|\,a_{1}\Phi(z_{1})+a_{2}\Phi(z_{2})+F(\Phi)\,\big|+\big|\,F(\Phi)\,\big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|F+\big(a_{1}z_{1}+a_{2}z_{2}\big)\Big\|+\|F\|\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{\Delta}\right)\Big\|F+\big(a_{1}z_{1}+a_{2}z_{2}\big)\Big\|

By
Theorem
3.10
there exists
h_{2}\in X^{*}
with
\|h_{2}\|<\dfrac{2}{\Delta}
such that
F(h_{2})=0
and
h_{2}(z_{1})=h_{2}(z_{2})=\Delta
. Then by (
40
) we can find
z_{3}\in X
with
\|z_{3}\|<1
such that
\Phi(z_{3})=\Delta
and
h_{1}(z_{3})=h_{2}(z_{3})=0
. Repeat the process above and eventually we have
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\|h_{n}\|<\dfrac{2}{\Delta}
for each
n
, and
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
such that
F(h_{n})=0
,
\Phi(z_{i})=\Delta
for each
i
and:

h_{n}(z_{i})=\begin{cases}\Delta,\hskip 25.03856ptn\geq i\\
0,\hskip 28.45274ptn<i\end{cases}

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>1
, define:

H_{n}=\big\{h_{i}-h_{i-1}:i\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}\big\}

Fix
n,p\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>1
and
z\in\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
with
\|z\|=1
. Since
\|z_{i}\|\leq 1
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, without losing generality, suppose
|\,\alpha_{n}\,|\geq 1
. Then:

(41)

\big|\,h_{n}(z)-h_{n-1}(z)\,\big|=|\,\alpha_{n}h_{n}(z_{n})\,|=|\,\alpha_{n}\,|\Delta\geq\Delta=\Delta\|z\|.

We can then conclude for each
z\in\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
, (
41
) holds and hence, for each
z\in\operatorname{Span}\{z_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
and
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{n<i\leq n+p}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
:

\left\|z+\sum_{n<i\leq n+p}\alpha_{i}z_{i}\right\|\geq\frac{\Delta}{4}\left|\,\big(h_{n}-h_{n-1}\big)\,\left(z+\sum_{n<i\leq n+p}\alpha_{i}z_{i}\right)\,\right|=\frac{\Delta}{4}\big|\,(h_{n}-h_{n-1})(z)\,\big|\geq\frac{\Delta^{2}}{4}\|z\|

Since
\Delta
is arbitrarily picked, we can assume
\Delta^{2}
is strictly greater than the
\sigma
that is picked from
(0,1)
and then the conclusion follows.

∎

7.
Characterizations of super-properties

In this section we will introduce properties stronger than reflexivity and give an overview of why they are stronger and how they can be characterized. As mentioned in
Introduction
, the
von Neumann-Jordan constant
(see
[
JVN35
]
) is one of the main tools to characterize super-reflexivity and uniformly non-squareness. The characterizations of uniformly non-squareness is purely geometric while the characterizations of super-reflexivity will involve representability of other Banach spaces and definitions of equivalent norms. We can also see from both
Section 1
and the current section that uniform convexity is the strongest among all other properties that have been covered. However, the existence of an equivalent uniform convex norm is a comparatively weak restriction.

7.1.
Uniformly non-square Banach space

Definition 7.1

(
[
JVN35
]
)
.

In a Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
, the
von Neumann-Jordan Constant for
(X,\|\cdot\|)
is defined as:

\inf\left\{C>0:\forall\,x,y\in X\,\,(\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}>0),\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{\|x+y\|^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2}}{2\|x\|^{2}+2\|y\|^{2}}\leq C\right\}

and is denoted by
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)
.

Remark 7.2
.

In any Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
, for each
x,y\in X
we have:

\|x+y\|^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2}\leq 2\|x\|^{2}+2\|y\|^{2}

When
\|x\|=1
:

\frac{\|x+x\|^{2}+\|x-x\|^{2}}{2\|x\|^{2}+2\|x\|^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}

Hence, we have
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)\in[1,2]
for any Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
. In particular, when
(X,\|\cdot\|)
is a Hilbert space, we have
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)=1
.

Definition 7.3
.

A normed linear space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
is said to be
uniformly non-square
if there exists
\delta>0
such that for any
x,y\in X_{\leq 1}
we have
\|x+y\|\leq 2(1-\delta)
whenever
\|x-y\|>2(1-\delta)
.

Theorem 7.4

(
[
TK98
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Fix
p>1
. Given
(X,\|\cdot\|)
a Banach space, if we use
\ell_{p}^{2}(X)
to denote the vector space
X\times X
equipped with the following norm:

\big\|(x,y)\big\|_{p}=\Big(\|x\|^{p}+\|y\|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

then the following statements are equivalent:

1)

X
is uniformly non-square.

2)

There exists
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
such that for any
x,y\in X_{\leq 1}
, if
\|x-y\|>2(1-\epsilon)
, then:

(42)

\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|^{p}\leq(1-\delta)\frac{\|x\|^{p}+\|y\|^{p}}{2}

3)

There exists
\delta\in(0,1)
such that for any
x,y\in X_{\leq 1}
, (
42
) holds if
\|x-y\|>2(1-\delta)
,

4)

There exists
\delta\in(0,2)
such that for any
x,y\in X
:

\left\|\frac{x-y}{2}\right\|^{p}+\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|^{p}\leq(2-\delta)\frac{\|x\|^{p}+\|y\|^{p}}{2}

5)

The following linear function:

(43)

A:X\times X\rightarrow X\times X,\hskip 8.5359pt(x,y)\mapsto(x+y,x-y)

is continuous and
\|A\|<2
when both
X\times X
are equipped with
\|\cdot\|_{p}
.

Proof.

•

1)\Longrightarrow 2)
: Assume by contradiction that
ii)
is false when
X
is uniformly non-square. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
x_{n},y_{n}\in X_{\leq 1}
with
\|x_{n}-y_{n}\|\geq 2(1-n^{-1})
such that:

(44)

\left\|\frac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}>\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{\|x_{n}\|^{p}+\|y_{n}\|^{p}}{2}

By dividing
\max(\|x_{n}\|,\|y_{n}\|)
in (
44
), we can assume that
\|x_{n}\|=1
and
\|y_{n}\|\leq 1
. Therefore we have:

(45)

2\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\leq\|x_{n}-y_{n}\|\leq\|x_{n}\|+\|y_{n}\|=1+\|y_{n}\|\leq 2

and from (
44
):

(46)

\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{1+\|y_{n}\|^{p}}{2}=\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{\|x_{n}\|^{p}+\|y_{n}\|^{p}}{2}<\Big\|\frac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\Big\|^{p}\leq\left(\frac{1+\|y_{n}\|}{2}\right)^{p}\leq 1

By (
45
) we have
\lim_{n}\|y_{n}\|=1
. Hence by (
45
) and (
46
) we have:

\lim_{n}\left\|\frac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\right\|=\lim_{n}\left\|\frac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{2}\right\|=1

Hence, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
we have both
\|x_{n}-y_{n}\|\geq 2(1-\delta)
and
\|x_{n}+y_{n}\|\geq 2(1-\delta)
for large enough
n
, which contradicts that
X
is uniformly non-square.

•

2)\Longrightarrow 3):
Immediate.

•

3)\Longrightarrow 1):
Let
\delta\in(0,1)
be given. Then for two arbitrary
x,y\in X
, suppose
\|x-y\|<2(1-\delta)
. By assumption we then have:

\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|^{p}\leq(1-\delta)\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|\leq(1-\delta)^{\frac{1}{p}}

Since
p>1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have
(1-\delta)^{\frac{1}{p}}\in(1-\delta,1)
and hence there exists
\delta_{0}\in(\delta,1)
such that
1-\delta_{0}=(1-\delta)^{\frac{1}{p}}
. Therefore, whenever
\|x-y\|>2(1-\delta_{0})
, we also have
\|x-y\|>2(1-\delta)
and then
\|x+y\|\leq 2(1-\delta_{0})
.

•

1)\Longrightarrow 4):
Suppose
4)
fails. Then similar to the proof of
1)\Longrightarrow 2)
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
\|x_{n}\|=1
and
y_{n}\in X_{\leq 1}
such that:

(47)

\left\|\frac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}+\left\|\frac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}>\left(2-\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{\|x_{n}\|^{p}+\|y_{n}\|^{p}}{2}=\left(2-\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{1+\|y_{n}\|^{p}}{2}

Since
\{\|y_{n}\|\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
[0,1]
, without losing generality suppose
\{\|y_{n}\|\}
is convergent (or replace it by a convergent subsequence) and suppose
\alpha\in[0,1]
is its limit. Then by (
47
) we have:

1+\alpha^{p}=\lim_{n}\left(2-\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{1+\|y_{n}\|^{p}}{2}\leq\lim_{n}2\left(\frac{1+\|y_{n}\|}{2}\right)^{p}=\frac{(1+\alpha)^{p}}{2^{p-1}}

Since for each
r\in[0,1]
, according to the binomial expansion of
(1+r)^{p}
, we have
(1+r)^{p}\geq 1+r^{p}
and the function
\dfrac{(1+r)^{p}}{1+r^{p}}
is increasing in
[0,1]
. Therefore the maximum of
\dfrac{(1+r)^{p}}{1+r^{p}}
in
[0,1]
is obtained when
r=1
and is equal to
2^{p-1}
. Therefore
\alpha=1
and from (
47
) we have:

\displaystyle 2=\lim_{n}\left(2-\frac{1}{n}\right)\frac{1+\|y_{n}\|^{p}}{2}\leq\lim_{n}\left\|\frac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}+\left\|\frac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}\leq\lim_{n}2\left(\frac{1+\|y_{n}\|}{2}\right)^{p}=2

\displaystyle\implies\,\lim_{n}\left\|\frac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}+\left\|\frac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}=2

Since both
\left\{\left\|\dfrac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}\right\}
and
\left\{\left\|\dfrac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}\right\}
are bounded above by
1
, we then can conclude:

\lim_{n}\left\|\frac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}=\lim_{n}\left\|\frac{x_{n}+y_{n}}{2}\right\|^{p}=1

or:

\lim_{n}\|x_{n}-y_{n}\|=\lim_{n}\|x_{n}+y_{n}\|=2

Hence, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
we will have both
\|x_{n}-y_{n}\|>2(1-\epsilon)
and
\|x_{n}+y_{n}\|>2(1-\epsilon)
whenever
n
is large enough, which implies that
X
is not uniformly non-square.

•

4)\Longrightarrow 1):
Let
\delta\in(0,2)
be given by
4)
and hence for every
x,y\in X_{\leq 1}
, we have:

\left\|\frac{x-y}{2}\right\|^{p}+\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|^{p}\leq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\max\left(\left\|\frac{x-y}{2}\right\|^{p},\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|^{p}\right)\leq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}

Since
p>1
and
\delta\in(0,2)
, there exists
\delta_{0}\in\left(0,\dfrac{\delta}{2}\right)
such that:

\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}=1-\delta_{0}

Hence for any
x,y\in X_{\leq 1}
, we have
\max\big(\|x-y\|,\|x+y\|\big)\leq 2(1-\delta_{0})
, which implies
X
is uniformly non-square.

•

4)\Longleftrightarrow 5):
By definition of the operator norm we have:

\|A\|^{p}=\sup_{x,y\in X}\frac{\|A(x,y)\|_{p}^{p}}{\|(x,y)\|_{p}^{p}}=\sup_{x,y\in X}\frac{\|x+y\|^{p}+\|x-y\|^{p}}{\|x\|^{p}+\|y\|^{p}}

If
4)
is true, since
p>1
, then:

\|A\|\leq 2^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)<2\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)<2

If
5)
is true, then there exists
\epsilon\in(0,2)
such that
\|A\|=2-\epsilon
and hence, for any
x,y\in X
:

\displaystyle\frac{\|x+y\|^{p}+\|x-y\|^{p}}{\|x\|^{p}+\|y\|^{p}}\leq(2-\epsilon)^{p}<(2-\epsilon)2^{p-1}

\displaystyle\implies\,\|x+y\|^{p}+\|x-y\|^{p}<2^{p}(2-\epsilon)\frac{\|x\|^{p}+\|y\|^{p}}{2}

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|^{p}+\left\|\frac{x-y}{2}\right\|^{2}<(2-\epsilon)\frac{\|x\|^{p}+\|y\|^{p}}{2}

∎

Lemma 7.5

(
[
TK98
, Lemma 2]
)
.

Let
A:X\times X\rightarrow X\times X
be given in (
43
) and both
X\times X
are equipped with
\|\cdot\|_{2}
. Then
\|A\|^{2}=2CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)

Proof.

By definition of
\|A\|
and
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)
we have:

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in X\\
\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|x+y\|^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2}}{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}}=\|A\|^{2}=2^{\frac{2}{t}}\leq 2CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)

If
2CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)>\|A\|^{2}
, then there exists
x_{0},y_{0}\in X
such that:

\frac{1}{2}\|A\|^{2}\geq\frac{\|x_{0}+y_{0}\|^{2}+\|x_{0}-y_{0}\|^{2}}{2\|x_{0}\|^{2}+2\|y_{0}\|^{2}}>CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)>\frac{1}{2}\|A\|^{2}

which is absurd. Hence
\|A\|^{2}=2CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)
.

∎

Theorem 7.6

(
[
TK98
, Theorem 2]
)
.

For a Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
,
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)<2
iff
X
is uniformly non-square.

Proof.

Let
A:X\times X\rightarrow X\times X
be define by (
43
). By
Theorem
7.4
,
X
is uniformly non-square iff
\|A\|<2
and by
Lemma
7.5
, we have
2\|A\|^{2}=CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)
and hence
\|A\|<2
iff
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)<2
.

∎

7.2.
Uniformly convexifiable
\,\Longrightarrow\,
no finite-tree property

Definition 7.7

(
[
Enf72
]
)
.

In a Banach space
X
, given an
\epsilon>0
, we call an ordered pair
(x_{1},x_{2})
a
(1,\epsilon)
-
part of a tree
if
\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|\geq\epsilon
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, given a
2^{n+1}
-tuple,
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2^{n+1}})
, we call
(x_{1},\cdots x_{2^{n+1}})
an
(n+1,\epsilon)
-part of a tree if for each
1\leq j\leq 2^{n}
,
\|x_{2j-1}-x_{2j}\|\geq\epsilon
and the following
2^{n}
-tuple:

\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},\frac{x_{3}+x_{4}}{2},\cdots,\frac{x_{2^{n+1}-1}+x_{2^{n+1}}}{2}\right)

is an
(n,\epsilon)
-part of a tree.
X
is said to have the
finite tree property
if there is an
\epsilon>0
such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and each
\delta\in(0,1)
, there is an
(n,\epsilon)
-part of a tree where the norm of each element in that
(n,\epsilon)
-part of a tree is bounded above by
1+\delta
.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and
x\in X
. Given
x_{1},x_{2},x\in X
, the ordered pair
(x_{1},x_{2})
is a
(1,\epsilon)

partition of

x
if
x_{1}+x_{2}=x,\|x_{1}\|=\|x_{2}\|
and
\left\|\dfrac{x_{1}}{\|x_{1}\|}-\dfrac{x_{2}}{\|x_{2}\|}\right\|\geq\epsilon
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>1
, a
2^{n+1}
-tuple
(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{2^{n+1}})
is an
(n+1,\epsilon)
-
partition of

x
if for each
1\leq j\leq 2^{n}
,
\|y_{2j-1}\|=\|y_{2j}\|
and
\left\|\dfrac{y_{2j-1}}{\|y_{2j-1}\|}-\dfrac{y_{2j}}{\|y_{2j}\|}\right\|\geq\epsilon
, and the following
2^{n}
-tuple:

\left(y_{1}+y_{2},y_{3}+y_{4},\cdots,y_{2^{n+1}-1}+y_{2^{n+1}}\right)

is an
(n,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
. If
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2^{n}})
is an
(n,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
, then for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k<n
, the
k
-
part of

(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2^{n}})
is the following
2^{k}
-tuple:

\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{n-k}}x_{i},\sum_{2^{n-k}<i\leq 2^{n-k+1}}x_{i},\cdots,\sum_{2^{n}-2^{n-k}<i\leq 2^{n}}x_{i}\right)

which is also a
(k,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
.

Definition 7.9
.

A normed linear space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is
finitely representable
in another normed linear space
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{Y})
iff for each finite dimensional subspace
X_{n}
of
X
and for each
\lambda>1
, there is an isomorphism
T_{n}:X_{n}\rightarrow Y
such that for each
x\in X_{n}
, we have:

\frac{1}{\lambda}\|x\|_{X}\leq\|T_{n}(x)\|_{Y}\leq\lambda\|x\|_{X}

The statement is equivalent to the following: for each finite dimensional subspace
X_{n}
of
X
and each
\epsilon>0
, there is an linear isomorphism
T_{n}:X_{n}\rightarrow Y
such that for each
x\in X_{n}
, we have:

(1-\epsilon)\|x\|_{X}\leq\|T_{n}x\|_{Y}\leq(1+\epsilon)\|x\|_{X}

Definition 7.10
.

A normed linear space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is
crudely finitely representatble
in a normed linear space
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{Y})
iff there is
\lambda>1
such that for each finite dimensional subspace
X_{n}
of
X
, there is an linear isomorphism
T_{n}:X_{n}\rightarrow Y
such that:

\frac{1}{\lambda}\|x\|_{X}\leq\|T_{n}x\|_{Y}\leq\lambda\|x\|_{X}

Definition 7.11
.

A Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is
super-reflexive
if any Banach space
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{Y})
that is finitely representatble in
X
is also reflexive.

Proposition 7.12

(
[
Jam72c
, Theorem 1]
)
.

A Banach space
Y
is super-reflexive iff no non-reflexive Banach space can be crudely finitely representable in
Y

Proof.

Suppose
Y
is not super-reflexive and hence there will be a non-reflexive Banach space
X
that is finitely representable in
Y
. Obviously, if
X
is finitely representable in
Y
, it will be crudely finitely representable in
Y
.

Now suppose a non-reflexive Banach space
X
is crudely representable in
Y
. By
30)
in
Theorem
6.9
, we can find
\delta_{0}>0
and a linearly independent sequence
\{x_{n}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
such that:

\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n},\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>n}\big]>\delta_{0}

Define
X_{n}=\operatorname{Span}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
and then there will be an isomorphism
T_{n}:X_{n}\rightarrow Y
such that for each
v\in X_{n}
:

(48)

\frac{1}{\lambda}\|v\|_{X}\leq\|T_{n}v\|_{Y}\leq\lambda\|v\|_{X}

Define
y_{k}^{n}=\frac{1}{\lambda}T_{n}(x_{k})
for each
k\leq n
and hence
\|y_{k}^{n}\|\leq 1
. For each
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k<n
, we have:

\displaystyle d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{k<i\leq n}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{k<i\leq n}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i>k}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta_{0}}{\lambda^{2}}

Since the inequality above holds for all
n>k
, we then can conclude:

(49)

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{i>k}\big]\geq\frac{\delta_{0}}{\lambda^{2}}

Then by using the subsequence:

\{y_{k}^{2^{n}}\}_{2^{n-1}<k\leq 2^{n},n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{y_{k}^{n}\}_{k\leq n,n\in\mathbb{N}}

we are going to construct a Banach space that is finitely representable in
Y
but non-reflexive. Let
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of symbols and in
V=\operatorname{Span}\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, define:

\left\|\sum_{k\leq n}a_{i}\xi_{i}\right\|_{V}=\limsup_{M}\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}a_{i}y_{i+2^{M-1}}^{2^{M}}\right\|_{Y}

Clearly
(V,\|\cdot\|_{V})
is a normed linear space. Let
\overline{V}
be the completion of
V
with respect to the norm topology so that
(\overline{V},\|\cdot\|_{V})
is a Banach space. Define
V_{n}=\operatorname{Span}\{\xi_{j}\}_{j\leq n}
. Fix
u=\sum_{k\leq n}a_{i}\xi_{k}\in V_{n}
and suppose
\|u\|_{V}=1
. Fix an arbitrary
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. We then can find
M(\epsilon,u)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
M(\epsilon,u)>\log_{2}n
and for each
M\geq M(\epsilon,u)
:

(50)

1-\epsilon\leq\left\|\sum_{k\leq n}a_{i}y_{k+2^{M-1}}^{2^{M}}\right\|_{Y}\leq 1+\epsilon

Fix an arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(V_{n})_{\leq 1}
be the closed unit ball of
V_{n}
and suppose
\{u_{j}\}_{j\leq m}
is a
\dfrac{\epsilon}{4}
-net of
(V_{n})_{\leq 1}
. For each
j\leq m
, put
M_{j}=M\left(\dfrac{\epsilon}{4},u_{j}\right)
. For each
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
, define:

P_{N}(\xi_{k})=\begin{cases}y_{k+2^{N-1}}^{2^{N}},\hskip 28.45274ptk\leq 2^{N-1}\\
0,\hskip 58.04343ptk>2^{N-1}\end{cases}

and linearly extend
P_{N}
to the entire
V
. Since the range of
P_{N}
is finite-dimensional,
P_{N}
is bounded and can be continuously extended to
\overline{V}
. For each
y\in V
, we have:

\limsup_{M}\|P_{M}(y)\|_{Y}=\|y\|_{V}\hskip 14.22636pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt\sup_{M}\|P_{M}(y)\|<\infty

Given
y\in\overline{V}
, suppose
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V
is a sequence that converges to
y
in
\|\cdot\|_{V}
. Observe that:

\limsup_{n}\|y_{n}\|_{V}=\liminf_{n}\|y_{n}\|_{V}=\lim_{n}\|y_{n}\|_{V}=\|y\|_{V}

Fix
N(\epsilon)\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

\|y\|_{V}-\epsilon<\inf_{n\geq N(\epsilon)}\|y_{n}\|_{V}\leq\sup_{n\geq N(\epsilon)}\|y_{n}\|_{V}<\|y\|_{V}+\epsilon

Fix an arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq N(\epsilon)
, and find
M(n)\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

\displaystyle\|y_{n}\|_{V}-\epsilon<\sup_{M\geq M(n)}\|P_{M}(y_{n})\|_{Y}<\|y_{n}\|_{V}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\implies\,\|y\|_{V}-2\epsilon<\sup_{M\geq M(n)}\|P_{M}(y_{n})\|_{Y}<\|y\|+2\epsilon

which implies:

(51)

\displaystyle\|y\|_{V}-2\epsilon<\sup_{n\geq N(\epsilon)}\sup_{M\geq M\big(N(\epsilon)\big)}\|P_{M}(y_{n})\|_{Y}<\|y\|_{V}+2\epsilon

\displaystyle\implies\,\|y\|_{V}-2\epsilon<\sup_{M\geq M\big(N(\epsilon)\big)}\sup_{n\geq N(\epsilon)}\|P_{M}(y_{n})\|_{Y}<\|y\|_{V}+2\epsilon

Since for each
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\lim_{n}P_{M}(y_{n})=P_{M}(y)
, from (
51
) we have:

\displaystyle\|y\|_{V}-2\epsilon<\sup_{M\geq M\big(N(\epsilon)\big)}\|P_{M}(y)\|_{Y}<\|y\|_{V}+2\epsilon

\displaystyle\implies\,\sup_{M}\|P_{M}(y)\|_{Y}<\infty

Since
y\in\overline{V}
is arbitrarily picked, by
Uniform Boundedness Theorem
, we then can conclude
R=\sup_{M}\|P_{M}\|<\infty
. Now fix an arbitrary
y\in(V_{n})_{\leq 1}
. Recall that
\{u_{j}\}_{j\leq m}
is an
\dfrac{\epsilon}{4}
-net of
(V_{n})_{\leq 1}
. Suppose
\|y-u_{i}\|_{V}<\dfrac{\epsilon}{4}
for some
i\leq m
. Then we have:

(52)

\|P_{M_{i}}(y)\|_{Y}\leq\|P_{M_{i}}(y-u_{i})\|_{Y}+\|P_{M_{i}}(u_{i})\|_{Y}<\|y-u_{i}\|_{V}\|P_{M_{i}}\|+\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\|u_{i}\|_{V}<\frac{\epsilon}{4}R+\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)

and by (
50
):

(53)

\displaystyle\|P_{M_{i}}(y)\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\geq\|P_{M_{i}}(u_{i})\|_{Y}-\frac{\epsilon}{4}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\|u_{i}\|_{V}-\|P_{M_{i}}(u_{i}-y)\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\|u_{i}\|_{V}-\frac{\epsilon}{4}R

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\|y\|_{V}-\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\|u_{i}-y\|_{V}-\frac{\epsilon}{4}R

\displaystyle>\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\|y\|_{V}-\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\frac{\epsilon}{4}R-\frac{\epsilon}{4}R

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\|y\|_{V}-\frac{\epsilon}{2}R

According to (
50
), both (
52
) and (
53
) also hold when
M_{i}
is replaced by any
M\geq M_{i}
. Put
M_{0}=\max_{i\leq m}M_{i}
. Since, for each
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
such that
R\epsilon<\delta
, together with (
51
) and (
52
), for all
M\geq M_{0}
and each
y\in V_{n}
with
\|y\|=1
, we have:

\left(1-\frac{\delta}{4}\right)-\frac{\delta}{2}<\|P_{M}(y)\|_{Y}<\frac{\delta}{4}+\left(1+\frac{\delta}{4}\right)

Therefore, for any
y\in V_{n}
and any
M\geq M_{0}
:

\displaystyle 1-\delta\leq\left\|P_{M}\left(\frac{y}{\|y\|_{V}}\right)\right\|_{Y}\leq 1+\delta

\displaystyle\implies\,(1-\delta)\|y\|_{V}\leq\|P_{M}(y)\|_{Y}\leq(1+\delta)\|y\|_{V}

Now we can conclude that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
M(n)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
with
M\geq M(n)
and any
y\in V_{n}
:

(54)

(1-\delta)\|y\|_{V}\leq\|P_{M}(y)\|_{Y}\leq(1+\delta)\|y\|_{V}

Suppose
U_{n}\subseteq\overline{V}
is an
n
-dimensional subspace
(n\geq 1)
and has basis
\{u^{i}\}_{i\leq n}
. Fix
u=\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}
with
\|u\|=1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Find
\epsilon\in(0,\delta)
such that
R\epsilon<\delta
. For each
i\leq n
, find
u_{i}\in V_{m_{i}}
such that:

\Big\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}\big(u^{i}-u_{i}\big)\Big\|<\epsilon

Then for each
m_{i}
, find
M_{i}
such that for each
M\geq M_{i}
, (
54
) holds for the given
\delta
and each vector in
V_{m_{i}}
. Put
M^{\prime}=\max_{i\leq n}M_{i}
. We then have
\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u_{i}\in V_{M^{\prime}}
and hence for each
M\geq M^{\prime}
we have:

\displaystyle\left\|P_{M}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}\right\|\right\|_{Y}\leq\left\|P_{M}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u_{i}\right)\right\|_{Y}+\left\|P_{M}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}u^{i}-u_{i}\right)\right\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|P_{M}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}\right)\right\|_{Y}\leq(1+\delta)\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u_{i}\right\|_{V}+R\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(u^{i}-u_{i})\right\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|P_{M}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}\right)\right\|_{Y}\leq(1+\delta)\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}\right\|_{V}+(1+\delta)\left\|\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}(u^{i}-u_{i})\right\|_{Y}+\delta

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|P_{M}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}\right)\right\|_{Y}\leq(1+\delta)+(1+\delta)\epsilon+\delta\leq 1+4\delta

Similarly, we can also show:

\left\|P_{M}\left(\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}\right)\right\|_{Y}\geq 1-4\delta

Since
u=\sum_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}u^{i}
is arbitrarily picked, we can then conclude for each
y\in U_{n}
:

\displaystyle 1-4\delta\leq\left\|P_{M^{\prime}}\left(\frac{y}{\|y\|_{V}}\right)\right\|_{Y}\leq 1+4\delta

\displaystyle\implies\,(1-4\delta)\|y\|_{V}\leq\|P_{M^{\prime}}(y)\|_{Y}\leq(1+4\delta)\|y\|_{V}

As a result,
(\overline{V},\|\cdot\|_{V})
is finitely representable in
Y
. Now fix arbitrary
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
k<n
. Then there exists
M(k)\in\mathbb{N}
such that (
53
) holds for all
y\in V_{k}
whenever
M\geq M(k)
. Fix
N\geq M(k)
and
N>\log_{2}n
. Then together with (
49
) we have:

(55)

\displaystyle d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{k<i\leq n}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{R}d\big[P_{N}(\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\leq k}),P_{N}(\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{k<i\leq n})\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{R}d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i+2^{N-1}}^{2^{N}}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i+2^{N-1}}^{2^{N}}\}_{k<i\leq n}\big]

\displaystyle\geq d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i}^{2^{N}}\}_{i\leq k+2^{N-1}},\operatorname{conv}\{y_{i}^{2^{N}}\}_{k+2^{N-1}<i\leq 2^{N}}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{R}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{i\leq k+2^{N-1}},\operatorname{conv}\{x_{i}\}_{k+2^{N-1}<i\leq 2^{N}}\big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta}{R\lambda^{2}}

Since (
55
) holds for all
n>k
, we then can conclude:

(56)

d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{i>k}\big]\geq\frac{\delta}{R\lambda^{2}}

and since the
k
in (
56
) is arbitrarily fixed, we then have:

\inf_{k\in\mathbb{N}}d\big[\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\leq k},\operatorname{conv}\{\xi_{i}\}_{i>k}\big]\geq\frac{\delta}{R\lambda^{2}}>0

Since
\|\xi_{i}\|_{V}\leq 1
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, by
Theorem
6.9
,
(30)
, we can conclude
\overline{V}
is not reflexive and hence
Y
is not super-reflexive.

∎

Proposition 7.13

(Milman–Pettis theorem)
.

A uniformly convex (see
Definition
1.1
) Banach space
X
is reflexive

Proof.

Pick
z\in X^{\ast\ast}
with
\|z\|=1
and fix an arbitrary
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
\delta
be given by uniform convexity. Pick
f\in X^{*}
with
\|f\|\leq 1
such that
\big|\,z(f)-\|z\|\,\big|<\dfrac{\delta}{2}
. Define:

C=\left\{x\in X_{\leq 1}:|\,f(x)-1\,|<\frac{\delta}{2}\right\}

Let
q:X\rightarrow X^{\ast\ast}
be the canonical mapping and we claim that
z\in C_{1}
, the weak-
\ast
closure of
q(C)
. Suppose
z\notin C_{1}
. Since
X^{*}
is a total subspace of
X^{\ast\ast\ast}
, by
Theorem
3.15
, the space of linear functionals that are defined on
X^{\ast\ast}
and continuous with respect to weak-
\ast
topology is
X^{*}
. Then by
Corollary
3.12
, there exists
g\in X^{*}
such that:

z(g)<\inf\big\{x(g):x\in C_{1}\big\}

Meanwhile, by
[
Gol38
, Goldstine’s Theorem]
, since
q(X_{\leq 1})
is weak-
\ast
dense in
X^{\ast\ast}_{\leq 1}
, there exists a net
\{x_{\lambda}\}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
such that
f(x_{\lambda})\rightarrow z(f)
for all
f\in X^{*}
. Since
\big|\,z(f)-1\,\big|<\dfrac{\delta}{2}
, there exists
\lambda_{0}
such that
x_{\lambda}\in C
for all
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
. Hence we have:

z(g)<\inf_{\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}}g(x_{\lambda})\leq\liminf_{\lambda}g(x_{\lambda})=\lim_{\lambda}g(x_{\lambda})=z(g)

which is absurd. Therefore
z\in C_{1}
. Since
C
is convex, for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in C
we have:

\left\|\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}\right\|\geq\left|\,f\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2}\right)\,\right|>1-\frac{\delta}{2}\hskip 8.5359pt\implies\hskip 8.5359pt\|x_{1}+x_{2}\|>2-\delta

By uniform convexity, we have
\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|<\epsilon
. Since
x_{1},x_{2}
are arbitrarily picked, the diameter of
C_{1}
is less than or equal to
\epsilon
. Hence there exists
x\in C
such that
\|x-z\|<\epsilon
. Since
\epsilon
is arbitrarily picked from
(0,1)
, we can now conclude
z\in q(X_{\leq 1})
. Since
z
is also arbitrarily picked,
X
is reflexive.

∎

Proposition 7.14
.

If a Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is uniformly convex, then it would be super-reflexive.

Proof.

Assume
X
is not super-reflexive and then by
Proposition
7.12
, there exists a non-reflexive Banach space
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{Y})
that is finitely representable in
X
. Since
Y
is not reflexive, by
Proposition
7.13
, it would not be uniformly convex. Hence in
Y
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for each
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y_{\leq 1}
such that
\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{Y}>\epsilon
and
\|y_{1}+y_{2}\|\geq 2-\delta
. Fix an arbitrary
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
such that
2\delta\in(0,1)
. Find
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y_{\leq 1}
such that
\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{Y}>\epsilon
and
\|y_{1}+y_{2}\|_{Y}\geq 2-\delta
. Define
V=\operatorname{Span}\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
. Since
Y
is finitely representable in
X
, there exists
T:V\rightarrow X
such that for each
v\in V
:

(1-\delta)\|v\|_{Y}\leq\|Tv\|_{X}\leq(1+\delta)\|v\|_{Y}

Hence we have:

\big\|T(y_{1}+y_{2})\big\|\geq(1-\delta)(2-\delta),\hskip 56.9055pt\big\|T(y_{1}-y_{2})\big\|>\epsilon(1-\delta)

Without losing generality, assume
\delta
is small enough such that:

\frac{1-\delta}{1+\delta}(2-\delta)\geq 2-2\delta,\hskip 56.9055pt\frac{1-\delta}{1+\delta}\epsilon>\frac{\epsilon}{2}

which implies that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
:

\left\|\frac{1}{1+\delta}Ty_{1}+\frac{1}{1+\delta}Ty_{2}\right\|\geq 2-2\delta\hskip 56.9055pt\left\|\frac{1}{1+\delta}Ty_{1}-\frac{1}{1+\delta}Ty_{2}\right\|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}

Since
\epsilon
is fixed and
\delta
is arbitrarily picked (and hence
2\delta
is also arbitrarily picked from
(0,1)
), we then can conclude
X
is not uniformly convex.

∎

Lemma 7.15

(
[
NA67
]
)
.

In
X
a LCTVS, given
K\subseteq X
a separable weakly compact convex subset and
p
a pseudo-norm given by the topology of
X
, for each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
there exists a closed convex subset
C\subseteq X
such that
K\backslash C\neq\emptyset
and
\big|\,p(x-y)\,\big|\leq\epsilon
for all
x,y\in K\backslash C
.

Theorem 7.16

(
[
Jam72b
, Theorem 2]
)
.

A Banach space
B
that has the finite-tree property is not reflexive.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that
B
has the finite-tree property and is reflexive. Fix the
\epsilon>0
given by the finite-tree property, an arbitrary
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N>1
. Let
F_{N}=\left(x^{N}_{i}:1\leq i\leq 2^{N+1}\right)
be the
(N,\epsilon)
-part tree given by the finite-tree property. Define:

K_{N}=\operatorname{conv}(F_{N}),\hskip 28.45274ptF^{\prime}_{N-1}=\left(\frac{x^{N}_{2i+1}+x^{N}_{2i+2}}{2}:0\leq i\leq 2^{N}-1\right),\hskip 28.45274ptK^{\prime}_{N-1}=\operatorname{conv}\big(F^{\prime}_{N-1}\big)

and by definition,
F^{\prime}_{N-1}
is a
(N-1,\epsilon)
-part tree. Since
B
is assumed to be reflexive, both
K_{N}
,
K^{\prime}_{N-1}
are weakly compact. According to
Lemma
7.15
, there exists
C
a closed convex set such that
K_{N}\backslash C\neq\emptyset
and:

\displaystyle\operatorname{diam}\big(K_{N}\backslash C\big)=\sup\big\{\|x-y\|:x,y\in K^{\prime}_{N_{1}}\backslash C\big\}<\dfrac{\epsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\implies\operatorname{diam}\big(K^{\prime}_{N-1}\backslash C\big)\leq\operatorname{diam}\big(K_{N}\backslash C\big)<\frac{\epsilon}{2}

If
F^{\prime}_{N-1}\subseteq C
,
K^{\prime}_{N-1}\subseteq C
. In this case, for any
0\leq i\leq 2^{N}-1
:

\frac{\epsilon}{2}>\operatorname{diam}\big(K_{N}\backslash C\big)\geq\left\|x^{N}_{2i+1}-\frac{x^{N}_{2i+1}+x^{N}_{2i+2}}{2}\right\|=\frac{1}{2}\big\|x^{N}_{2i+1}-x^{N}_{2i+2}\big\|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}

Clearly we must have
F^{\prime}_{N-1}\backslash C\neq\emptyset
. Suppose that for some
0\leq i\leq 2^{N}-1
:

x^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{x^{N}_{2i+1}+x^{N}_{2i+2}}{2}\notin C

Then we either have
x^{N}_{2i+1}\notin C
or
x^{N}_{2i+2}\notin C
. If both
x^{N}_{2i+1}
and
x^{N}_{2i+2}\in K_{N}\backslash C
, we will then have:

\frac{\epsilon}{2}>\operatorname{diam}\big(K_{N}\backslash C\big)\geq\big\|x^{N}_{2i+1}-x^{N}_{2i+2}\big\|\geq\epsilon

which is absurd. Therefore, both
x^{N}_{2i+1}
and
x^{N}_{2i+2}
are in
C
, which implies that
x^{\prime}_{i}\in C
and contradicts that
x^{\prime}_{i}\notin C
. Therefore
B
cannot be reflexive.

∎

Theorem 7.17
.

If a Banach space
(B,\|\cdot\|)
can be given a uniform convex norm that is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|
, then
B
will not have the finite-tree property.

Proof.

The conclusion follows immediately by
Proposition
7.13
and
Theorem
7.16
.

∎

7.3.
No finite tree property
\,\Longrightarrow\,
uniformly convexifiable

After showing that a Banach space being super-reflexive is equivalent to being crudely representable, one can expect that, as in the proof of
Proposition
7.12
, the proof of the direction in this section will also require defining an equivalent norm, and that is where we can see the
finite-tree property
again plays an important role. All results in this subsection are originally from
[
Enf72
]
.

Lemma 7.18
.

If a Banach space
X
does not have finite tree properties, then for every
\epsilon>0
, we can find
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
so that for each
x\in X
and each
(m,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
,
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2^{m}})
:

\sum_{j\leq 2^{m}}\|x_{j}\|\geq(1+\delta)\|x\|

Proof.

Fix
\|x\|=1
and
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2^{m}})
a
(m,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
. Consider the following
1
-part of the fixed
(m,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
:

\big(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m-1}},x_{2^{m-1}+1}+x_{2^{m-1}+2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m}}\big)

By definition, we have
\|x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m-1}}\|=\|x_{2^{m-1}+1}+x_{2^{m-1}+2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m}}\|
and:

\displaystyle\big\|x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m-1}}\big\|=\big\|x_{2^{m-1}+1}+x_{2^{m-1}+2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m}}\big\|\geq\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\implies\,\big\|(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m-1}})-(x_{2^{m-1}+1}+x_{2^{m-1}+2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m}})\big\|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\implies\,\big\|2(x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m-1}})-2(x_{2^{m-1}+1}+x_{2^{m-1}+2}+\cdots+x_{2^{m}})\big\|\geq\epsilon

Hence we obtain a
(1,\epsilon)
-part of a tree from the fixed
(m,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
. By induction, for all
k\leq m
, we can obtain a
(k,\epsilon)
-part of a tree by multiplying
2^{k}
to each vector in the
k
-part of a given
(m,\epsilon)
-partition.

Now suppose
X
does not have the finite tree property. Fix
x\in X
with
\|x\|=1
. Then for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
such that any
(N,\epsilon)
-part of a tree contains an element that has norm strictly greater than
1+\delta
. Next fix
\big(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2^{m}}\big)
an arbitrary
(m,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
.

•

If
m=N
, then by the previous remark
\left(2^{N}x_{1},2^{N}x_{2},\cdots,2^{N}x_{2^{N}}\right)
is a
(N,\epsilon)
-part of a tree. By assumption, there exists
1\leq j\leq 2^{N}
such that
\left\|2^{N}x_{j}\right\|\geq 1+\delta
. Since
\big(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2^{N}}\big)
is an
(N,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
, we have
\|x_{i}\|=\|x_{j}\|
for all
1\leq i\leq 2^{N}
. Hence:

\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{N}}\|x_{i}\|=2^{N}\|x_{j}\|\geq 1+\delta=\big(1+\delta\big)\|x\|

•

If
m>N
, by definition the following is a
(N,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
:

\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{m-N}}x_{i},\,\sum_{2^{m-N}<i\leq 2^{m-N+1}}x_{i},\,\cdots,\,\sum_{2^{m}-2^{m-N}<i\leq 2^{m}}x_{i}\right)

By the previous remark, the following is a
(N,\epsilon)
-part of a tree:

\left(2^{N}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{m-N}}x_{i},\,2^{N}\sum_{2^{m-N}<i\leq 2^{m-N+1}}x_{i},\,\cdots,\,2^{N}\sum_{2^{m}-2^{m-N}<i\leq 2^{m}}x_{i}\right)

Then by our assumption, there exists
1\leq j\leq 2^{N}
such that:

\left\|2^{N}\sum_{(j-1)2^{m-N}<i\leq j2^{m-N}}x_{i}\right\|\geq 1+\delta

and hence:

\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{m}}\|x_{i}\|\geq\sum_{1\leq j\leq 2^{N}}\left\|\sum_{(j-1)2^{m-N}<i\leq j2^{m-N}}x_{i}\right\|=2^{N}\left\|\sum_{(j-1)2^{m-N}<i\leq j2^{m-N}}x_{i}\right\|\geq 1+\delta

•

If
m<N
, observe that for any
1\leq i<2^{m}
we have:

\left\|\frac{x_{i}\,/\penalty\,2}{\left\|x_{i}\,/\penalty\,2\right\|}-\frac{x_{i+1}\,/\penalty\,2}{\left\|x_{i+1}\,/\penalty\,2\right\|}\right\|=\left\|\frac{x_{i}}{\|x_{i}\|}-\frac{x_{i+1}}{\|x_{i+1}\|}\right\|\geq\epsilon

Hence the following
2^{m+1}
-tuple is a
(m+1,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
:

(57)

\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2},\,\frac{x_{2}}{2},\,\frac{x_{1}}{2},\,\frac{x_{2}}{2},\,\cdots,\,\frac{x_{i}}{2},\frac{x_{i+1}}{2},\,\frac{x_{i}}{2},\,\frac{x_{i+1}}{2},\,\cdots,\,\frac{x_{2^{m}-1}}{2},\,\frac{x_{2^{m}}}{2},\,\frac{x_{2^{m}-1}}{2},\,\frac{x_{2^{m}}}{2}\right)

Similarly, we can induce a
(m+2,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
based on the
(m+1,\epsilon)
-partition in (
57
). Then by induction, suppose
(y_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2^{N}}
is the
(N,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
and is induced by the fixed
(m,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
in the way of (
57
). Hence we have:

\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{m}}\|x_{i}\|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq 2^{N}}\|y_{j}\|

By assumption, there exists
1\leq j\leq 2^{N}
such that
\|y_{j}\|\geq 1+\delta
. This immediately gives:

\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{m}}\|x_{i}\|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq 2^{N}}\|y_{j}\|\geq 1+\delta=(1+\delta)\|x\|

∎

Definition 7.19
.

In a Banach space
X
, a real-valued function
f
is said to be an
ecart
if
f
is non-negative,
f^{-1}\big(\{0\}\big)=\{0\}
, and for all
x\in X
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
f(\alpha x)=|\,\alpha\,|f(x)
.

Lemma 7.20
.

Suppose
X
is a Banach space without finite tree property. Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists an ecart
f_{n}
and
\delta_{n}>0
such that for all
x,y\in X
:

(i)

\left(1-\dfrac{\delta}{2^{n}}\right)\|x\|<f_{n}(x)<\left(1-\dfrac{1}{3}\dfrac{\delta}{2^{n}}\right)\|x\|
.

(ii)

f_{n}(x+y)\leq f_{n}(x)+f_{n}(y)

(iii)

if
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq\dfrac{\epsilon}{2^{n}}
, then
f_{n}(x+y)<f_{n}(x)+f_{n}(y)-\delta_{n}
.

where
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
is given by
Lemma
7.18
.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
be given by
Lemma
7.18
. Since
\epsilon\in(0,1)
can be arbitrarily small and
\delta
is always strictly smaller than
\epsilon
, it suffices to find an
ecart

f_{n}
and
\delta_{n}>0
such that
(1-\delta)\|x\|<f_{n}(x)<\left(1-\dfrac{\delta}{3}\right)\|x\|
for all
x\in X
and, whenever
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq\epsilon
,
f_{n}(x+y)<f_{n}(x)+f_{n}(y)-\delta_{n}
. Then, by the same method, we can replace
\epsilon
and
\delta
by
\dfrac{\epsilon}{2^{n}}
and
\dfrac{\delta}{2^{n}}
.

(i)

For each
x\in X
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, let
\mathcal{P}(x,k,\epsilon)
be the family of all
(k,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
(i.e.
\mathcal{P}(x,k,\epsilon)
is a family of
2^{k}
-tuple of points from
X
). Then define a function
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as the following: for each
x\in X
:

f_{n}(x)=\inf\left\{\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{m}}\|x_{i}\|\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq m}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}:\big(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2^{m}}\big)\in\bigcup_{0\leq k\leq n}\mathcal{P}(x,k,\epsilon)\right\}

Clearly
f
is an
ecart
. For each
x\in X
, the
1
-tuple
(x)
is viewed as the
(0,\epsilon)
-partition of
x
. Since
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have
\left(1+\dfrac{\delta}{2}\right)\,\left(1-\dfrac{\delta}{3}\right)=1+\dfrac{1}{6}(\delta-\delta^{2})>1
. Hence:

f(x)\leq\frac{\|x\|}{1+\dfrac{\delta}{2}}<\left(1-\dfrac{\delta}{3}\right)\|x\|

Meanwhile, since
\delta\in(0,1)
, clearly we have
(1+\delta)>(1-\delta)\,\left(1+\frac{2}{3}\delta\right)
. Then by
Lemma
7.18
we have that for all
x\in X
:

f(x)\geq\frac{(1+\delta)\|x\|}{1+\dfrac{2\delta}{3}}>(1-\delta)\|x\|

(ii)

Fix
k,l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and
\big(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2^{k}}\big)\in\mathcal{P}(x,k,\epsilon)
,
\big(y_{1},\cdots,y_{2^{l}}\big)\in\mathcal{P}(y,l,\epsilon)
. Without losing generality, assume
k\geq l
. Then there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that:

(58)

\alpha\big(2^{k}-2^{l}\big)\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k+1}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)<\frac{1}{4^{k+1}}\frac{\delta(1+\delta)}{2}

Define the following
2^{k+1}
-tuple:

\left(\underbracket{x_{1},\,x_{2},\,\cdots,\,x_{2^{k}}}_{1\,\sim\,2^{k}},\,\underbracket{y_{1},\,y_{2},\,\cdots,\,y_{2^{l}}}_{2^{k}+1\,\sim\,2^{k}+2^{l}},\,\underbracket{\alpha x,\,-\alpha x,\,\alpha x,\,-\alpha x,\,\cdots,\,\alpha x,\,-\alpha x}_{2^{k}+2^{l}+1\,\sim\,2^{k+1}}\right)

and clearly the
2^{k+1}
-tuple above is a
(k+1,\epsilon)
-partition of
x+y
. Therefore by (
58
) and
Lemma
7.18
, we have:

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|x_{i}\|\right)\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}+\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{l}}\|y_{i}\|\right)\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq l}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle-\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|x_{i}\|+\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{l}}\|y_{i}\|+\alpha\big(2^{k}-2^{l}\big)\|x\|\right)\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k+1}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle>\left(\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\frac{1}{4^{k+1}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|x_{i}\|\right)-\alpha\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)(2^{k}-2^{l})\|x\|\right)\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k+1}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-2}

\displaystyle\geq\|x\|\left(\frac{1}{4^{k+1}}\frac{\delta(1+\delta)}{2}-\alpha\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)(2^{k}-2^{l})\right)\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k+1}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-2}>0

Since
\big(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2^{k}}\big)\in\mathcal{P}(x,k,\epsilon)
and
\big(y_{1},\cdots,y_{2^{l}}\big)\in\mathcal{P}(y,l,\epsilon)
are arbitrarily picked, we then can conclude that
f_{n}(x+y)\leq f_{n}(x)+f_{n}(y)
.

(iii)

Now fix
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq\epsilon
. Then we can see
(x,y)
is a
(1,\epsilon)
-partition of
x+y
. Fix an arbitrary
\gamma\in\left(0,\dfrac{1}{8^{n}}\right)
. Then by definition of
f
, there exists
k,l\in\{0,1,\cdots,n\}
, and
\big(x_{1},\cdots,x_{2^{k}}\big)\in\mathcal{P}(x,k,\epsilon)
,
\big(y_{1},\cdots,y_{2^{l}}\big)\in\mathcal{P}(y,l,\epsilon)
such that:

(59)

f(x)+\frac{\gamma}{2}>\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|x_{i}\|\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1},\hskip 28.45274ptf(y)+\frac{\gamma}{2}>\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{l}}\|y_{i}\|\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq l}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}

Without losing generality, suppose
l\geq k
. Then let
\big(w_{1},\cdots,w_{2^{k}}\big)
be the
k
-part of
(y_{1},\cdots,y_{2^{l}}\big)
. Then by (
59
) we have:

(60)

f(y)+\frac{\gamma}{2}\geq\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|w_{i}\|\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq l}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}\geq\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|w_{i}\|\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{1}{4^{k}}+\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)\right)^{-1}

Observe that
(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{2^{k}},w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{2^{k}})
is a
(k+1,\epsilon)
-partition of
x+y
. Hence together with (
59
) and (
60
), we have:

(61)

\displaystyle f(x)+f(y)+\gamma-f(x+y)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|x_{i}\|\right)\,\left(\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}-\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k+1}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2^{k}}\|w_{i}\|\right)\,\left(\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}\frac{1}{4^{k}}+\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)\right)^{-1}-\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}\sum_{0\leq j\leq k+1}\frac{1}{4^{j}}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\dfrac{\delta}{2}\dfrac{1}{4^{k+1}}}{\left(1+\dfrac{\delta}{6}\right)^{2}}+\frac{\dfrac{\delta}{2}\dfrac{1}{4^{k+1}}-\dfrac{\delta}{6}\dfrac{1}{4^{k}}}{\left(1+\dfrac{\delta}{6}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle>\frac{\dfrac{\delta}{2}\dfrac{1}{4^{k+1}}}{\left(1+\dfrac{\delta}{6}\right)^{2}}-\frac{\dfrac{\delta}{2}\dfrac{1}{4^{k+1}}-\dfrac{\delta}{6}\dfrac{1}{4^{k}}}{\left(1+\dfrac{\delta}{6}\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{4^{k}}\frac{\delta}{6}\left(1+\frac{\delta}{6}\right)^{-2}

Since
\gamma\in\left(0,\dfrac{1}{8^{n}}\right)
, we have:

(62)

\frac{1}{4^{k}}\frac{\delta}{6}\left(1+\frac{\delta}{6}\right)^{-2}-\gamma>\underbracket{\frac{1}{4^{n}}\left(\frac{\delta}{6}\left(1+\frac{\delta}{6}\right)^{-2}-\frac{1}{2^{n}}\right)}_{\delta_{n}}

and
\delta_{n}
is defined only depending on the fixed integer
n
. Together with (
61
) and (
62
) we can now conclude
f(x+y)>f(x)+f(y)-\delta_{n}
for all
x,y\in X
with
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq\epsilon
.

∎

Lemma 7.21
.

Let
(X,\|\cdot\|)
be a Banach space without finite tree property. Fix an arbitrary
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
be given by
Lemma
7.18
. Let
f_{n}
be the ecart that is given by
Lemma
7.20
and corresponds to
\epsilon
. Let
\delta_{n}>0
be given by
Lemma
7.20
and correspond to
\epsilon
. Then there exists a norm
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{n,\epsilon}
on
X
such that for all
x,y\in X
:

(i)

\left(1-\dfrac{\delta}{2^{n}}\right)\|x\|<\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}<\left(1-\dfrac{1}{3}\dfrac{\delta}{2^{n}}\right)\|x\|

(ii)

if
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq\dfrac{3}{2^{n}}\epsilon
, then
\|x+y\|_{n,\epsilon}\leq\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}+\|y\|_{n,\epsilon}-\delta_{n}

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
Lemma
7.20
, since
\epsilon
can be arbitrarily small and so is
\delta
, it suffices to find a new norm
\|\cdot\|_{n,\epsilon}
such that
(1-\delta)\|x\|<\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}<\left(1-\dfrac{\delta}{3}\right)\|x\|
and, whenever
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq 3\epsilon
,
\|x+y\|_{n,\epsilon}\leq\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}+\|y\|_{n,\epsilon}-\delta_{n}
. Then by the same method, we can replace
\epsilon
and
\delta
by
\dfrac{\epsilon}{2^{n}}
and
\dfrac{\delta}{2^{n}}
.

For each
x\in X
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 2
, with the fixed
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, define:

\mathcal{F}(n,x)=\left\{\big(x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\big)\in X^{n+1}:x_{0}=0,\,x_{n}=x,\,\|x_{n-1}\|=\|x\|,\,\|x_{n-1}-x\|<\epsilon\right\}

and:

\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\\
n\geq 2\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{0\leq i<n}f_{n}(x_{i+1}-x_{i}):\big(x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\big)\in\mathcal{F}(n,x)\right\}

Fix an arbitrary
x\in X
. Since
(0,x,x)\in\mathcal{F}(2,x)
,
\|x\|_{\epsilon}\leq f_{n}(x)
. Meanwhile, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\big(x_{0},\,x_{1},\,\cdots,\,x_{n}\big)\in\mathcal{F}(n,x)
, by
Lemma
7.20
:

f_{n}(x)=f_{n}\left(\sum_{0\leq i<n}(x_{i+1}-x_{i})\right)\leq\sum_{0\leq i<n}f_{n}(x_{i+1}-x_{i})

we then have
f_{n}(x)=\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}
. By
Lemma
7.20
and that
f_{n}
is an
ecart
,
\|\cdot\|_{n,\epsilon}
defines a norm on
X
and for each
x\in X
,
(1-\delta)\|x\|<\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}<\left(1-\dfrac{\delta}{3}\right)\|x\|
. Next fix
x,y\in X
with
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq 3\epsilon
. Fix an arbitrary
(x_{0},x_{1},x)\in\mathcal{F}(2,x)
and, for an arbitrary
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geq 2
, fix
\big(y_{0},\,y_{1},\,\cdots,\,y_{m}\big)\in\mathcal{F}(m,y)
. Then we have:

3\epsilon\leq\|x-y\|\leq\|x-x_{1}\|+\|x_{1}-y_{m-1}\|+\|y-y_{m-1}\|<2\epsilon+\|x_{1}-y_{m-1}\|

and hence
\|x_{1}-y_{m-1}\|\geq\epsilon
. By definition we have
\|x_{1}\|=\|x\|=\|y\|=\|y_{m-1}\|
, and hence by
Lemma
7.20
we have:

(63)

\displaystyle f(x+y_{m-1})+f(y-y_{m-1})

\displaystyle\leq f(x-x_{1})+f(x_{1}+y_{m-1})+f(y-y_{m-1})

\displaystyle\leq f(x_{1})+f(x-x_{1})+f\left(\sum_{0\leq i<m-1}y_{i+1}-y_{i}\right)+f(y-y_{m-1})-\delta_{n}

\displaystyle\leq f(x_{1})+f(x-x_{1})+\sum_{0\leq i<m}f(y_{i+1}-y_{i})-\delta_{n}

Since
\big(0,x+y_{m-1},x+y\big)\in\mathcal{F}(2,x+y)
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}\,(m\geq 2)
,
\big(y_{0},\,y_{1},\,\cdots,\,y_{m}\big)\in\mathcal{F}(m,y)
are arbitrarily picked, by (
63
) we can conclude:

(64)

\|x+y\|_{n,\epsilon}\leq f(x_{1})+f(x-x_{1})+\|y\|_{n,\epsilon}-\delta_{n}

Therefore (
64
) proves the base case for
\mathcal{F}(k,x)
when
k=2
. Assume the
k=l
case for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
l\geq 2
. Then for
l+1
, fix an arbitrary
\big(x_{0},\,x_{1},\,\cdots,\,x_{n+1}\big)\in\mathcal{F}(l+1,x)
. We then have
\big(x_{0},\,x_{2},\,\cdots,\,x_{l+1}\big)\in\mathcal{F}(l,x)
and by our assumption and
Lemma
7.20
:

\displaystyle\|x+y\|_{n,\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq f(x_{2})+\sum_{2\leq i<l+1}f(x_{i+1}-x_{i})+\|y\|_{n,\epsilon}-\delta_{n}

\displaystyle\leq f(x_{1})+f(x_{2}-x_{1})+\sum_{2\leq i<l+1}f(x_{i+1}-x_{i})+\|y\|_{n,\epsilon}-\delta_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0\leq i<l+1}f(x_{i+1}-x_{i})+\|y\|_{n,\epsilon}-\delta_{n}

We can now conclude that
\|x+y\|_{n,\epsilon}\leq\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}+\|y\|_{n,\epsilon}-\delta_{n}
.

∎

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
X
be a normed linear space endowed with two equivalent norms,
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
, such that for each
x\in X
,
\|x\|_{2}\leq\|x\|_{1}\leq 2\|x\|_{2}
. Suppose for each
x,y\in X
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, whenever
\|x\|_{1}=\|y\|_{1}=1
and
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq\epsilon
, there exists
\delta(\epsilon)\in(0,1)
such that
\|x+y\|_{2}\leq\|x\|_{2}+\|y\|_{2}-\delta(\epsilon)
. Then
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is uniformly convex.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Pick
x,y\in X
with
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq\epsilon
. Then for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
:

\|\lambda x-y\|_{1}\geq\big|\,\|\lambda x+(1-\lambda)x\|_{1}-\|(1-\lambda)x-y\|_{1}\,\big|\geq\big|\,\|x\|_{1}-\|x-y\|_{1}-\lambda\,\big|=\big|\,1-\lambda-\|x-y\|_{1}\,\big|

Since both
x,y\in X
are arbitrarily picked, we then can conclude:

(65)

\forall\,x,y\in X,\hskip 22.76228pt\big(\|x\|_{1}=\|y\|_{1}=1,\,\|x-y\|_{1}\geq\epsilon\big)\hskip 8.5359pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt\inf_{\lambda\in(0,1)}\|\lambda x-y\|_{1}\geq\epsilon

Next pick
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in X
with
\|x^{\prime}\|_{2}=\|y^{\prime}\|_{2}=1
and
\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|_{2}\geq\epsilon
. Then there exists
\alpha,\beta\in\left[\dfrac{1}{2},1\right]
such that
\alpha\|x^{\prime}\|_{1}=\beta\|y^{\prime}\|_{1}=1
. Without losing generality, suppose
\beta\geq\alpha
. Since
\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|_{1}\geq\|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\|_{2}\geq\epsilon
, by (
65
) we have:

2\big\|\alpha x^{\prime}-\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\geq\big\|\alpha x^{\prime}-\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{1}\geq\inf_{\lambda\in(0,1)}\big\|\lambda\alpha x^{\prime}-\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{1}\geq\inf_{\lambda\in(0,1)}\big\|\lambda x^{\prime}-\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{1}\geq\epsilon\hskip 11.38092pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 11.38092pt\big\|\alpha x^{\prime}-\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and hence
\big\|\alpha x^{\prime}-\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{1}\geq\dfrac{\epsilon}{2}
. By our assumption on
\delta
, we then have:

\displaystyle\big\|\alpha x^{\prime}-\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\leq\alpha\|x^{\prime}\|_{2}+\beta\|y^{\prime}\|_{2}=\delta\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\implies\|x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\big\|\alpha x^{\prime}+\beta y^{\prime}\big\|_{2}+\big\|(1-\alpha)x^{\prime}+(1-\beta)y^{\prime}\big\|_{2}\leq 2-\delta\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

Since
\epsilon
is arbitrarily picked, we then can conclude
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is uniformly convex.

∎

Theorem 7.23
.

A Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
without finite tree property can be given a uniformly convex norm that is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|
.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
\epsilon\in\left(0,\dfrac{1}{2}\right)
. Let
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
be given by
Lemma
7.18
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\|\cdot\|_{n,\epsilon}
be the norm given by
Lemma
7.21
and
\delta_{n}
be given by
Lemma
7.21
. Then for each
x\in X
, define:

\|x\|_{u}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\|x\|_{n,\epsilon}

By
Lemma
7.21
, clearly
\|\cdot\|_{u}
is a norm, and for each
x\in X
:

(66)

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\|x\|=\|x\|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{n}}(1-\epsilon)<\|x\|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2^{n}}\right)<\|x\|_{u}<\|x\|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\left(1-\frac{1}{3}\frac{\delta}{2^{n}}\right)<\|x\|

\displaystyle\implies\,\|x\|_{u}<\|x\|<\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\|x\|_{u}<2\|x\|_{u}

We will prove that
\|\cdot\|_{u}
is uniformly convex using
Lemma
7.22
. Fix an arbitrary
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Pick
x,y\in X
with
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and suppose
\|x-y\|\geq\dfrac{3\epsilon}{2^{k}}
. Then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq k
,
\|x-y\|\geq\dfrac{3\epsilon}{2^{k}}
and hence by
Lemma
7.21
:

(67)

\displaystyle\|x+y\|_{u}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<k}\frac{1}{2^{i}}\big(\|x\|_{i,\epsilon}+\|y\|_{i,\epsilon}\big)+\sum_{i\geq k}\frac{1}{2^{i}}\|x+y\|_{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<k}\frac{1}{2^{i}}\big(\|x\|_{i,\epsilon}+\|y\|_{i,\epsilon}\big)+\sum_{i\geq k}\frac{1}{2^{i}}\big(\|x\|_{i,\epsilon}+\|y\|_{i,\epsilon}-\delta_{i}\big)

\displaystyle=\|x\|_{u}+\|y\|_{u}-\sum_{i\geq k}\frac{1}{2^{i}}\delta_{i}

Therefore, for any
x,y\in X
with
\|x\|=\|y\|=1
and
\|x-y\|\geq\lambda
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\lambda>\dfrac{\epsilon}{2^{N}}
. By (
67
), we have
\|x+y\|_{u}\leq\|x\|_{u}+\|y\|_{u}-\sum_{i\geq N}\dfrac{1}{2^{N}}
, and together with (
66
) and
Lemma
7.22
,
\|\cdot\|_{u}
is uniformly convex.

∎

Corollary 7.24
.

A Banach space
\big(X,\|\cdot\|\big)
can be given a uniformly convex norm that is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|
if and only if
X
does not have the finite-tree property.

Proof.

The conclusion follows immediately by
Theorem
7.23
and
Theorem
7.17
.

∎

Corollary 7.25
.

A Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
is super-reflexive iff
X
does not have the finite-tree property.

Proof.

By
Theorem
7.23
and
Proposition
7.14
, when
X
does not possess the finite-tree property, it is super-reflexive. When
X
is super-reflexive, clearly
X
can be crudely finitely representable in
X
itself and hence
X
is reflexive. Then the conclusion follows by
Theorem
7.17
.

∎

7.4.
Characterizations by von Neumann constant

Definition 7.26
.

Given a Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
, define:

\tilde{CJ}(X)=\inf\big\{CJ(X,\|\cdot\|):\|\cdot\|\,\text{ is equivalent to }\,\|\cdot\|_{X}\big\}

Theorem 7.27

(
[
KT97
, Theorem 2]
)
.

If a Banach space
X
is uniform convex, then
CJ(X)<2
.

Proof.

Pick two different non-zero
x,y\in X
such that
\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}=1
. Fix an arbitrary
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. If
\|x-y\|\geq\epsilon
, then according to
Proposition
1.6
, there exists
\delta\in(0,1)
such that:

\|x+y\|<2(1-\delta)\max\big(\|x\|,\|y\|\big)

which implies:

(68)

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\frac{x-y}{2}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{(1-\delta)\max\big(\|x\|,\|y\|\big)}{2}+\frac{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}}{2}\leq 1-\frac{\delta}{2}

\displaystyle\implies\,\|x+y\|^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2}\leq 2(2-\delta)

If
\|x-y\|<\epsilon
, we will have:

(69)

\|x+y\|^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2}\leq 2\big(\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}\big)+\epsilon^{2}\leq 2\left(1+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)

Combining (
68
) and (
69
) gives us:

\frac{\|x+y\|^{2}+\|x-y\|^{2}}{2\big(\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}\big)}\leq 1+\max\left(1-\delta,\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\right)<2

which implies that
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)<2

∎

Definition 7.28
.

In a Banach space
X
, given
(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X^{*}_{\leq 1}
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define:

S\big(\{p_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 2n};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)=\left\{x\in X:\forall\,1\leq i\leq 2n,\,\forall\,k\in\big\{p_{2i-1}+1,\cdots,p_{2i}\big\},\hskip 5.69046pt\frac{3}{4}\leq(-1)^{i-1}g_{k}(x)\leq 1\right\}

where
(p_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2n}
is a strictly increasing sequence of integers. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define:

K\big(n,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)=\liminf_{p_{1}\rightarrow\infty}\,\cdots\,\liminf_{p_{2n}\rightarrow\infty}\inf\left\{\|z\|:z\in S\big(\{p_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 2n};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\right\}

and:

K_{n}(X)=\inf\left\{K\big(n,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big):(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X^{*}_{\leq 1}\right\}

Lemma 7.29

(
[
Bea85
, Lemma III.
\lx@sectionsign
I.2]
)
.

Given a Banach space
X
, we have
K_{n}(X)\leq 2n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
if
X
is not reflexive.

Proof.

When
X
is not reflexive, fix
\theta\in\left(\dfrac{3}{4},1\right)
and let
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X^{*}_{\leq 1}
,
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X_{\leq 1}
be given by
Theorem
3.17
. Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(p_{i})_{i\leq 2n}
a strictly increasing sequence of integers. Define:

w_{n}=\sum_{j\leq n}(-1)^{j-1}\big(x_{p_{2j-1}}+x_{p_{2j}}\big)

Therefore for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
k\in\big\{p_{2i-1}+1,p_{2i-1}+2,\cdots,p_{2i}\big\}
. According to
Theorem
3.17
, for all
1\leq j\leq 2n
we have:

f_{k}\big(x_{p_{2j-1}}+x_{p_{2j}}\big)=\begin{cases}0,\hskip 28.45274ptj\neq i\\
\theta,\hskip 28.45274ptj=i\end{cases}\hskip 14.22636pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 14.22636ptf_{k}(w_{n})=(-1)^{i-1}\theta

which implies
w_{n}\in S\big((p_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 2n};(f_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)
. Clearly
\|w_{n}\|\leq 2n
and hence
K_{n}(X)\leq 2n
.

∎

Proposition 7.30
.

[
Bea85
]

If a Banach space
X
is uniformly non-square, then it is reflexive.

Proof.

Assume that
X
is not reflexive. Then according to
Lemma
7.29
, we have
K_{n}(X)\leq 2n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Clearly
K_{n}(X)
increases as
n
increases. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X^{*}_{\leq 1}
,
\{p_{i}\}_{i\leq 2n}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
that is strictly increasing, if
x\in S\big(\{p_{i}\}_{i\leq 2n},(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)
, then clearly
\|x\|\geq\dfrac{3}{4}
and hence
K_{n}(X)\geq\dfrac{3}{4}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
and
r\in(1-\delta,1)
. Therefore, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

(70)

\displaystyle\forall\,\epsilon\in\left(0,\frac{3}{4}\frac{1-r}{2r+1}\right),\hskip 8.5359ptK_{n}(X)\geq\frac{3}{4}>\frac{(2r+1)\epsilon}{1-r}

\displaystyle\implies\,\forall\,\epsilon\in\left(0,\frac{3}{4}\frac{1-r}{2r+1}\right),\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{K_{n}(X)-\epsilon}{K_{n}(X)+2\epsilon}>r

From
K_{n}(X)\leq 2n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can see the increasing speed of
K_{n}(X)
is at most linear. Also, from (
70
), we have:

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\,\forall\,\epsilon\in\left(0,\frac{3}{4}\frac{1-r}{2r+1}\right),\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{K_{n}(X)-\epsilon}{K_{n-1}(X)-\epsilon}=\frac{K_{n}(X)-\epsilon}{K_{n}(X)+2\epsilon}\frac{K_{n}(X)+2\epsilon}{K_{n-1}(X)-\epsilon}>r

which implies that
\liminf_{n}\dfrac{K_{n}(X)}{K_{n-1}(X)}=1
. Therefore, for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
m\geq M
such that
\dfrac{K_{m-1}(X)-\epsilon}{K_{m}(X)+2\epsilon}>1-\delta
. Fix such an
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X^{*}_{\leq 1}
be such that:

K\big(m,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)<K_{m}(X)+\epsilon

Fix two strictly increasing sequence of integers
(p_{i})_{i\leq 2m}
,
(q_{j})_{j\leq 2m}
such that for any
1\leq i<m
, we have
p_{2i}<p_{2i+1}<q_{2i}<q_{2i+1}<p_{2i+2}<p_{2i+3}
and
p_{1}<q_{1}<p_{2}
,
q_{2m-1}<p_{2m}<q_{2m}
. Define:

\displaystyle\forall\,1\leq i\leq 2m,\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{p}_{i}=\begin{cases}q_{i},\hskip 29.02193pt2\nmid i\\
p_{i},\hskip 28.45274pt2\mid i\end{cases}

\displaystyle\forall\,1\leq i\leq 2m-2,\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{q}_{i}=\begin{cases}p_{i+2},\hskip 17.92537pt2\nmid i\\
q_{i},\hskip 28.45274pt2\mid i\end{cases}

Furthermore, by definition of
K_{n}\big(n,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)
, we can assume
(p_{i})_{i\leq 2m}
and
(q_{j})_{j\leq 2m}
satisfies:

(71)

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\|z\|:z\in S\big((p_{i})_{i\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\right\}<K\big(m,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\|z\|:z\in S\big((q_{j})_{j\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\right\}<K\big(m,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\|z\|:z\in S\big((\tilde{p}_{i})_{i\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\right\}>K\big(m,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}-\epsilon

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\|z\|:z\in S\big((\tilde{q}_{i})_{i\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\right\}>K\big(m-1,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}-\epsilon

We can then find
u\in S\big((p_{i})_{i\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)
and
v\in S\big((q_{i})_{i\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)
such that:

(72)

\max\big(\|u\|,\|v\|\big)<K\big(m,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)+\epsilon

and for all
1\leq i\leq m
:

\begin{cases}\dfrac{3}{4}\leq(-1)^{i-1}g_{k}(u)\leq 1,\hskip 28.45274ptp_{2i-1}<k\leq p_{2i}\\
\\
\dfrac{3}{4}\leq(-1)^{i-1}g_{k}(v)\leq 1,\hskip 28.45274ptq_{2i-1}<k\leq q_{2i}\end{cases}

Since for each
1\leq i\leq m
we have
q_{2i-1}<p_{2i}<q_{2i}
, therefore for all
1\leq i\leq m
:

\forall\,q_{2i-1}<k\leq p_{2i},\hskip 8.5359pt\dfrac{3}{4}\leq(-1)^{i-1}g_{k}\left(\frac{u+v}{2}\right)\leq 1\hskip 14.22636pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{v+u}{2}\in S\big((\tilde{p}_{i})_{i\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)

which, according to (
71
), implies:

\left\|\frac{v+u}{2}\right\|\geq K\big(m,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)-\epsilon

Similarly, we have:

\frac{v-u}{2}\in S\big((\tilde{q}_{i})_{i\leq 2m};(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)\hskip 14.22636pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt\left\|\frac{v-u}{2}\right\|\geq K\big(m,(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big)-\epsilon

Next put:

x=\frac{u}{K_{m}(X)+2\epsilon},\hskip 28.45274pty=\frac{v}{K_{m}(X)+2\epsilon}

By (
72
) we have both
\|x\|<1
and
\|y\|<1
. Meanwhile:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|=\frac{1}{K_{m}(X)+2\epsilon}\left\|\frac{u+v}{2}\right\|\geq\frac{K_{m}(X)-\epsilon}{K_{m}(X)+2\epsilon}>1-\delta

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{x-y}{2}\right\|=\frac{1}{K_{m}(X)+2\epsilon}\left\|\frac{u-v}{2}\right\|\geq\frac{K_{m-1}(X)-\epsilon}{K_{m}(X)+2\epsilon}>1-\delta\

Therefore,
X
is not uniformly non-square.

∎

Theorem 7.31
.

A Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
is super-reflexive if and only if
\tilde{CJ}(X)<2
.

Proof.

When
X
is super-reflexive, according to
Corollary
7.24
,
Corollary
7.25
and
Theorem
7.27
we immediately have
\tilde{CJ}(X)<2
. Conversely, given
\tilde{CJ}(X)<2
, there exists a norm
\|\cdot\|
that is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{X}
such that
CJ(X,\|\cdot\|)<2
, which implies that
(X,\|\cdot\|)
is uniformly non-square according to
Theorem
7.4
. Next suppose
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{Y})
is a Banach space that can be crudely representable in
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
. Then clearly
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{Y})
can be crudely representable in
(X,\|\cdot\|)
. Then there exists
\lambda\in(1,\infty)
such that for any two
x,y\in Y_{\leq 1}
, there exists a linear mapping
T:\operatorname{Span}\big(\{x,y\}\big)\rightarrow X
such that:

(73)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\|x\pm y\|_{Y}\leq\|T(x\pm y)\|\leq\lambda\|x\pm y\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\|x\|_{Y}\leq\|Tx\|\leq\lambda\|x\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\|x\|_{Y}\leq\|Ty\|\leq\lambda\|y\|_{Y}

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
be given by the uniform non-squareness of
(X,\|\cdot\|)
and together with (
73
) we have:

\displaystyle\left\|T\left(\frac{x}{\lambda}\right)\pm T\left(\frac{y}{\lambda}\right)\right\|\leq 2(1-\delta)

\displaystyle\implies\,\left\|T\left(\frac{x}{\lambda}\right)\mp T\left(\frac{y}{\lambda}\right)\right\|>2(1-\delta)

\displaystyle\implies\,\|x\mp y\|_{Y}\geq\left\|T\left(\frac{x}{\lambda}\right)\mp T\left(\frac{y}{\lambda}\right)\right\|>2(1-\delta)

which implies that
(Y,\|\cdot\|_{Y})
is also uniformly non-square. Therefore, together with
Proposition
7.30
and
Proposition
7.12
, we can then conclude
X
is super-reflexive.

∎
